A further overlooked problem is the violation of the i.i.d. latent condition after watermarking, which leads to latent correlation degradation and generation fidelity loss. Although this has been externally measured by FID, the internal correlation structure has yet to be rigorously characterized. To address the above issues, and motivated by the rotation-invariant property of isotropic Gaussian, we propose \textit{Latent Angular Watermarking (LAW)}, which encodes watermark bits as antipodal angles ($\pmπ/2$ relative to a reference pair) between disjoint pairs of latent elements while preserving the Gaussianity. The antipodal ($π$-separation) encoding maximizes geometric separation between bit values, and we prove that the decoding angular-error variance is proportional to the norm of the latent pair, i.e., $\operatorname{var}(Δφ) \propto 1/ρ^2$. We further propose a magnitude-driven variant, LAW-M, which anchors watermark bits in the most geometrically stable latent dimensions, yielding additional robustness gains. Theoretically, we provide a rigorous characterization of the induced correlation degradation, deriving in closed form the autocorrelation structure of the watermarked latent and proving that correlations are confined to a sparse, structured set of off-diagonal elements with fixed $\pmπ/4$ values.

</details>


### [51] [Deformable Triangle Splatting: Flexible Primitives for Real-Time Radiance Field Rendering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22446)
*Oriol Jiménez-Ayguadé,Antonio Agudo*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Deformable Triangle Splatting enables non-convex scene primitives by per-edge, scalar-displaced control points on triangles, with differentiable rasterization and a winding-number-based inside-test, yielding improved visual quality over convex-only 2D primitives and competitive efficiency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current radiance-field representations using convex 2D primitives (e.g., Gaussian disks, triangles) struggle to capture curved/concave geometry efficiently, often requiring many primitives. A deformable, differentiable primitive could represent non-convex shapes more compactly while preserving rendering differentiability.

Method: Each triangle is augmented with K control points per edge; each control point has a learnable scalar displacement shifting the boundary inward/outward, preserving the triangle's defining 3D plane. Rasterization occurs in barycentric space; a winding-number test determines pixel inclusion. A window function, controlled by sharpness and corner-smoothness parameters, plus a per-primitive opacity, yields smooth interior-to-boundary transitions. This yields differentiable rendering of non-convex primitives.

Result: Validation on diverse real-world scenes shows the method surpasses recent non-volumetric-primitives baselines in visual quality and versatility, while maintaining competitive rendering efficiency.

Conclusion: Deformable triangles with per-edge deformations enable compact, non-convex representations for radiance fields. The approach preserves differentiability and efficiency, expanding the practical expressiveness beyond convex primitives.

Abstract: Recent radiance field methods represent scenes with 2D primitives that offer surface alignment and efficient rasterization, from Gaussian disks to triangles, yet all rely on convex boundaries: curved and concave structures demand excessive primitives. We introduce Deformable Triangle Splatting, which augments each triangle with $K$ control points per edge, each parameterized by a single learnable scalar displacement that shifts the boundary inward or outward, enabling non-convex shape representation while preserving the three base vertices that define the 3D plane. To render these non-convex primitives differentiably, we design a rasterization pipeline in the triangle's barycentric coordinate space, ensuring view-consistent rendering. A winding number test determines whether each pixel lies inside the deformed primitive, and a window function controlled by two learnable parameters, sharpness and corner smoothness, together with a per-primitive scalar opacity, produces the smooth opacity transition from interior to boundary. Validation is done in a variety of real-world scenes, outperforming recent works based on non-volumetric primitives in terms of visual quality and versatility while still achieving competitive rendering efficiency.

</details>


### [52] [Optimal Transport Image Representation and Deep Covariance Alignment (CORAL) for Control Valve Stiction Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22486)
*Seshu K. Damarla*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: OT-imaging of control-loop signals plus Deep CORAL for domain-invariant stiction detection; 18/20 loops diagnosed, 100% recall on 13 cases, 90% accuracy, 92.9% F1, outperforming handcrafted features.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Generalization gap between simulated-data-trained detectors and real industrial control loops due to domain shift; need domain-invariant representations for robust stiction detection.

Method: Convert controller output and process variable into 2D Optimal Transport images. Use a CNN encoder to learn domain-invariant features by optimizing a joint objective: cross-entropy on labeled simulation data + Deep CORAL covariance-alignment loss between simulated and unlabeled target industrial data. Train downstream classifiers on invariant features and test on an independent industrial benchmark (20 loops).

Result: Achieved 18/20 correct diagnoses, 100% recall across all 13 stiction cases, 90.00% accuracy, 92.86% F1-score; significantly mitigates domain shift compared to hand-crafted features baselines.

Conclusion: The proposed OT imaging with Deep CORAL provides robust, reliable stiction detection in real-world industrial control loops by reducing domain shift and leveraging domain-invariant representations, outperforming traditional hand-crafted approaches.

Abstract: Control valve stiction is a common cause of unwanted oscillations and poor control-loop performance in industrial processes. Data-driven methods can automatically detect stiction, but models trained purely on simulated data often struggle to generalize to real industrial control loops due to domain shift. To bridge this gap, this work propose a novel stiction detection methodology that combines optimal transport (OT) imaging technique with deep correlation alignment (Deep CORAL) algorithm. Closed loop signals: controller output and process variable are converted into two-dimensional OT images. These images capture the dynamic behaviour of control loops. The proposed methodology includes a convolutional neural network (CNN) encoder (or feature extractor) trained to learn domain-invariant representations by optimizing a combined objective: a cross-entropy loss on labeled simulation data and a Deep CORAL (covariance-alignment) loss between simulation data and unlabeled target-domain industrial data. Downstream classifiers trained on the domain-invariant target features were evaluated on an independent test set of 20 benchmark loops from industrial stiction data benchmark. The proposed methodology successfully diagnosed 18 out of the 20 loops and achieved100% recall across all 13 stiction cases, an accuracy of 90.00% and an F1-score of 92.86%. Compared to standard baseline approach (hand-crafted features-based method), the proposed methodology significantly mitigates domain shift, providing robust, highly reliable stiction detection for real-world industrial control loops.

</details>


### [53] [Twins: Learn to Predict Unified Representations with Focal Loss](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22531)
*Kaixiong Gong,Xin Cai,Bin Lin,Hao Wang,Yunlong Lin,Mingzhe Zheng,Bohao Li,Jian-Wei Zhang,Miles Yang,Zhao Zhong,Liefeng Bo,Xiangyu Yue*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Twins proposes a unified continuous token space by channel-wise concatenation of ViT and VAE features on the same grid, enabling unchanged sequence length and attention, and uses a focal regression objective to balance diffusion model optimization across modalities, achieving significant gFID improvements on ImageNet and competitive multimodal understanding performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current continuous multimodal pipelines separate semantic and latent representations, causing mismatched latent spaces and optimization imbalance; a unified, continuous token space could align understanding and generation.

Method: Concatenate ViT and VAE features channel-wise on the same token grid; train a Diffusion Transformer; apply a focal regression objective for flow matching that upweights high-error VAE dimensions to balance ViT/VAE optimization; evaluate on ImageNet and multimodal benchmarks.

Result: Achieves up to 10.57 gFID improvement over naive MSE loss without guidance; competitive results on multimodal understanding benchmarks; improved reconstruction fidelity, narrowing the gap between understanding and generation representations.

Conclusion: A balanced optimization via focal regression mitigates heterogeneity in Twins and enables more coherent joint understanding-generation representations, validating the unified continuous token space approach.

Abstract: Unified multimodal models seek a shared visual token space that supports both multimodal understanding and image generation. Discrete methods unify the interface via a shared codebook, whereas continuous pipelines often rely on two disparate representations -- semantic features (e.g., ViT) for understanding and low-level latents (e.g., VAE) for synthesis -- resulting in mismatched latent spaces. We propose Twins, a unified continuous token space formed by channel-wise concatenating ViT and VAE features on the same token grid, so the sequence length is unchanged and attention cost does not increase. However, jointly modeling Twins in a Diffusion Transformer exposes a severe optimization imbalance: the model fits the ViT component well but struggles to match the VAE latent distribution. We trace this imbalance to three sources of heterogeneity: frequency bias, intrinsic dimensionality, and condition-aligned vs condition-independent uncertainty. To address it, we adapt a focal regression objective for flow matching that upweights large-error VAE dimensions, better balancing optimization across the ViT and VAE components. On ImageNet, this yields up to 10.57 gFID gain over naive MSE loss without classifier-free guidance. Twins also performs competitively on multimodal understanding benchmarks and improves reconstruction fidelity, narrowing the gap between understanding- and generation-oriented representations.

</details>


### [54] [An Explainable FFT-Based Spatial-Frequency Fusion Framework for Deepfake Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.17441)
*Pamela Kirui,Cho Hyuk,Qingzhong Liu,Haodi Jiang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: MSCA-FFT is an FFT-based multi-scale cross-attention framework for image-level deepfake detection that fuses a partially fine-tuned spatial Xception branch with a frequency branch operating on log-scaled FFT magnitude, using cross-attention and an MLP classifier; it outperforms DCT-based fusion methods and baselines, with interpretable Grad-CAM/LIME results highlighting manipulation regions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To improve deepfake detection by leveraging complementary spatial and spectral cues while avoiding heavy reconstruction steps from DCT pipelines; aim for better accuracy and more interpretable localization of manipulated regions.

Method: A partially fine-tuned Xception spatial branch processes the image; an FFT-based frequency branch processes the log-scaled magnitude spectrum through shallow conv layers (no inverse FFT); both representations are refined by transformer encoders, fused via cross-attention, and passed to an MLP classifier for real/fake prediction.

Result: MSCA-FFT achieves consistently higher performance than DCT-based state-of-the-art spatial-frequency fusion methods and other baselines; ablation shows the FFT branch provides complementary spectral cues; Grad-CAM/LIME explanations align with manipulation-sensitive facial regions (eyes, mouth, nose, boundaries).

Conclusion: FFT-based frequency analysis, when fused with spatial features via cross-attention, is effective for image-level deepfake detection and yields interpretable cues around manipulation regions.

Abstract: Deepfake generation has raised growing concerns regarding digital media authenticity, misinformation, identity fraud, and public trust. Recent studies show that combining spatial and frequency features leads to stronger detection results than using independently. This paper presents MSCA-FFT, a Fast Fourier Transform (FFT)-based multi-scale cross-attention framework for image-level deepfake detection. The model combines a partially fine-tuned Xception spatial branch with an FFT-based frequency branch. The frequency branch processes the log-scaled FFT magnitude spectrum through shallow convolutional layers, avoiding inverse frequency-to-image reconstruction used in DCT-based pipelines. The spatial and frequency representations are refined by transformer encoders, fused through cross-attention, and passed to an MLP classifier for real/fake prediction. Experimental results show that MSCA-FFT achieves consistently higher performance than the DCT-based state-of-the-art spatial-frequency fusion method and the compared baseline models. The ablation study further indicates that the FFT-based frequency branch provides complementary spectral cues when fused with spatial features. In addition, FFT-based frequency analysis and Grad-CAM/LIME explanations show consistent evidence around manipulation-sensitive facial regions, including the eyes, mouth, nose, and facial boundaries.

</details>


<div id='cs.AI'></div>

# cs.AI [[Back]](#toc)

### [55] [FlowEvo: Self-Evolving Agents through the Co-Evolution of Workflows and Executable Skills](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21596)
*Zeyu Ren,Ling Yue,Ran Li,Yishu Wang,Shengxiang Xu,Hanmo Liu,Shaowu Pan,Shimin Di*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: FlowEvo compiles successful inference-time workflows into reusable skill records, enabling a training-free workflow–skill loop that improves task-solving without updating model parameters; shows strong accuracy/efficiency gains on ALFWorld, HumanEval, GSM8K; code released.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Procedures discovered during execution are transient and not retained for future tasks, limiting reusability and scalability of LLM agent workflows.

Method: Three coupled mechanisms: (1) workflow-to-skill compilation extracting reusable executable artifacts; (2) skill-to-workflow feedback retrieving skills to support future problems via direct execution or structured injection; (3) skill curation monitoring utility and suppressing negative transfer; admission/replay/safety checks; skill bank persists at inference time.

Result: On ALFWorld: 82.8% success, 23.6 percentage points above strongest baseline; token usage per episode is less than half that of the most efficient baseline; HumanEval and GSM8K show improved accuracy-cost tradeoff; controlled ablations confirm each mechanism's contribution; code publicly available at the provided repository.

Conclusion: Training-free FlowEvo enables accumulation and refinement of task-solving capabilities over time without parameter updates, improving efficiency and performance across tasks.

Abstract: Large language model agents increasingly solve complex tasks by constructing inference-time workflows that combine reasoning, tool use, and code execution. While such workflows enable flexible problem solving, the useful procedures discovered during execution are often transient: they help solve the current task but are not retained in a form that can systematically benefit future tasks. We present FlowEvo, a training-free framework that compiles successful traces into reusable skill records. Each record pairs a callable artifact with auxiliary structured guidance, and admission applies interface, replay, and safety checks where feasible. These skill records persist in a skill bank at inference time. FlowEvo is organized around three coupled mechanisms: (1)~workflow-to-skill compilation, which extracts reusable executable artifacts from successful traces; (2)~skill-to-workflow feedback, which retrieves accumulated skills to support future problem solving through either direct execution or structured context injection; and (3)~skill curation, which monitors downstream utility and suppresses skills that cause negative transfer. Through this workflow--skill--workflow feedback loop, FlowEvo enables agents to accumulate and refine task-solving capability over time without updating model parameters. Experiments on benchmarks spanning interactive environments (ALFWorld) and code/math generation (HumanEval, GSM8K) show that FlowEvo achieves the best accuracy-cost tradeoff among the evaluated baselines under our implementation settings. On ALFWorld, FlowEvo achieves an 82.8\% success rate, 23.6 percentage points above the strongest baseline, while its average token usage per episode is less than half that of the most efficient baseline. Controlled ablations confirm that each mechanism contributes to the overall result. The code is public at https://github.com/DEFENSE-SEU/FlowEvo.

</details>


### [56] [Risk Is Not the Target: A Monotonic Framework for Evaluating Wildfire Operational Risk Signals](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21597)
*Nicolas Caron,Christophe Guyeux,Maxime Coulmeau,Benjamin Aynes*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Monotonic evaluation for wildfire risk signals; DFE best monotonic balance across risk scale; GRU strong locally but uneven risk distribution; FARS reveals upstream limitations; paradigm: ordinal signal explaining operations; code available.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard ML metrics (F1/IoU) miss operational coherence of continuous risk signals; need framework to evaluate monotonic relation between risk score and operational load.

Method: Compare three systems (DFE index, GRU models, FARS multi-agent + LLM) on Alpes-Maritimes; develop monotonic evaluation measuring whether higher risk scores align with higher operational load (fires, interventions, resources).

Result: DFE exhibits most balanced monotonic behavior despite poor classification metrics; GRU shows strong local monotonicity but poor distribution of risk levels; FARS reveals upstream signal structural limitations.

Conclusion: Good risk model is one with ordinal scale that meaningfully explains operational dynamics; shift from predictive accuracy to monotonic-operational alignment; code available.

Abstract: Evaluating wildfire risk systems using standard machine-learning metrics such as F1-score or IoU is fundamentally flawed: these metrics assess event prediction accuracy, not the operational coherence of a continuous risk signal. This work proposes a novel monotonic evaluation framework that measures whether increases in a predicted risk score consistently correspond to increases in observed operational load, such as number of fires, intervention time, and deployed resources. Moreover, we compare three structurally different approaches on the French Alpes-Maritimes department: the expert-based DFE index, GRU- based predictive models, and FARS, a hybrid multi-agent system combining predictive AI with LLM-based reasoning. Experimental results reveal that the DFE, despite poor classification metrics, exhibits the most balanced monotonic behavior across the full risk scale. GRU models achieve strong local monotonicity but fail to produce well-distributed risk levels. FARS inherits and reveals the structural limitations of upstream signals rather than correcting them. The central finding is a paradigm shift: a good risk model does not predict fires accurately, but one whose ordinal scale meaningfully explains operational dynamics, as proved in this paper. Code of the monotonic framework is available on github.

</details>


### [57] [Securing Multimodal AI through Internal Information Decomposition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21600)
*Jehyeok Yeon,Hyeonjeong Ha,Qiusi Zhan,Heng Ji*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: FlowGuard uses cross-modal consistency signals (FlowVectors) based on Partial Information Decomposition to detect harmful multimodal inputs at inference time. Trained as one-class on benign data, it reduces attack success from >90% to <15% with minimal utility loss and faster latency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multimodal LLMs introduce attack surfaces not present in unimodal systems. Adversaries can distribute malicious intent across text and vision modalities; existing defenses that inspect raw inputs/outputs miss the internal fusion process, making them brittle and costly.

Method: Compute FlowVectors that quantify cross-modal redundancy, synergy, and modality-specific dominance from unimodal and fused predictions. Train a one-class classifier on benign FlowVector profiles to detect anomalous fusion behavior during inference, enabling lightweight, inference-time detection of harmful inputs.

Result: Significant defense gains: Attack Success Rate drops from >90% to <15% on unseen attacks; utility loss under 3%; latency improvement up to 6x; demonstrates effectiveness of monitoring cross-modal consistency.

Conclusion: Cross-modal consistency monitoring is an effective, efficient defense for multimodal reasoning. FlowGuard provides a lightweight, inference-time framework leveraging internal fusion signals to detect adversarial inputs.

Abstract: Multimodal large language models introduce attack surfaces absent in unimodal systems: adversaries can distribute malicious intent across modalities to evade unimodal safeguards. This motivates using cross-modal consistency as a detection signal rather than inspecting each modality in isolation. Our key observation is that benign inputs induce compatible predictive behavior from text-only and vision-only reasoning that stabilizes when fused, whereas adversarial manipulation disrupts this consistency, causing abnormal multimodal behavior. Existing defenses that examine raw inputs or outputs overlook this internal fusion process, rendering them brittle and computationally expensive. We propose FlowGuard, a lightweight inference-time framework that detects harmful inputs by monitoring internal multimodal consistency. Unlike approaches that rely on scalar confidence metrics, FlowGuard derives FlowVectors inspired by Partial Information Decomposition that quantify cross-modal redundancy, synergy, and modality-specific dominance, capturing whether fused multimodal predictions remain aligned with unimodal semantic evidence. In a one-class classification problem trained solely on benign data, FlowGuard reduces Attack Success Rates from >90% to <15% on unseen attacks, with <3% utility loss and up to a 6 times latency reduction. Our results demonstrate that monitoring cross-modal consistency offers an efficient and effective defense for multimodal reasoning.

</details>


### [58] [From Frame-Level Recognition to Event-Level Confirmation: Repair Traces and Runtime Failure Analysis of Public-Space Gesture Interaction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21601)
*M. Meng,Yansong Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Deployment-grounded analysis of public-space gesture interactions reveals a recognition-to-interaction gap, with 20 failure instances from 8 repair records across 4 gesture tasks, organized into six non-exclusive failure classes; proposes an event-level runtime abstraction between hand-landmark models and interaction tasks; bounded contributions focused on taxonomy and practical repair insights.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Bridge the gap between frame-level gesture recognition and stable interaction in public-space systems by documenting deployment failures and repairs, rather than pursuing new models or large-scale evaluation.

Method: Empirical study of 8 repair records from a scenic-area interactive kiosk project. Extract 20 failure instances from 4 gesture tasks (two-hand bowing, single-hand fist shaking, two-hand catching control, knowledge-graph node hovering). Classify failures into six non-exclusive working failure classes. Propose an event-level runtime abstraction that separates hand-landmark modeling from interaction task execution to support repair and deployment understanding.

Result: A deployment-grounded failure taxonomy with six classes (model-output degeneration, temporal mismatch, geometric-scale instability, coordinate-rendering mismatch, runtime lifecycle failure, feedback synchronization and recovery failure) and an event-confirmation runtime abstraction; case-study findings highlight practical repair mechanisms and boundaries of contribution.

Conclusion: The paper provides bounded, deployment-focused insights—no new recognition model, no large-scale user evaluation, or quantified accuracy gains—emphasizing taxonomy, runtime abstraction, and pragmatic repair findings.

Abstract: Public-space gesture interaction is often evaluated as a frame-level recognition problem, but deployed systems expose a different failure boundary. In scenic kiosks, exhibition halls, and service terminals, users experience whether an intended action becomes a stable interaction event, not whether individual hand-landmark frames are correct. We call this the recognition-to-interaction gap.
  This paper analyzes 8 engineering repair records from a scenic-area interactive kiosk project, covering 4 gesture tasks: two-hand bowing, single-hand fist shaking, two-hand catching control, and knowledge-graph node hovering. From these traces, we extract 20 failure instances and organize them into six non-exclusive working failure classes: model-output degeneration, temporal mismatch, geometric-scale instability, coordinate-rendering mismatch, runtime lifecycle failure, and feedback synchronization and recovery failure.
  We further organize recurring repair mechanisms into an event-level runtime abstraction between the hand-landmark model and the interaction task. The contribution is deliberately bounded: a deployment-grounded failure taxonomy, an event-confirmation runtime abstraction, and case-study findings. We do not claim a new recognition model, large-scale user evaluation, or quantified accuracy gains.

</details>


### [59] [Transferable Latency Prediction for Fast LLM Screening on Heterogeneous Edge Devices](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21602)
*Xiaolong Tu,Vinod K. Mishra,Venkat R. Dasari,Anu G. Bourgeois,Haoxin Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A runtime-aware, calibration-friendly latency predictor for heterogeneous edge platforms that models each LLM inference as a hardware-runtime-model-prompt config, separating prefill and decode steps and fusing static descriptors with dynamic telemetry using a gated predictor.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Accurate latency prediction on diverse edge hardware is essential for deploying LLMs efficiently. Latency is affected by model architecture, prompts, runtime backend, hardware utilization, DVFS, and thermal variation; static predictors perform poorly across devices and runtime conditions.

Method: Encode each inference as a hardware-runtime-model-prompt configuration. Decompose latency into prefill and decode phases. Use a gated model to selectively fuse static descriptors with dynamic hardware telemetry. Employ lightweight calibration to adapt predictions across devices. Deploy and profile on Pixel devices and validate profiling pipeline on Jetson Nano, Orange Pi 5 Pro, and RTX 3090-class GPU.

Result: On Pixel 8, full predictor raises total-latency R^2 from 0.953 to 0.960 and decode-latency R^2 from 0.957 to 0.973 over static baseline. On Pixel 8 Pro, prefill-latency R^2 improves from -1.383 to 0.966. Cross-device calibration improves Pixel 8 Pro to Pixel 8 total-latency R^2 from -0.974 to 0.940 and decode-latency R^2 from -1.085 to 0.927. Heterogeneous profiling shows latency varies by device (e.g., SmolLM2: 8.42 tokens/s on Orange Pi 5 Pro vs 64.38 tokens/s on RTX 3090).

Conclusion: Runtime-aware latency prediction with lightweight calibration enables more accurate, deployment-oriented LLM selection across heterogeneous edge platforms and can reduce profiling costs.

Abstract: Accurate latency prediction is critical for deploying large language models (LLMs) on heterogeneous edge devices, where inference latency is affected by model architecture, prompt behavior, runtime backend, hardware utilization, dynamic voltage and frequency scaling (DVFS), and thermal variation. This paper presents a runtime-aware latency prediction framework for deployment-oriented LLM selection. The framework represents each inference request as a hardware-runtime-model-prompt configuration, separates inference into prefill and decode phases, and adaptively fuses static descriptors with dynamic hardware telemetry through a gated prediction model.
  We evaluate the framework using Pixel mobile devices and validate the profiling pipeline on Jetson Nano, Orange Pi 5 Pro, and an RTX 3090-class GPU platform. On Pixel 8, the full predictor improves total-latency R-squared from 0.953 to 0.960 and decode-latency R-squared from 0.957 to 0.973 over a static-only baseline. On Pixel 8 Pro, it improves prefill-latency R-squared from -1.383 to 0.966. For cross-device transfer, calibration improves Pixel 8 Pro to Pixel 8 total-latency R-squared from -0.974 to 0.940 and decode-latency R-squared from -1.085 to 0.927. Heterogeneous profiling further shows that latency is highly device- and runtime-dependent: the same SmolLM2 model family reaches 8.42 tokens/s on Orange Pi 5 Pro but 64.38 tokens/s on an RTX 3090-class GPU. These results demonstrate that runtime-aware prediction with lightweight calibration can reduce profiling cost and support latency-aware LLM deployment across heterogeneous edge platforms.

</details>


### [60] [AgentKVShift: Efficient KV Cache Reuse for Agentic Memory Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21604)
*Nilesh Prasad Pandey,Jason Kong,Lanxiang Hu,Quanling Zhao,Yujie Zhao,Onat Gungor,Hao Zhang,Tajana Rosing*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AgentKVShift is a training-free, probe-guided KV residual correction for memory-augmented LLM agents that converts a small cache-refresh budget into near-full recompute performance by estimating a shared memory-level offset from a probe and applying per-token corrections, outperforming prior KV reuse methods across 3B–32B models and long-horizon benchmarks, and is compatible with KV cache quantization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Memory-augmented LLM agents retain long histories, but re-encoding retrieved, metadata-rich memory units into KV states is costly and dominates prefill latency. Existing training-free KV reuse methods are designed for raw passages and degrade on structured, agentic memories.

Method: AgentKVShift operates per retrieved memory unit. It identifies a shared memory-level offset plus small token-wise fluctuations in the KV residuals, estimates the offset from a small probe set, and applies a single weighted correction to all reused tokens. It also corrects tokens not recomputed, turning the refresh budget into useful signal across the entire memory chunk. It is orthogonal to KV cache quantization.

Result: Across 4 open-source LLMs (3B–32B) and two long-horizon memory benchmarks, AgentKVShift achieves near-full recompute with only 10–30% cache refresh, requiring up to 5x less recompute to reach this level versus baselines (which need 45–55%). It yields 2–3.5x prefill speedups over no-KV-reuse on a single A100 and maintains >2x F1 under aggressive 2- and 4-bit KV quantization.

Conclusion: AgentKVShift delivers efficient, high-accuracy KV cache reuse for memory-augmented LLM agents, enabling substantial inference speedups and compatibility with quantization, addressing structured memory challenges where prior reuse methods falter.

Abstract: Memory-augmented LLM agents maintain context across hundreds of interactions through agentic memory systems that actively curate retrieved content with LLM-generated metadata such as summaries, keywords, and tags. From an inference cost standpoint, every retrieval triggers a full re-encoding of these structured memory units into Key-Value (KV) states, which dominates prefill latency. Existing training-free KV reuse methods mitigate this by selectively recomputing a small fraction of tokens, but were designed for RAG-style raw passages and degrade on structured agentic memories. We present AgentKVShift, a training-free, probe-guided KV residual correction method that operates per retrieved memory unit. One of the crucial insights we demonstrate is that the per-memory KV reuse residual decomposes into a shared memory-level offset plus small token-wise fluctuations. Estimating this offset from a small probe set allows us to correct every reused token by a single weighted correction. Unlike prior reuse methods which decide which tokens to recompute and leave the rest of the cache stale, AgentKVShift also corrects the tokens it does not recompute, turning the refresh budget into useful signal across the entire chunk. Across four open-source LLMs spanning 3B to 32B parameters and two long-horizon agentic memory benchmarks (long-term dialogue and agentic applications), AgentKVShift achieves near full recompute performance while refreshing only 10-30% of the cache, outperforming baselines at the same recompute ratio. It requires up to 5x lower recompute to reach this near-full performance, which prior reuse methods only attain at 45-55% refresh. In this regime, AgentKVShift delivers prefill speedups of 2-3.5x over no-KV-reuse on a single A100. AgentKVShift orthogonally composes with KV cache quantization, retaining over 2x the F1 of prior reuse methods under aggressive 2- and 4-bit settings.

</details>


### [61] [TILT: Improving Compositional Generation in Diffusion Models with a Model-Intrinsic Reward](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21606)
*Debottam Dutta,Jaehoon Hahm,Jianchong Chen,Romit Roy Choudhury*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: TILT introduces a training-free test-time framework for compositional text-to-image generation that uses an intrinsic KL-constrained reward to tilt diffusion sampling toward joint presence of multiple concepts, yielding two guidance strategies and a hybrid approach that improves compositional alignment without external supervision.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Compositional prompts frequently fail due to overlap between joint and single-concept distributions; a test-time, training-free method is needed to steer sampling toward accurate concept combination without external reward models.

Method: Interpret overlap modes between joint and single-concept distributions, define an intrinsic reward that favors samples where all concepts co-occur, and derive a KL-constrained tilted target distribution with a closed-form solution. This yields two guidance strategies based on concept interaction and a hybrid approach that balances them for stronger performance.

Result: On prompts from T2ICompBench, the method improves compositional alignment while preserving image quality relative to previous baselines.

Conclusion: Training-free, reward-aligned diffusion sampling can effectively improve compositional fidelity in text-to-image generation without external supervision, by exploiting an intrinsic, KL-constrained reward and two guiding strategies (with a beneficial hybrid).

Abstract: Recent advances in powerful text-to-image generation models have made it increasingly important to develop test-time methods that modify the sampling trajectory to produce images more faithful to complex compositional prompts. We present TILT, a training-free framework for compositional text-to-image generation via test-time reward alignment. We interpret compositional failures as overlap modes between joint and single-concept distributions, and define a reward that favors samples where all concepts are jointly present. This reward is intrinsic to the base model and does not require any external supervision or reward models. This yields a KL-constrained objective with a closed-form tilted target distribution and principled guiding steps for diffusion sampling. The interaction of concept distributions together with the above reward naturally leads to two different guidance strategies while a hybrid approach that balances their respective benefits produces stronger performance. Experiments on prompts from T2ICompBench show that our method improves compositional alignment while preserving image quality compared to previous baselines.

</details>


### [62] [Spectral Flow Certificates for Depth-Aware Long-Range Propagation in Graph Neural Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21607)
*Ranjan Veerabhadraswamy,Ajith Jubilson Emerson*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A single Laplacian-derived scalar, the Spectral Flow Certificate (SFC), predicts whether a graph's topology permits sufficient long-range information flow for GNNs, without training, by combining algebraic connectivity with depth, and it outperforms traditional metrics in predicting long-range accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing GNNs lack a cheap pre-training diagnostic to assess whether a graph's structure will support information propagation over multiple layers; we need a depth-aware, spectral indicator of topology bottlenecks.

Method: Compute a normalized Laplacian of the graph; derive a scalar SFC that fuses algebraic connectivity with the chosen message-passing depth; SFCs adapt as depth increases and require only a single eigenvalue computation, no training or labels.

Result: SFCs explain >2x more variance in trained long-range accuracy than classical measures like average effective resistance or diameter; on 25 synthetic graph families and 150 real molecular graphs, SFCs predict training accuracy before gradients with >90% explanatory power across tested depths.

Conclusion: A single eigenvalue-based metric suffices to flag topology-limited graphs before expensive training, offering a principled pre-deployment filter for GNNs.

Abstract: Graph Neural Networks propagate information through local message passing, but the graph topologies themselves can silently prevent any amount of training from solving long-range tasks. When we deploy GNNs on new graphs, there is currently no inexpensive way to know, before training begins, whether the graphs' structures will allow information to travel far enough between distant nodes. We address this gap by proposing Spectral Flow Certificates (SFCs), single scalars computed from the graphs' normalised Laplacians in seconds, requiring no model training and no labelled data. An SFC fuses a graph's algebraic connectivity with the chosen message-passing depth into one number that measures how much of the critical spectral bottleneck can be traversed within the available depth budget. Unlike raw spectral gaps, which are static and depth-agnostic, SFCs adapt as the number of layers increases and therefore carry strictly more diagnostic information when depths vary. Compared with classical structural statistics such as average effective resistance and graph diameter, SFCs explain more than twice as much variance in trained GNN long-range accuracy. Across twenty-five synthetic graph families spanning paths, cycles, grids, regular graphs, and random graphs, SFCs predict trained accuracy before any gradients are computed, achieving explanatory power above ninety percent at all tested depths. The same predictive relationships hold on one hundred fifty real molecular graph topologies drawn from three independent benchmark datasets, confirming that the findings are not artefacts of their synthetic construction. Taken together, these results show that a single eigenvalue computation is sufficient to flag topology-limited graphs before committing to expensive training pipelines, providing a principled first filter for GNN deployments.

</details>


### [63] [Coupled Hierarchical Search over Topology and Execution for Agentic Workflow Synthesis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21609)
*Dong Li,Yanchi Liu,Xujiang Zhao,Wei Cheng,Zhengzhang Chen,Xintao Wu,Zhong Chen,Chen Zhao,Haifeng Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: HierFlow is a training-free, test-time hierarchical search framework that automates LLM workflow design by coupling topology optimization with execution-guided search, using a gating mechanism to trigger sub-workflow searches; proven effective across QA, math reasoning, and code generation with improved efficiency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Automating structured workflow generation for LLMs is hampered by a combinatorial search space and dependency on offline training; a training-free, adaptive approach that integrates topology and execution can achieve higher efficiency and flexibility.

Method: Proposes HierFlow: an intertwined topology-and-execution search architecture combining feedback-guided topology adjustments with a fast, MCTS-inspired tree search for sub-workflow optimization. Includes an intelligent gating module that selectively triggers execution-level searches based on context, and analyzes how cross-task coupling affects hierarchical splitting. No training overhead.

Result: Empirical evaluation across QA, mathematical reasoning, and code generation shows HierFlow consistently outperforms strong baselines, delivering high-quality results with favorable computational efficiency and without additional training.

Conclusion: The work demonstrates that joint topology-execution search can enable effective, training-free workflow design. The gating mechanism and cross-task coupling analysis provide practical insights for scalable hierarchical planning in LLMs.

Abstract: Although structured workflows empower Large Language Models (LLMs) to tackle complex problems, automating their creation is severely hindered by a vast combinatorial search space, frequently resulting in inflexible and resource-heavy offline training dependencies. To address this, we conceptualize workflow generation as an intertwined topology-and-execution search paradigm, where the broader topological layer dictates subtask boundaries and lower-level execution outcomes actively reshape the topology itself. Building on this foundation, we introduce HierFlow, a training-free, test-time hierarchical search architecture that automates agentic workflow design by merging feedback-guided topology adjustments with a fast, MCTS-inspired tree search for sub-workflow optimization. HierFlow maximizes efficiency through an intelligent gating module that selectively triggers execution-level searches based on contextual necessity, a mechanism we further support with an in-depth analysis detailing how varying degrees of cross-task coupling impact the effectiveness of hierarchical splitting. Comprehensive testing across question answering, mathematical reasoning, and code generation benchmarks confirms that HierFlow consistently outperforms strong baselines, delivering an optimal balance of high-quality results and computational efficiency without any additional training overhead.

</details>


### [64] [SCOPE and SCION: A Benchmark and an Auditable Reference Pipeline for Schema Induction and Fusion from Text](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21610)
*Miaobo Hu,Xiaobo Guo,Shuhao Hu,Bokun Wang,Rui Chen,Xin Wang,Daren Zha,Jun Xiao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A train-text-only benchmark and auditable reference pipeline (SCOPE/SCION) enable corpus-to-schema induction and optional schema fusion from raw text, with strong results on a normalized schema-graph benchmark and reusable, reproducible artifacts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Addresses the bottleneck of schema graphs in information extraction/knowledge graph construction by enabling end-to-end schema construction from text and providing an auditable, reproducible pipeline and benchmark.

Method: SCOPE is built from 24 public information extraction sources, producing evaluation-only gold schema graphs; SCION acts as an auditable reference pipeline that constructs candidate spaces, applies restricted naming/merging/validation, and conservative fusion under strict JSON contracts. Variants include SCION-lite (high F1 among baselines) and SCION-RL (open-model, less reliance on proprietary LLM schema engineers). Evaluation uses Literal, Fuzzy, Continuous, and Graph schema-graph metrics against normalized typed-edge targets. The release includes evidence-linked outputs, logs, code, and benchmarks at the provided URL.

Result: SCION-lite achieves the highest F1 among released source-schema references and baselines on the SCOPE core suite; SCION-RL reduces reliance on proprietary LLM schema engineers while maintaining competitive performance. Results are reported against normalized targets rather than claims of superior human ontologies.

Conclusion: The work delivers a reproducible, auditable end-to-end framework for corpus-to-schema induction and optional schema fusion, plus a public benchmark suite enabling robust evaluation of schema-construction methods on raw text.

Abstract: Schema graphs are an upstream bottleneck of schema-grounded information extraction and knowledge graph construction, yet most extraction systems assume the schema is already available. We introduce SCOPE (Schema Construction and Ontology-induction Pipeline Evaluation), a train-text-only benchmark for corpus-to-schema induction and optional schema fusion from raw text, built from 24 public information extraction sources (15 RE and 9 EE) normalized into evaluation-only gold schema graphs; its core event-extraction target covers event types and within-event argument roles, with inter-event links reported separately. We present SCION (Schema Construction and Induction with Ontology Normalization), an auditable reference pipeline rather than a new extraction architecture; it constructs candidate spaces from train text and restricts naming, merging, filtering, validation, and conservative fusion to candidate-linked evidence under strict JSON contracts. On the SCOPE core suite, SCION-lite attains the highest F1 among released source-schema references, Text2Onto-style, LLM-only, and matched extract-then-aggregate baselines under Literal, Fuzzy, Continuous, and Graph schema-graph metrics, while the compact open-model SCION-RL variant reduces reliance on proprietary LLM schema engineers. These results are reported against normalized typed-edge targets rather than as claims that induced schemas surpass human ontology design; the release includes evidence-linked outputs, parse/fallback logs, candidate retention/merging logs, run manifests, code, and benchmark packages at https://github.com/wandugu/paper_scion.

</details>


### [65] [Procedural Knowledge Is Not Low-Rank: Why LoRA Fails to Internalize Multi-Step Procedures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21612)
*Simon Dennis,Kevin Shabahang,Hao Guo,Rivaan Patil*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LoRA underperforms full fine-tuning for procedural, multi-step tasks across several domains; even at high ranks, a large portion of the task-relevant parameter updates remains inaccessible to low-rank adapters, and increasing rank yields limited gains.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Assess whether parameter-efficient fine-tuning (LoRA) can match full fine-tuning for procedural knowledge tasks that require following multi-step procedures with conditional branching.

Method: Systematic ablation across LoRA ranks r=16–128 on a 14-node procedural travel booking task; cross-domain replication on Zoom support (14 nodes) and insurance claims (55 nodes) at 8B; evaluation via task success and conversation completion; and SVD analysis of weight updates comparing LoRA vs full fine-tuning.

Result: LoRA configurations fail uniformly on procedural tasks (task success <= 2.54 vs 4.11 for full FT; all p<0.001); performance declines with higher ranks; conversation completion remains 95–99% while task success stays low. Cross-domain gaps of 0.8–2.2 points at r=32 and r=128 for 8B models. Increasing rank from 32 to 128 provides marginal gains and does not close the gap. SVD shows mean effective update rank 761–1,026; rank 128 captures only 43–51% of the squared Frobenius norm.

Conclusion: For procedural tasks, LoRA falls short of full fine-tuning; this limitation challenges its use in agentic or planning-focused applications where multi-step reasoning and branching are essential.

Abstract: Parameter-efficient fine-tuning methods like LoRA have become the default for adapting large language models, succeeding across instruction following, style transfer, and factual adaptation. We show that for procedural knowledge--the ability to follow multi-step procedures with conditional branching through to terminal states--LoRA fails to match full fine-tuning at the ranks where it retains its efficiency advantage. In a systematic ablation (r = 16--128) on a procedural travel booking task (14 nodes), all LoRA configurations fail uniformly (task success <= 2.54 vs. 4.11 for full fine-tuning, all p < 0.001), with scores decreasing at higher ranks--despite maintaining 95--99% conversation completion rates. Cross-domain replication on Zoom support (14 nodes) and insurance claims (55 nodes) at 8B confirms the failure generalizes: LoRA underperforms full fine-tuning by 0.8--2.2 points on average at both r = 32 and r = 128, with the largest gap on the most complex procedure. Quadrupling rank from 32 to 128 provides marginal improvement but does not close the gap. SVD analysis of the weight changes produced by full fine-tuning explains why: across three domains at both 3B and 8B, the mean effective rank of the update ranges from 761 to 1,026, and rank 128 captures only 43--51% of the squared Frobenius norm. Together, these findings establish that for procedural tasks LoRA falls well short of full fine-tuning--a fundamental limitation for agentic applications.

</details>


### [66] [The Hard Decision Layer: Evidence for Committed Inference in Transformers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21613)
*Ashwath Vaithinathan Aravindan,Mayank Kejriwal*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A study uncovers a Hard Decision Layer (HDL) in transformer MCQ answering, where option rankings stabilize abruptly during inference. Validated across four models (Qwen, Llama, Granite, Mistral) and four datasets; HDL is invariant to fine-tuning and yields notable accuracy gains (up to +0.61 on CommonsenseQA) before stabilizing. Ablations show the phenomenon is architecture-driven and label/form complexity robust. Implications include insights into transformer inference and potential for efficient reasoning and steering; code is released at the provided repository.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand how transformer-based language models commit to predictions in multiple-choice settings and to identify a robust architectural phenomenon that governs decision dynamics, with potential to improve efficiency and controllability.

Method: Empirical analysis across four LM families and four MCQ benchmarks, tracking answer option rankings during inference to identify abrupt stabilization (HDL). Systematic ablations on label formats and problem complexity; test invariance to fine-tuning; replicate across models/datasets.

Result: Identification of HDL as a consistent, architecture-driven stabilization of option rankings during inference, with no need for learned routing policies. HDL yields accuracy gains (up to +0.61 on CommonsenseQA) and persists after fine-tuning. Results hold across label formats and varying problem complexity; code and data are available.

Conclusion: HDL is a fundamental, architecture-induced phenomenon in transformer inference, offering mechanistic insight into decisions and avenues for efficient reasoning and model steering. The work provides reproducible evidence across multiple models and datasets.

Abstract: We investigate where and how transformer-based language models commit to predictions in multiple-choice question answering. We identify the _Hard Decision Layer_ (HDL), a natural architectural property where answer option rankings stabilize abruptly during inference. Empirical validation across four language models (Qwen, Llama, Granite, Mistral) and four benchmark datasets demonstrates consistent HDL emergence without learned routing policies. We also show that the HDL is invariant to fine-tuning. Our results reveal striking accuracy improvements at the HDL: up to +0.61 (Qwen on CommonsenseQA), after which performance stabilizes. Systematic ablations on label formats and problem complexity confirm the phenomenon is fundamental to model architecture. These findings offer mechanistic insights into transformer inference and suggest opportunities for efficient reasoning and model steering. All code and results required to reproduce this work are available in https://github.com/Mystic-Slice/hard-decision-layer

</details>


### [67] [Household Movement Detection in Mixed-Format Occupancy Data Using LLM-Based Entity Resolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21614)
*Sasirekha Oguri,John R. Talburt,Mert Can Cakmak*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: An AI-enhanced framework detects indirect entity links via household movement in unstandardized name-address data, using prompt-based LLM NER, semantic embeddings, and graph reasoning to improve ER recall with maintained precision on synthetic datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard ER based on pairwise similarities misses indirect relationships, such as groups moving together between addresses. Noisy, mixed-format records and lack of stable identifiers exacerbate this. Detecting household movement can boost linkage quality.

Method: 1) Prompt-based LLM NER to extract personal names and addresses with minimal preprocessing. 2) Semantic embeddings to compute robust similarity. 3) Graph-based reasoning to infer group-level movement patterns and indirect links. 4) Evaluation on SPX benchmark S8-S12 datasets generated by the Synthetic Occupancy Generator.

Result: Recall improved by 8-15% and F1-score gains of 6-8% over a strong pairwise baseline, while maintaining high precision.

Conclusion: Incorporating indirect household movement evidence via LLM NER, embeddings, and graph reasoning enhances ER in unstandardized data. The approach is effective on synthetic occupancy data; future work should assess real-world generalization and address prompt reliability and data quality challenges.

Abstract: Entity resolution (ER) typically relies on pairwise similarity comparisons between records, which limits its ability to capture indirect relationships present in demographic occupancy data. An important indirect pattern arises from household movement, where multiple individuals relocate together across addresses, but detecting such patterns is difficult due to mixed-format records, noise, duplication, and the absence of stable identifiers. This paper proposes an AI-enhanced framework for detecting indirect entity links associated with household movement in unstandardized name-address data. The approach integrates prompt-based large language model (LLM) named entity recognition for extracting personal names and addresses without extensive preprocessing, semantic text embeddings for robust similarity computation, and graph-based reasoning to infer group-level movement patterns. Experimental evaluation on SPX benchmark datasets (S8-S12) generated using the Synthetic Occupancy Generator demonstrates that incorporating indirect household movement evidence improves recall by 8-15% while maintaining high precision, yielding F1-score gains of 6-8% over a strong pairwise baseline.

</details>


### [68] [FrED: External Data Influence Estimation via Domain Knowledge Graph Grounding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21615)
*Theodoros Aivalis,Iraklis A. Klampanos,Antonis Troumpoukis,Joemon M. Jose*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A black-box data-attribution framework for generative AI that combines continuous feature similarity with discrete Knowledge Graphs (KGs) to ground post-hoc attribution in domain structure, without model access.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address the need for transparent, domain-grounded training-data attribution in generative models, especially when model weights are inaccessible; improve over naive similarity while incorporating structural context.

Method: A probabilistic framework operating in a black-box setting that fuses continuous feature similarities with discrete domain-specific KGs. It rewards specific historical samples leveraging KG structure to prevent generic data from dominating, and it uses domain KGs to retrieve physically consistent analogs for downstream tasks; evaluated as post-hoc influence analysis.

Result: Across abstract artistic image synthesis and environmental forecasting, the method yields a strong Linear Datamodeling Score surpassing standard black-box baselines and approaching gradient-based estimators; cross-domain case study improves geographic localization over latent-only baselines.

Conclusion: The approach provides an efficient, interpretable, black-box post-hoc attribution mechanism that grounds data influence in domain structure, enabling domain-consistent retrieval and analysis without internal model access.

Abstract: The rapid deployment of generative AI has amplified the critical need for Training Data Attribution to ensure transparency and accountability. However, current parametric approaches require computationally prohibitive access to model weights, while similarity-based methods ignore deep structural context. We propose a novel probabilistic framework that operates entirely in a black-box setting. Our method fuses continuous feature similarities with discrete, domain-specific Knowledge Graphs (KGs). This approach ensures the attribution is grounded in structural reality, explicitly rewarding highly specific historical samples while preventing generic background data from dominating the results. We evaluate our framework across two distinct domains where linking outputs to data and domain context is inherently complex: abstract artistic image synthesis and high-dimensional physical weather forecasting. Extensive benchmarking demonstrates the robust efficacy of our approach. In the artistic domain, it achieves a strong Linear Datamodeling Score that exceeds standard black-box similarity baselines, while closing much of the gap to gradient-based estimators. We additionally present a cross-domain feasibility case study in environmental forecasting, where we use domain KGs to retrieve physically consistent historical analogs for regional flood forecasts, improving geographic localisation over a latent-only baseline. Operating entirely without internal model access, our approach provides an efficient, interpretable mechanism for post-hoc influence analysis and domain-grounded retrieval.

</details>


### [69] [Lost in Context: Addressing Context Anxiety in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21616)
*Ifueko Igbinedion,Jillian Ross,Etienne Ricardez,Sertac Karaman,Eric So*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Context anxiety in frontier reasoning models causes premature self-doubt and inefficiency; improving self-assessment of task length enables alternative long-horizon strategies and performance gains without solely scaling capabilities.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand why capable frontier models fail on long-horizon tasks: whether self-doubt stems from misjudging required token budget and its impact on efficiency, and whether better self-awareness can unlock alternative strategies.

Method: Systematic study comprising evaluation of models' token-budget estimation, measurement of efficiency losses under perceived constraints, and experiments enabling alternative problem-solving strategies that avoid context anxiety; across long-horizon tasks.

Result: Context anxiety partially arises from inaccurate estimates of tokens needed; it causes material efficiency losses when models operate under perceived limits; models can learn strategies to solve long-horizon problems without experiencing context anxiety, implying gains via better self-assessment rather than mere scaling.

Conclusion: Improving models' ability to assess and adapt to their own limitations can yield performance improvements on long-horizon tasks; context anxiety is a tractable bottleneck and a target for training and inference-time interventions.

Abstract: Conventional wisdom suggests that reasoning models fail when problems exceed their capabilities. However, we find that frontier reasoning models sometimes possess the necessary capabilities to solve problems but fail due to premature self-doubt -- a phenomenon informally known as context anxiety. We provide the first systematic study of context anxiety, demonstrating that it arises, in part, from a model's inability to accurately estimate the tokens required to complete a task. We also show that context anxiety leads to material efficiency losses when models operate under perceived constraints. Building on this analysis, we further show that models can learn alternative strategies for solving long-horizon problems without exhibiting context anxiety, suggesting that performance improvements may be achievable not through scaling model capabilities, but by improving models' ability to accurately assess and adapt to their own limitations.

</details>


### [70] [Do VLMs Read or Rewrite? On Transcription Faithfulness in Vision-Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21617)
*Gwang Gook Lee,Kenan Emir Ak,Jay Mohta,Yan Xu,Dimitrios Dimitriadis*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: VLMs are not always faithful transcribers under imperfect text; FaithC4 reveals rewriting behavior across multilingual documents and perturbations; results show varying robustness among model types and a rewriting mechanism tied to internal FFN representations and word length.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: OCR benchmarks miss transcription fidelity failures in VLMs. There is a need to quantify how VLMs handle perturbed text and to compare general VLMs, OCR-specialized VLMs, and traditional OCR on a multilingual, perturbation-based benchmark.

Method: Construct FaithC4: 1,455 single-page documents (English, Chinese, Korean) with three perturbations (scramble, random substitution, visually similar substitution). Evaluate 15 systems spanning three categories (general-purpose VLMs, OCR-specialized VLMs, traditional OCR). Layer-by-layer probing of Qwen3-VL-4B to determine when rewriting occurs, analyze WER degradation, and study the influence of word length on rewriting behavior.

Result: General-purpose VLMs show up to 4.5-point WER degradation under perturbation; OCR-specialized VLMs show 0.2–2 points; traditional OCR <0.6 points on English. Rewriting occurs when perturbed word FFN final-layer representations stay near the original encoding; if representations diverge, transcription is faithful. Short words (4–6 chars) are rewritten up to ~10% of the time, with a sharp cutoff at 8 chars above which rewriting drops to 0%.

Conclusion: VLMs are not universally faithful transcribers; FaithC4 provides a targeted benchmark to expose transcription fidelity issues across languages and perturbations; the rewriting behavior is governed by internal representations and is influenced by word length. This motivates improved model design and evaluation that prioritize faithful transcription for document understanding.

Abstract: Vision Language Models (VLMs) are increasingly used in place of traditional OCR pipelines for document understanding. In this paper, we show they do not always act as faithful transcribers: when text is imperfect, they often tend to rewrite it into a more plausible form - a behavior that clean-text OCR benchmarks cannot detect. We introduce FaithC4, a multilingual perturbation benchmark of 1,455 single-page documents (English, Chinese, Korean) with three perturbation families: scramble, random substitution, and visually similar substitution. We use the benchmark to evaluate 15 systems spanning general-purpose VLMs, OCR-specialized VLMs, and traditional OCR pipelines. These three categories differ in WER degradation under perturbation: general-purpose VLMs degrade by up to 4.5 points, OCR-specialized VLMs by 0.2-2 points, and traditional OCR by less than 0.6 points on English. Probing Qwen3-VL-4B layer-by-layer, we identify a consistent pattern: rewriting fires only when a perturbed word's final layer FFN representation stays close to the original encoding; when the representation diverges sufficiently, the model transcribes faithfully. Word length affects rewriting rate: short words (4-6 characters) are rewritten up to 10% of the time, with a sharp cutoff at 8 characters above which rewriting drops to 0%.

</details>


### [71] [LeafData: An Agentic System for Data Migration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21618)
*Sadanand Katukuri,Rajasekhar Bada,Navya Induri,Rohit Gandham,Lynette Pinto,Joses Selvan,Abishek Krishnamoorthy,Joseph Rozario,Pu Tian,Pavan Poudel,Yalong Wu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LeafData is an agentic system that converts user intents into validated, executable JSON configuration for data migration, using a chat-based frontend and a backend service to produce orchestration-ready artifacts across heterogeneous data sources.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Data migration is complex and error-prone when driven by manual JSON configuration. An interactive, schema-validated approach lowers the barrier, reduces errors, and speeds up end-to-end pipeline generation and execution.

Method: A frontend chatbot incrementally gathers required information and performs schema-driven validation; a backend service processes validated inputs to generate JSON configuration artifacts directly consumable by orchestration platforms. The system supports heterogeneous data sources (relational databases, file systems, document stores, REST APIs) via connectors.

Result: Generates validated, executable JSON configuration artifacts that are directly consumable by orchestration platforms, enabling end-to-end pipeline generation and execution without manual coding.

Conclusion: LeafData offers a user-friendly, extensible solution for data migration across diverse sources, streamline configuration-to-execution workflow, and reduce reliance on specialized domain knowledge.

Abstract: Modern data migration relies on JSON configuration to define data connection, pipeline logic, and orchestration behavior. This requires domain knowledge from users and is time-consuming and error-prone. In this paper, we present LeafData, an agentic system that converts user intent into validated and executable JSON configuration for data migration. Specifically, LeafData comprises a frontend chatbot and the backend service. The chatbot incrementally collects required information from users and performs schema-driven validation, while the backend service processes validated inputs and generates JSON configuration artifacts. These artifacts are directly consumable by orchestration platforms, enabling end-to-end pipeline generation and execution without manual coding. LeafData supports heterogeneous data migration across various data sources and connectors including relational databases, file-based systems, document-oriented databases, and REST APIs.

</details>


### [72] [From Profiles to Steering Vectors: Global Sparse Priors and Local Semantic Calibration for Personalized Text Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21620)
*Liuji Chen,Zeyu Zhang,Xinyuan Zhang,Shuai Nie,Qiang Liu,Shu Wu,Liang Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: GLASS is a training-free, personalization framework using Global-Local Activation Steering with Sparse priors to capture user style without retrieval or fine-tuning. It builds a global user-style prior from historical responses via sparse autoencoders and local, contrastive style vectors over clustered scenarios, then injects these vectors during inference to personalize generation. It outperforms baselines on LaMP/LongLaMP (ROUGE, LLM-judge) and shows SAE representations are robust to topic/length shifts, indicating better style-content disentanglement.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To personalize text generation without expensive retrieval or parameter updates and with better separation between stylistic signals and semantic content.

Method: Extract a global user-style prior using sparse autoencoders (SAE) on historical responses. Construct local contrastive style vectors from clustered interaction scenarios. At inference, jointly inject global and local vectors into different layers of the model to achieve context-aware personalization without updates or retrieval.

Result: GLASS outperforms retrieval-, fine-tuning-, and steering-based baselines on LaMP and LongLaMP across ROUGE metrics and LLM-as-judge evaluations. SAE-based representations show robustness to topic and length shifts, suggesting improved disentanglement of style from semantic content.

Conclusion: GLASS provides a training-free path to personalized generation by combining a global user-style prior with locally clustered style signals, enabling effective, context-aware personalization with robust style-content disentanglement and without retraining or storing additional retrieval data.

Abstract: Personalized text generation requires models to capture user-specific writing styles from historical data. Existing approaches based on retrieval, parameter-efficient fine-tuning, or activation steering either introduce inference and storage overhead or struggle to separate stylistic signals from semantic content. We propose GLASS, a training-free framework for personalized generation via Global-Local Activation Steering with Sparse priors. GLASS uses sparse autoencoders to extract a global user-style prior from historical responses and constructs local contrastive style vectors over clustered interaction scenarios. During inference, it jointly injects global and local vectors into different model layers, enabling context-aware personalization without retrieval or parameter updates. Experiments on LaMP and LongLaMP show that GLASS outperforms retrieval-, fine-tuning-, and steering-based baselines across ROUGE metrics and LLM-as-judge evaluations. Further analyses show that SAE-based representations are more robust to topic and length shifts, suggesting better disentanglement of stylistic information from semantic residue.

</details>


### [73] [FBLayout: Optimizing Memory Layout for Efficient LLM Finetuning on Mobile GPUs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21624)
*Kahou Tam,Wei Niu,Yu Bao,Xiaomin Ouyang,Chengzhong Xu,Li Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: FBLayout is a layout-aware co-design framework for mobile GPUs that enables efficient on-device fine-tuning of transformer models by harmonizing tensor layouts across forward/backward passes, avoiding data movement, and guiding layout choices; it yields 2.2–5.7x speedups over existing mobile frameworks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: On-device fine-tuning offers privacy-preserving personalization but is hampered by severe memory constraints and frequent layout transformations in attention during training. Existing mobile training frameworks either use unified layouts causing fragmented memory access and poor GPU utilization, or rely on explicit layout conversions that incur transformation overhead.

Method: FBLayout introduces: (1) a unified R-Tile layout for multi-dimensional reductions across forward/backward passes; (2) tile-based index transformation to eliminate physical data movement; and (3) activation-guided layout selection to propagate efficient layouts globally.

Result: Evaluations on seven transformer models across mobile phones (ARM Mali and Qualcomm Adreno GPUs) show 2.2–5.7x speedup over MNN, TFLite, and TVM, with improved cache efficiency and reduced memory footprint.

Conclusion: FBLayout demonstrates that layout-aware co-design with mobile GPU platforms enables practical on-device large-model fine-tuning, delivering substantial performance and memory benefits.

Abstract: Transformer-based models have enabled unprecedented capabilities across language, vision, and multimodal tasks. On-device fine-tuning of transformer models offers a privacy-preserving path to personalized AI, yet remains inefficient on mobile GPUs due to severe memory constraints and frequent layout transformations in attention mechanism during training. Existing mobile training frameworks either use unified layouts for forward and backward passes -- leading to fragmented memory access and poor GPU utilization during backpropagation -- or rely on explicit layout conversions, which introduce significant transformation overhead.
  To overcome this, we propose FBLayout, a layout-aware framework that co-designs tensor organization with mobile GPU platforms. FBLayout introduces: (1) a unified R-Tile layout for multi-dimensional reductions across forward/backward passes; (2) tile-based index transformation to eliminate physical data movement; and (3) activation-guided layout selection to propagate efficient layouts globally. Evaluations on seven transformer models across different mobile phones (including ARM Mali and Qualcomm Adreno GPUs) show that FBLayout achieves 2.2-5.7x speedup over MNN, TFLite, and TVM, while significantly improving cache efficiency and reducing memory footprint, enabling practical on-device large model fine-tuning.

</details>


### [74] [Trajectory-Aware Retrieval Agents for Temporal Decision- Making](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21625)
*Jing Wang,Jie Shen,Xing Niu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Trajectory Language Model (TLM) integrates SHAP-guided feedback and a latent growth curve model over retrieved chunks to preserve temporal structure in long-form text; it iteratively refines evidence, achieving monotonic improvement in the probability of the correct label and outperforming zero-shot LLMs and standard RAG on temporally grounded tasks (medical QA and financial predictions).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard retrieval-augmented generation fragments temporal context, discarding trajectory information critical for correct decisions; there is a need for models that exploit temporal structure in long-form evidence.

Method: Proposes TLM, a closed-loop agentic framework; uses SHAP-guided feedback to refine evidence; introduces latent growth curve model (LGCM) over retrieved chunk embeddings to detect trajectory trends, turning points, and information gaps; under scorer-calibration, refinement is monotone non-decreasing in probability of correct label.

Result: Empirical evaluation on three temporally grounded tasks: medical QA, earnings call surprise, overnight stock gap prediction. TLM outperforms zero-shot LLMs and standard RAG on the medical task; yields consistent, economically meaningful gains on the financial tasks.

Conclusion: Incorporating trajectory-aware evidence refinement via LGCM and SHAP feedback improves decision quality from long-form temporally structured text, offering interpretable trajectory insights and practical gains in temporally grounded decision tasks.

Abstract: We study the problem of decision-making from long-form, temporally structured text using large language model (LLM) agents. Standard retrievalaugmented generation (RAG) pipelines fragment chronological context into isolated snippets, discarding the temporal structure that is often critical for correct downstream decisions. We introduce TLM (Trajectory Language Model), a closed-loop agentic framework that iteratively refines the evidence set using SHAP-guided feedback. The key technical contribution is the latent growth curve model (LGCM) over retrieved chunk embeddings, which provides an interpretable mechanism for detecting trajectory trends, turning points, and information gaps. We show that, under a scorer-calibration assumption (which holds approximately in practice), the iterative refinement procedure is monotonically non-decreasing in the probability assigned to the correct label. Empirically, TLM is evaluated on three temporally grounded decision tasks: medical question answering, earnings call surprise prediction, and overnight stock gap prediction. TLM substantially outperforms both zero-shot LLM baselines and standard retrieval-augmented approaches on the medical task, and yields consistent, economically meaningful gains on the two financial tasks.

</details>


### [75] [Discrete Action Space as a Prerequisite for GRPO Convergence in Small-Model Continuous Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21626)
*Dmytro Filatov,Valentyn Fedorov,Vira Filatova*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: GRPO fine-tuning of small language models for quadrotor control collapses to zero actions; ablations prevent collapse but don’t enable learning; discretizing to PID presets yields convergence and a Pareto frontier; a classical PID baseline matches the outcome; high-fidelity sim reveals a hover-region distribution gap.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Evaluate the viability of Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) for fine-tuning small language models to perform continuous-control tasks in quadrotor flight, analyze instability issues (entropy collapse), assess cross-model generalization, and compare against classical baselines and high-fidelity dynamics.

Method: Fine-tune Qwen-0.5B (and two other pretrained LMs) with GRPO at 25 Hz quadrotor velocity control. Test ablations: remove the jerk-penalty term; remove the KL anchor to pretrained prior. Replace action interface with a 5-way categorical choice over PID presets. Evaluate on three pretrained LMs and compare to a re-tuned PID baseline (Ki=0.30, vmax=2.5). Use a high-fidelity Crazyflie 2.1 dynamics simulation to probe distribution shift.

Result: GRPO with vanilla LM collapses to trivial zero action: 0% success; entropy falls from 0.35 to 0.03 in 60 steps. Ablations prevent entropy collapse but do not enable learning. With a 5-way PID preset action space, training converges, yielding a Pareto-front of smoothness vs. reliability: at 64 steps, 98.6% success with jerk 0.656 m/s^3; at 256 steps, 100% success with jerk 1.103 m/s^3 (or 0.796 with a velocity cap). Across three LMs. Re-tuned PID baseline achieves 100% at jerk 0.736 m/s^3. High-fidelity Crazyflie 2.1 dynamics reveal a hover-region training-distribution gap.

Conclusion: LM-based fine-tuning for continuous-control tasks via GRPO is fragile: entropy collapse can derail learning; discretizing action space to PID presets enables reliable learning and competitive performance, with classical PID achieving similar results. A distribution shift to high-fidelity dynamics highlights transfer gaps, suggesting the need for more robust alignment between LM policies and physical robot dynamics.

Abstract: We study whether Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) can fine-tune small language models for simulated quadrotor continuous-control tasks.
