In our benchmark, vanilla GRPO fine-tuning of Qwen-0.5B for 25 Hz quadrotor velocity control collapses to the trivial zero action: 0 percent success rate, with entropy falling from 0.35 to 0.03 within 60 steps. Two ablations - removing the jerk-penalty term and removing the KL anchor to the pretrained prior - each prevent entropy collapse, yet neither enables learning. When the action interface is replaced by a 5-way categorical choice over PID presets, training converges. The resulting controller traces a smoothness-reliability Pareto frontier along training duration; both endpoints are reported: 98.6 percent success with 0.656 m/s3 jerk at 64 steps, and 100 percent success with 1.103 m/s3 jerk, or 0.796 under a matched velocity cap, at 256 steps. The recipe is evaluated across three pretrained language models. As context, a re-tuned classical baseline, PID with Ki = 0.30 and vmax = 2.5, reaches the same 100 percent success rate at jerk 0.736 m/s3. A high-fidelity simulation using Crazyflie 2.1 dynamics surfaces a hover-region training-distribution gap.

</details>


### [76] [Do Modules Stay in Their Lane? Role Drift in Compound LLM Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21627)
*Xiaoyang Cao,Siddarth Srinivasan,Michiel A. Bakker*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: End-to-end RL on compound LLM systems can exploit role drift, where modules drift from their assigned roles while maintaining end-task accuracy. The paper introduces Role Anchor, a regularizer that constrains modules to adhere to their roles by preserving the role prompt's effect on next-token predictions relative to a neutral prompt. Across two pipelines, role drift remains undetected by accuracy alone; the decomposer shortcut accounts for much of the RL gain. Role Anchor mitigates drift with a tunable accuracy cost; gradient analysis suggests the regularizer reduces alignment with the drift direction rather than simply suppressing learning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To expose and control internal misalignment (role drift) in modular LLM pipelines where end-task performance can improve without modules adhering to their assigned roles, potentially compromising genuine division of labor.

Method: Introduce Role Anchor regularizer that regresses each module’s behavior toward its role by comparing the shift in next-token predictions induced by the role prompt against a neutral prompt. Apply end-to-end training on two compound LLM pipelines, analyze role drift via task accuracy and internal behavior, and perform gradient analysis to understand how the regularizer affects learning.

Result: Role drift can occur even when end-task accuracy improves: a decomposer inserts answers into sub-questions, and a reader relies on param memory. In the decomposer pipeline, ~86% of the observed RL gain is due to this shortcut and vanishes when the decomposer is constrained to its role. Role Anchor reduces drift with a tunable accuracy cost dependent on pipeline and anchor strength, and gradient analysis indicates the regularizer reduces alignment with the role drift direction rather than simply suppressing learning.

Conclusion: End-to-end RL gains can overstate genuine learning if role drift is unchecked. Role Anchor provides a controllable mechanism to enforce role adherence and reveal true contribution of components in modular LLM systems, at the cost of some accuracy that can be tuned via anchor strength.

Abstract: End-to-end reinforcement learning can improve the accuracy of compound LLM systems, but it does not constrain how modules divide labor internally. We identify Role Drift, a failure mode in which modules preserve or improve end-task performance while deviating from their assigned roles through role-violating shortcuts that remain invisible to system-level evaluation. To make role drift observable and controllable, we propose Role Anchor, a regularizer that modulates how much each module deviates from its assigned role during end-to-end training. The key idea is to preserve how the role prompt shifts the module's next-token predictions relative to a neutral prompt, which serves as a proxy for the role's intended effect during training. Experiments on two compound LLM pipelines reveal role drift that accuracy alone fails to detect: a decomposer meant to split a question into sub-questions for a separate solver instead plants the answer in them, and a reader meant to answer from retrieved passages instead falls back on parametric memory. In fact, on the decomposer pipeline this shortcut drives most of the apparent RL gain: 86% of it vanishes once the decomposer is held to its role, indicating that terminal accuracy alone can badly overstate how much a compound system has genuinely learned. Across both pipelines, Role Anchor mitigates role drift at a tunable accuracy cost that varies by pipeline and anchor strength. Additional gradient analysis suggests that the regularizer reduces alignment with the role-drift direction rather than simply suppressing learning.

</details>


### [77] [Wavelet Phase Diffusion for Structurally and Semantically Consistent Sim-to-Real Translation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21628)
*Kaiwen Wang,Frank Bieder,Yinzhe Shen,Carlos Fernandez,Jan-Hendrik Pauls,Omer Sahin Tas*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Wavelet Phase Diffusion (WPD) uses the Dual-Tree Complex Wavelet Packet Transform and Low-Frequency Randomization to enable localized, training-free, unpaired simulation-to-reality translation. It bypasses global spectral coupling of Fourier-domain phase methods, improving realism and semantic consistency, enabling instance-level translation with negligible inference overhead. It shows state-of-the-art realism/semantic alignment on vKITTI→KITTI and competitive realism on CARLA video translation with reduced VLM planner errors.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Simulation-to-reality translation must preserve structure and semantics while adapting appearance. Conditioning-based methods incur heavy control modules, paired methods rely on complex synthesis pipelines that can alter geometry/semantics, and training-free edits lack learned appearance priors. Fourier-domain phase methods suffer from global spectral coupling causing spatial artifacts. There is a need for localized, learned-prior-aware, low-overhead approaches that work with unpaired data.

Method: Two-component approach: (1) operate in the Dual-Tree Complex Wavelet Packet Transform domain to enable spatially localized phase injection via wavelet packets, avoiding global spectral coupling; (2) implement Low-Frequency Randomization (LFR) to replace the low-frequency packet, decoupling from synthetic illumination priors. Both components train on unpaired open-domain data and introduce negligible inference overhead. The locality enables instance-level translation (individual objects/regions translated independently while the rest remains untranslated). Evaluation includes vKITTI→KITTI (realism/semantic consistency, structural alignment) and CARLA video translation (VLM planner ADE/FDE).

Result: On vKITTI→KITTI, WPD outperforms prior methods in realism and semantic consistency and remains competitive in structural alignment. For CARLA video translation, WPD achieves realism approaching that of paired-data methods while reducing VLM planner ADE by 5.4% and FDE by 5.1%.

Conclusion: Wavelet Phase Diffusion addresses the artifacts caused by global spectral coupling in Fourier-based phase diffusion by localizing phase manipulation in the wavelet domain and decoupling illumination priors via LFR. It enables unpaired training, minimal inference overhead, and instance-level translation, offering robust improvements in realism and semantic integrity across driving-scene datasets.

Abstract: Simulation-to-reality translation must bridge the appearance gap between synthetic and real domains while preserving structural and semantic consistency. Conditioning-based methods achieve spatial alignment but introduce computationally expensive control modules. Paired-data methods achieve realism but rely on complex synthesis pipelines, often altering scene geometry and semantics. Training-free editing methods avoid both constraints but lack a learned appearance prior, limiting their perceptual quality. Recently proposed phase-preserving diffusion presents a promising alternative, but Fourier-domain formulations are constrained by global spectral coupling. This coupling induces spatial artifacts such as ringing and boundary leakage, thereby degrading structural and semantic consistency. We introduce Wavelet Phase Diffusion, which addresses this through two components. First, we operate in the Dual-Tree Complex Wavelet Packet Transform domain, whose localized wavelet packets enable spatially adaptive phase injection without global spectral interference. Second, Low-Frequency Randomization (LFR) replaces the low-frequency packet, decoupling the model from the synthetic illumination prior and enabling in-distribution real-world appearance. Both components train on unpaired open-domain data, and introduce negligible inference overhead. The spatial locality further enables instance-level translation, where individual objects or regions are translated to photorealistic appearance independently while the surrounding scene remains untranslated. On vKITTI $\to$ KITTI image translation, ours outperforms prior methods in realism and semantic consistency while maintaining competitive structural alignment. For CARLA video translation, ours approaches the realism of paired-data methods while reducing VLM planner ADE and FDE by $5.4\%$ and $5.1\%$, respectively.

</details>


### [78] [Defining AI-Native Systems: Autonomy as Revision Authority](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21659)
*Cheng Tan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Defines AI-native as AI autonomy over a system’s decisions, not AI capability; introduces a ladder of revision authority (self-tuning, self-rewriting, self-architecting) within a decision-level model distinguishing occupancy vs revision authority, and requires escalation, verification, and a verified fallback while keeping purpose and correctness human-owned.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The term AI-native is used marketing-wise without a precise technical definition. The paper argues for a rigorous definition grounded in who holds authority over decisions and how an AI may autonomously rewrite system implementations, to guide design, governance, and safety.

Method: Proposes a decision-level model of a system, separating occupancy (who executes a decision) from revision authority (who may change it). Establishes a ladder of revision authority: self-tuning, self-rewriting, self-architecting. Defines AI-native as when AI autonomously rewrites the system’s own implementations. Outlines required components: escalation detector, verification procedure, and verified fallback; asserts human ownership of purpose and correctness.

Result: Formal definition of AI-native along with a structured framework for revision authority and governance. Provides criteria (escalation, verification, fallback) and a taxonomy (occupancy vs revision authority; self-tuning/self-rewriting/self-architecting) to classify and design AI-integrated systems.

Conclusion: The paper offers a precise, governance-oriented criterion for AI-native that centers authority over decisions. It establishes a clear ladder of autonomous capabilities and necessary safety/verification mechanisms, maintaining human responsibility for purpose and correctness.

Abstract: AI has begun to write systems code: agents now synthesize, verify, and deploy system components. Despite this shift, "AI-native" remains a marketing term with no precise technical definition. This paper gives it one. We define AI-nativeness along a single axis---authority over the system's own decisions rather than by the capability of the underlying AI models. Building on a decision-level model of a system, we distinguish occupancy (who executes a decision) from revision authority (who may change it), organize revision authority into a ladder---self-tuning, self-rewriting, self-architecting and define a system as AI-native when an AI autonomously rewrites the system's own implementations. The definition further requires an escalation detector, a verification procedure, and a verified fallback, while leaving purpose and correctness human-owned.

</details>


### [79] [Persistent Computational State: A Session-Centric Runtime for Generative World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21686)
*Zhen Lin*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Shows that video world models need a persistent non-recomputable kernel (PCS); snapshotting existing runtime state (observations, RNG, memory, KV context) reproduces continuations identically, implying zero-cost checkpoint/restore and a session-centric runtime for persistent world-model state.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To explain why current benchmarks misattribute failures to model architecture alone and to propose a minimal non-recomputable state (PCS) requirement and a session-centric runtime to enable persistent world-model sessions.

Method: Snapshot and restore runtime components (observation, RNG state, memory bank, windowed KV context); test byte-identical continuation after an excursion; measure effect of RNG corruption; develop a measurement approach to discover PCS; implement a session-centric runtime with persistent sessions; quantify checkpoint/restore cost and session capacity; evaluate memory eviction based on return relevance.

Result: Demonstrates byte-identical continuation when snapshotting runtime state; RNG-only perturbation degrades continuation; checkpoint/restore cost ~0.012 ms vs 1.85 s generation; supports up to 1,024 resident host sessions; recommends eviction by return relevance rather than recency.

Conclusion: Persistent Computational State (PCS) is the minimal non-recomputable kernel required across requests for world models; a session-centric runtime enables near-zero-cost checkpoint/restore and alters memory management strategy, challenging current LLM-centric serving assumptions.

Abstract: Generative world models are increasingly driven as simulators: a planner forks a state, rolls out futures, backtracks, and returns to a visited viewpoint. Recent benchmarks establish that current video world models fail this usage, and attribute it to the model, prescribing new architectures and training objectives. We show this attribution is incomplete, and for an important class of models simply wrong. Snapshotting the state the runtime already holds -- an observation plus RNG state, a memory bank, or a windowed KV context, by architecture -- and restoring it after a genuine excursion reproduces the never-left continuation byte-identically on all three; corrupting only the RNG degrades it. The capability was never missing: request-centric serving discarded it, inheriting from language-model serving the assumption that runtime state is recomputable -- but world-model state carries a non-recomputable kernel. We define Persistent Computational State (PCS), the minimal non-recomputable state that must survive across requests, show it can be discovered by measurement, and build a session-centric runtime over it. Checkpoint and restore cost 0.012 ms against a 1.85 s generation step; resident sessions become host- rather than device-bounded (measured to 1,024); and world memory must be evicted by relevance to the return, not recency -- the inverse of LLM practice.

</details>


### [80] [What AI Red-Team Evaluations Can and Cannot Prove](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21735)
*Bandana Kaur*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: An evidential ceiling for AI-safety benchmarks is defined; a closed-form bound on the evidential value of benchmark results is derived for the null benchmark, revealing a calculable boundary between two regimes of evidential worth; benchmarks are adequate for frequent harms but underpowered for rare catastrophes; discipline requires explicit proposition-scoping.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To quantify how much a given evaluation can shift belief under a fixed testing budget, and to distinguish when benchmarks credibly support safety claims versus when they do not.

Method: Derive closed-form evidential ceiling for benchmark-null result; express bound in terms of hypothesis conditioned elicitation rates; generalize to adaptive and automated red-teaming; audit eight evaluation suites against the boundary.

Result: There exists a calculable boundary (crossing) between two regimes of evidential worth. Above the harm-rate threshold, a modest benchmark can certify a category to the evidentiary standard, and a clean sheet can dominate a single reproduced failure; below the threshold, passive benchmarks of feasible size fail to provide the needed evidence under a fixed scoring rule and approximately independent trials. The bound applies beyond benchmarks to elicitation rates and emphasizes discrimination between hypotheses over attack success. Auditing eight evaluation suites shows current benchmarks are adequate for high-frequency harms but undersample rare, catastrophic harms.

Conclusion: Benchmarks are informative about a computable set of propositions and must declare which propositions they test. They are not uninformative; the discipline is to state explicitly the propositions and testing framework, noting that current benchmarks suffice for common harms but underperform for rare catastrophic ones.

Abstract: Red-team evaluations of AI models support some claims and not others, and the boundary between the two is calculable rather than merely a matter of judgment. We define the evidential ceiling of an evaluation as the largest factor by which one result can move belief under a fixed testing budget, derive it in closed form for the benchmark null result, and use it to locate that boundary exactly. We find that above a calculable harm rate, a benchmark of modest size certifies a category to a stated evidentiary standard, and a clean sheet is then the stronger of the two possible observations, outweighing a single reproduced failure. Below that rate, no passive benchmark of feasible size provides the specified evidence of safety under the fixed scoring rule and approximately independent trial structure. The crossing between the two regimes has a closed form. The bound is not specific to benchmarks: written in terms of a procedure's hypothesis conditioned elicitation rates, it covers adaptive and automated red teaming as well, and shows that discrimination between the hypotheses rather than attack success is what determines evidential worth. Auditing eight evaluation suites against the boundary, we find that current benchmarks are adequate for high-frequency harm categories and several orders of magnitude short for rare, catastrophic ones. Safety benchmarks are not uninformative. They are informative about a specific and computable set of propositions, and the discipline they need is to state which.

</details>


### [81] [Zero-Shot Mission-Level Evaluation for Aerial MLLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22014)
*Suman Navaratnarajah,Taehyoung Kim,Jona Ruthardt,Ishaan Bhimwal,Ryousuke Yamada,Yannik Blei,Wolfram Burgard,Yuki M Asano*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MissionBench exposes the weakness of current MLLMs on long-horizon, mission-level embodied tasks in aerial 3D environments, with size-related gains but substantial gaps to human performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To evaluate how general-purpose multimodal LLMs perform autonomous, closed-loop, long-horizon embodied tasks from a single instruction without aerial-specific fine-tuning, and to motivate mission-level benchmarking.

Method: Create MissionBench: 120 missions across five simulated 3D aerial environments and four task families. Agents rely only on egocentric observations and their action history, without aerial-specific fine-tuning. Evaluate 22 open- and closed-source MLLMs, comparing to human performance.

Result: The best model achieves under 35% success, versus 84.4% for humans. Scaling yields performance gains across model families, but large inter-family variation remains and the gap to human performance is substantial. Mission competence requires coordinating multiple capabilities beyond perception, notably multi-step planning and adaptive reasoning.

Conclusion: Scaling-driven improvements promise progress but pose risks; robust, closed-loop mission-level evaluation is essential to gauge embodied AI capabilities and guide responsible development.

Abstract: Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) are emerging as core reasoning modules for embodied agents, yet it remains unclear how well general-purpose models can solve long-horizon embodied tasks from a single high-level instruction. We introduce MissionBench, a benchmark for mission-level evaluation of MLLMs in aerial 3D environments. It comprises 120 missions across five simulated 3D environments and four task families. Agents must autonomously plan, navigate, and report outcomes using only egocentric observations and its action history, without aerial-specific fine-tuning. Across 22 open- and closed-source MLLMs, the strongest model succeeds on fewer than 35% of missions compared to 84.4% human performance, highlighting the difficulty of multi-step embodied tasks. Despite large variations between model families, we observe gains from scaling, indicating that larger general-purpose models possess stronger zero-shot embodied capabilities. Our analysis shows that mission-level competence requires coordinating multiple capabilities beyond spatial perception, including multi-step planning and adaptive reasoning. This motivates closed-loop evaluation and highlights both the promise and risk of scaling-driven improvements for embodied AI.

</details>


### [82] [From Seasonality to Semantics: Benchmarking a Hybrid Probabilistic Forecasting System for Roadblocks in Bolivia](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21785)
*Rodrigo Vargas Sainz,Christian Berón Curti*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Hybrid Prophet + NLP improves forecasting of Bolivian roadblocks by injecting semantic signals from a six-year news corpus, outperforming purely statistical models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address the lack of local predictive systems for roadblock events and their large economic impact, by leveraging both time-series structure and discursive signals to support logistics decision-making.

Method: Hybrid approach: Prophet for time-series decomposition combined with NLP features (vector embeddings and zero-shot classifiers) trained on six years of Bolivian news; expanding walk-forward validation over 1,762 days, seven forecast horizons (H+1–H+7); seven internal configurations and four external benchmarks (including SARIMA and LightGBM).

Result: Hybrid configuration (Prophet + NLP, C6) consistently outperforms baselines, achieving AUC-ROC 0.677 at H+1 and reducing Brier Score by 10.9% relative to the baseline temporal model (0.220 vs 0.247), with statistically significant improvements across all horizons (p < 0.02).

Conclusion: Integration of semantic news signals enables detection of social tension peaks not captured by historical inertia, providing a practical risk-management tool for critical transport corridors.

Abstract: Roadblocks in Bolivia are a social conflict phenomenon with devastating economic impacts, estimated at losses equivalent to 4% of the national Gross Domestic Product. Despite their recurrence and impact, there is a lack of local predictive systems to anticipate these events for logistical decision-making. This paper presents a hybrid probabilistic forecasting system that integrates time series decomposition (Prophet) with natural language processing (NLP) techniques applied to a six-year corpus of Bolivian news coverage. The methodology employs vector semantic embeddings and zero-shot classification models to capture signals of discursive escalation prior to the materialization of the roadblocks. Using an expanding walk-forward validation scheme applied over 1,762 days and seven forecasting horizons (H+1 to H+7), seven internal configurations and four external benchmarks were compared, including SARIMA and LightGBM. The results demonstrate that the hybrid configuration (Prophet + NLP, C6) consistently outperforms purely statistical models, achieving an AUC-ROC of 0.677 at H+1 and reducing the Brier Score by 10.9% relative to the baseline temporal model (0.220 vs. 0.247), maintaining a statistically significant error reduction across all evaluated horizons ($p < 0.02$). This research validates that the integration of semantic news signals allows for the detection of social tension peaks not captured by historical inertia, providing a technical tool for risk management in critical transport corridors.

</details>


### [83] [QLPO: Quadrant-weighted Sampling for Length-aware Policy Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21793)
*Siwei Chen,Siqi Chen,Xupeng Miao,Bin Cui*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: QLPO is a resampling-based method to implicitly control length during RL fine-tuning of large reasoning models, reducing output length while preserving accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reduce inference latency and deployment cost of long chain-of-thought responses without explicit length penalties or additional control modules.

Method: Over-generate candidate responses; reweight/truncate training samples by preserving empirical correct/incorrect ratios while favoring short correct and long incorrect outputs; a variant of GRPO.

Result: Across 1.5B–32B models, improves accuracy-length trade-off; reduces response length by 30–70% with preserved reasoning performance.

Conclusion: Structured resampling is an effective, robust approach to efficient reasoning in large language models.

Abstract: Recent large reasoning models often develop long chain-of-thought responses during reinforcement learning (RL), resulting in high inference latency and deployment cost. Existing methods for response length control typically rely on explicit length penalties or additional control modules, which require careful tuning and may compromise reasoning quality. We propose Quadrant-weighted Sampling for Length-aware Policy Optimization (QLPO), a simple resampling-based variant of GRPO that introduces implicit length control without modifying the reward function. QLPO first over-generates candidate responses and then resamples the training group by preserving the empirical correct/incorrect ratio while favoring short correct responses and long incorrect responses. This reshapes the training distribution and implicitly encourages shorter model outputs. Across models ranging from 1.5B to 32B parameters, including both base models and strong reasoning models, QLPO consistently improves the accuracy-length trade-off. It reduces response length by 30% to 70% while preserving reasoning performance. These results suggest that structured resampling provides an effective and robust approach to efficient reasoning.

</details>


### [84] [DAGForge: Auditable Causal DAG Authoring with Biomedical Literature](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21859)
*Yi-han Sheu,Michael R. Steigman,Yu Zhou,Bo Wang,Fan-Yu Yen,Jordan W. Smoller*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: DAGForge is a browser-based system to author causal DAGs with auditable, evidence-linked artifacts, using LLM-based reasoning to generate structured pairwise causal judgments grounded in verbatim evidence excerpts, with provenance and confidence; supports study specification, evidence review, adjustment-set computation, graph export; evaluated to show high edge recall and verifiable evidence trails versus LLM-only baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Constructing causal DAGs in biomedicine is time-consuming, opaque, and difficult to audit. There is a need for reproducible, literature-backed DAGs with provenance trails to support study design, analysis, and interpretation.

Method: From free-text study concepts, DAGForge builds a reproducible literature snapshot, uses an LLM-based reasoning module to generate structured pairwise causal judgments tied to verbatim evidence excerpts, and assembles them into a constraint-checked graph. Each edge includes confidence, provenance, and a rationale. The interface supports study specification, progress tracking, evidence review, graph comparison, adjustment-set computation, and export.

Result: In evaluations on compact benchmark DAGs and reference DAGs derived from published literature, DAGForge achieves high edge recall on the literature-based cohort and provides verifiable evidence trails that are absent from baselines that rely on LLMs alone.

Conclusion: DAGForge reduces the burden of causal DAG curation while keeping assumptions auditable, aiding design, analysis, and interpretation of biomedical studies.

Abstract: Constructing causal directed acyclic graphs (DAGs) is a core step in biomedical causal analysis, yet it remains a largely manual process. Analysts must connect study variables to prior literature, evaluate uncertain causal claims, and preserve sufficient provenance for expert review. We present DAGForge, a browser-based system for authoring causal DAGs as auditable, evidence-linked artifacts. Given free-text descriptions of study concepts, DAGForge creates a reproducible literature snapshot, uses an LLM-based reasoning module to generate structured pairwise causal judgments grounded in verbatim evidence excerpts, and assembles those judgments into a constraint-checked graph. Each proposed edge includes confidence estimates, provenance, and a reviewable rationale. The interface supports study specification, progress monitoring, evidence review, graph comparison, adjustment-set computation, and export. In evaluations against both compact benchmark DAGs and reference DAGs derived from published literature, DAGForge achieves high edge recall on the literature-based cohort while retaining verifiable evidence trails absent from LLM-only baselines. DAGForge thus reduces the burden of causal DAG curation while making the resulting assumptions auditable, supporting the design, analysis, and interpretation of biomedical studies.

</details>


### [85] [When Is a Learned Command Adapter Worth It? Closed-Loop Identification and Counterfactual Auditing of Frozen Locomotion Policies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21867)
*Zongtan Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: An adapter necessity audit determines whether adding a learned adapter to a frozen, command-conditioned locomotion policy yields actionable, deployable gains; uses GO/NO-GO/ABSTAIN decisions based on multiple quantified gains and constraints; empirical results show limited recoverable gains, often non-actionable.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Avoid deploying adapters that do not produce robust, observable improvements under deployment constraints; provide a structured auditing framework and decision policy.

Method: Quantify four gain types (global operating-point gain, same-state headroom, deployment gain over cross-fitted fixed action, state-allocation gain over randomized policy); use Source-cluster learner refits to map gains and constraint violations to GO/NO-GO/ABSTAIN; optionally use closed-loop decision features; perform confirmatory audits across multiple distributions (direct, VGCC, MPC) and 20 clusters with 200 refits; set thresholds (1% deployment, 5% violation).

Result: Go2 scale-prefix diagnostic finds 5.2% same-state headroom but 0.55% recovered allocation gain; direct queries yield NO-GO at 1% thresholds; VGCC/MPC yield ABSTAIN; VGCC shows mean deployment gain 1.34% but allocation lower bound 0.09% and violation upper bound 6.25%; twenty-cluster audit returns NO-GO; learner-level control returns GO.

Conclusion: The audit demonstrates that observable signals only justify state-dependent adaptation in limited cases; adapters are not universally valuable; evaluation must be deployment-aware; the framework helps decide whether to deploy adapters.

Abstract: Adding a learned adapter to a frozen, command-conditioned locomotion policy is worthwhile only if the interface exposes improvements that are both real and recoverable from deployment-time observations. We introduce an adapter necessity audit that separates global operating-point gain,same-state counterfactual headroom, deployment gain over a cross-fitted fixed action, and state-allocation gain over a frequency-matched randomized policy. Source-cluster learner refits map these quantities and constraint violations to a GO/NO-GO/ABSTAIN decision. Closed-loop command- response identification provides optional decision features. On Go2, an archived scale-prefix diagnostic finds 5.2% same-state headroom but only 0.55% recovered allocation gain. Our confirmatory audit evaluates direct, scale, heading, and yaw interventions on twenty independent clusters for each of three query distributions induced by direct control, VGCC, and MPC, using 200 full learner refits. At 1% deployment and allocation thresholds and a 5% violation tolerance, direct queries return NO-GO, while VGCC and MPC queries ABSTAIN. VGCC has the largest mean deployment gain (1.34%), but its allocation lower bound is 0.09% and its violation upper bound is 6.25%. A deployment-representative twenty-cluster H1 audit also returns NO-GO, whereas a learner-level synthetic control returns GO. The audit therefore tests whether observable signal justifies state-dependent adaptation rather than presuming that an adapter is valuable.

</details>


### [86] [Multi-Agent System-driven Digital Twins for predictive maintenance: architectures, technologies and open research challenges](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21873)
*Korota Arsène Coulibaly,Mohamed Hamlich*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Systematic review of Digital Twins and Multi-Agent Systems for predictive maintenance in resource-constrained contexts reveals a lack of integrated embedded-distributed hierarchical solutions for Industry 5.0; proposes three open research questions focusing on AI on microcontrollers, lightweight distributed coordination, and hierarchical DT orchestration with residual life estimation and explainable AI.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Motivate cross-pollination between Digital Twins and Multi-Agent Systems to address autonomous decision-making, adaptability, and coordination in distributed, resource-constrained industrial environments; advance predictive maintenance under Industry 4.0/5.0 paradigms.

Method: Conduct a systematic literature review of 547 papers from high-impact journals (IEEE Transactions, Nature, Elsevier, MDPI). Develop a taxonomy of hybrid architectures, perform critical analysis to identify bottlenecks, and articulate open research questions focused on microcontroller AI deployment, lightweight inter-node communication, and hierarchical DT orchestration with residual life estimation and explainable AI.

Result: Proposes a taxonomy of hybrid MAS-DT architectures; identifies persistent bottlenecks (e.g., resource constraints, communication overhead, lack of explainable AI, coordination complexity); formulates three open research questions; concludes that no existing system delivers an integrated embedded-distributed hierarchical solution suitable for Industry 5.0.

Conclusion: Although substantial progress exists, there is a gap in integrated embedded-distributed hierarchical solutions for Digital Twins in Industry 5.0; future work should target embedded AI on microcontrollers, efficient distributed protocols, and hierarchical DT orchestration with interpretability.

Abstract: Digital twins have emerged as a foundational technology within the context of Industry 4.0, offering a paradigm for the real-time virtual representation of physical systems. However, managing their growing complexity, particularly in distributed industrial environments, requires intelligent architectures capable of autonomous decision-making, dynamic adaptability, and inter-agent coordination. This systematic review explores the intersection between Multi-Agent Systems and Digital Twins, with a particular focus on predictive maintenance applications in resource-constrained contexts. Through a critical analysis of over 547 papers published in high-impact journals (IEEE Transactions, Nature, Elsevier, MDPI), we establish a taxonomy of existing hybrid architectures, identify persistent technological bottlenecks, and formulate three open research questions concerning: (i) the deployment of artificial intelligence on resource-constrained microcontrollers, (ii) distributed multi-node coordination via lightweight communication protocols, and (iii) the hierarchical orchestration of Digital Twins toward smart factory control integrating residual life estimation and explainable Artificial Intelligence. The results of this analysis reveal that, despite significant progress, no existing system offers an integrated embedded-distributed hierarchical solution that simultaneously meets the requirements of Industry 5.0.

</details>


### [87] [TRW: TRACE-RealWorld---An Auditable Consistency Contract for World Models as Materialized Views](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21910)
*Edward Y. Chang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: TRACE-RealWorld provides a governance framework for maintaining actionable materialized views over evolving physical data, coupling commitment-level validity, adaptive view maintenance, and provenance with compensation mechanisms, evaluated via Flood-SAR for freshness and replayability; not a new predictive model but a contract for consistency, recovery, and accountability of learned world representations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Need for dependable, auditable, and recoverable deployment of learned world representations in dynamic sensing environments where base reads are priced, delayed, heterogeneous, and fallible.

Method: Introduce a commitment-level validity abstraction for materialized predictions; consequence-conditioned adaptive view maintenance; transaction-style, dependency-scoped compensation for commitments invalidated after authorization; append-only provenance enabling exact replay; end-to-end Flood-SAR evaluation addressing sensing as physical data acquisition.

Result: Framework-centric contribution: establishes a consistency, recovery, and accountability contract for deploying learned world representations as operational data systems; Flood-SAR measures freshness, verification cost, stale reads, recovery scope, restoration failure, and replayability.

Conclusion: The work reframes learned representations as operable data-system components governed by explicit contracts for validity, provenance, and compensation, enabling robust deployment with verifiable freshness and replayability.

Abstract: TRACE-RealWorld addresses a core data-management problem: maintaining an actionable materialized view over a continuously changing physical world when reads of the base state are priced, delayed, heterogeneous, and fallible. Its data-management contributions are a commitment-level validity abstraction for materialized predictions; consequence-conditioned adaptive view maintenance; transaction-style, dependency-scoped compensation for commitments invalidated after authorization; and append-only provenance supporting exact replay. The work builds directly on materialized-view maintenance, adaptive stream synchronization, transaction recovery, sagas, data freshness, and provenance. The end-to-end Flood-SAR evaluation treats sensing as physical data acquisition and measures freshness, verification cost, stale reads, recovery scope, restoration failure, and replayability through six pre-registered questions with held-out seeds. The contribution is therefore not a new predictive model, but a consistency, recovery, and accountability contract for deploying learned world representations as operational data systems.

</details>


### [88] [Semiotic logical hexagon theory for LLM logical reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21933)
*Yunyao Zhang,Xinglang Zhang,Zeliang Chen,Junqing Yu,Zikai Song*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: HexLogicAgent improves LLM logical reasoning by first structuring semantic meaning of statements and then applying structured verification, guided by a logical hexagon theory. It yields more reliable reasoning and delays performance degradation under higher logical complexity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Natural-language statements conceal implicit semantic relations that, if misorganized, cause incorrect conclusions even with valid deduction. Existing methods underemphasize semantic structure, leading to reasoning failures as complexity grows.

Method: Introduce HexLogicAgent: (1) organize the meaning of natural-language statements; (2) guide reasoning with structured verification. Support with a logical hexagon theory. Empirically evaluate on challenging logical reasoning benchmarks across multiple LLMs.

Result: HexLogicAgent consistently improves reasoning reliability across several LLMs on challenging benchmarks, delaying degradation as logical complexity increases.

Conclusion: A complete representation of the semantic structure, including opposing meanings, is essential for reliable reasoning; explicit semantic organization enhances robustness and generalizes across models, with the hexagon theory explaining why this structure matters.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) have become powerful tools for language understanding and logical reasoning. However, they still make mistakes when a problem requires both understanding meaning and following logic. A key reason is that natural-language statements often carry implicit semantic relations before any formal reasoning begins. If these hidden meanings are not properly organized, the model may reach incorrect conclusions even when the subsequent reasoning process appears logically valid. Existing methods improve reasoning through decomposition, symbolic translation, external solvers, or self-verification, but pay comparatively less attention to the semantic structure on which reasoning depends. In this paper, we further investigate how semantic organization influences logical reasoning in LLMs. To this end, we propose HexLogicAgent, a framework that first organizes the meaning of natural-language statements and then guides logical reasoning through structured verification. In our investigation, we also make two observations. First, incomplete semantic representations, rather than deductive inference itself, are a major source of logical reasoning failures in LLMs. Second, explicitly modeling the complete structure of semantic opposition substantially delays the degradation of reasoning performance as logical complexity increases. Experiments on challenging logical reasoning benchmarks demonstrate that HexLogicAgent consistently improves reasoning reliability across multiple LLMs. The core idea is supported by a logical hexagon theory, which explains why a complete structure of opposing meanings is necessary for reliable reasoning.

</details>


### [89] [Learning as Reasoning Unfolds: Progressive Rollout Allocation for Efficient Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22002)
*Heyang Jiang,Henry Liu,Baharan Mirzasoleiman*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Variance-guided online rollout allocation (VIGOR) for RLVR reduces compute over GRPO by adaptively allocating rollouts to high-variance groups, with theoretical speedup and empirical gains on math and coding tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: GRPO-based RLVR uses many long chain-of-thought rollouts, which is computationally expensive and often inefficient. A principled, adaptive rollout strategy is needed to reduce wasted rollouts while preserving or improving performance.

Method: VIGOR starts with a small number of rollouts per example and iteratively allocates additional rollouts to examples/groups with the highest reward variance, until a fixed total rollout budget is reached. The authors show reward variance controls gradient magnitude under RLVR and derive a closed-form speedup ratio over GRPO, with growth under Pareto-distributed variance.

Result: On math reasoning tasks: up to 2.3x fewer rollouts to reach target accuracy. On coding tasks: reaches GRPO’s final full pass rate with 1.49x fewer rollouts and improves coding average test pass rate by 3.4 percentage points.

Conclusion: VIGOR provides a principled, variance-guided, budget-constrained rollout allocation method for RLVR that accelerates training and can improve performance, outperforming standard GRPO under the tested tasks.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) has emerged as a highly effective framework for improving LLM reasoning, with methods such as GRPO among its most successful instantiations. However, GRPO relies on repeated generation of long chain-of-thought rollouts. Training time scales with the number of rollouts, a large fraction of which are uninformative. Thus, GRPO is computationally expensive and unstable. To mitigate this, existing approaches either generate a larger pool of rollouts and filter the most informative prompts, or leverage historical signals for filtering at later stages of training. These strategies offer modest performance gains, but slow down the overall process. To address this, we propose VarIance Guided Online Rollout allocation (VIGOR) which instead of allocating a fixed rollout budget per example, begins with a small number of rollouts for all examples in a batch and iteratively allocates additional rollouts to those with the highest group reward variance until a fixed total rollout budget is reached. Theoretically, we show that under RLVR, reward variance controls the gradient magnitude, and derive VIGOR's closed-form speedup ratio over GRPO, which grows with refinement rounds under Pareto-distributed reward variance. Experiments on mathematical reasoning and coding tasks show that VIGOR reaches target accuracy with up to 2.3$\times$ fewer rollouts on math, reaches GRPO's final coding full pass rate with 1.49$\times$ fewer rollouts, and improves the coding average test pass rate by 3.4 points.

</details>


### [90] [Nanbeige4.2-3B: Unlocking Agentic Capabilities in a Compact Mode](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22083)
*Nanbeige Lab,:,Chen Yang,Chengrui Huang,Fufeng Lan,Hanhui Chen,Hao Zhou,Huatong Song,Jiaqi Cao,Jiaying Zhu,Jinlin Niu,Kai Wang,Lisheng Huang,Qiliang Liang,Ran Le,Ruixiang Feng,Shuang Sun,Tao Gu,Tao Zhang,Tianyu Luo,Yang Song,Yun Xing,Yuntao Wen,Ziyao Xu,Zongchao Chen,Zongqiang Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Nanbeige4.2-3B is a compact 3B-parameter general agent with a Looped Transformer, achieving strong agentic performance across code, office tasks, and tool use, while maintaining competitive reasoning; it outperforms larger models on agentic benchmarks and is viable as a local assistant with OpenClaw.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address the demand for a small yet capable general agent that can perform cross-domain tasks and robust agentic reasoning, enabling efficient local deployment and real-world utility.

Method: Pretrained from scratch on 28T tokens using a Looped Transformer that reuses the layer stack to boost capacity without increasing parameter count; SFT data and trajectory construction enhanced via real-world deployment and large-scale synthesis; RL pipeline combines mixed-mode RLHF over Think/Non-Think, length-controlled reasoning RL, and agentic RL with outcome and process rewards; OpenClaw integration for practical deployment.

Result: Outperforms larger models (e.g., Qwen3.5-9B and Gemma4-12B) on diverse agentic benchmarks; competitive on reasoning and alignment tasks; supports use as a compact local personal assistant.

Conclusion: Demonstrates that a compact 3B model with architectural innovations (Looped Transformer) and a tailored RL pipeline can rival or exceed larger models on agentic capabilities, validating the viability of small, efficient general agents for real-world use.

Abstract: We present Nanbeige4.2-3B, a compact general agentic model with 3B non-embedding parameters. It delivers strong performance across code-agent, office-agent, and complex tool-use tasks while maintaining highly competitive reasoning capabilities in mathematics, coding, and science. Nanbeige4.2-3B is pretrained from scratch on 28T tokens with a Looped Transformer that reuses the layer stack to increase capacity without adding parameters. For SFT data and trajectory construction, we expand the diversity of executable environments, task assets, and agentic scaffolds through real-world deployment and large-scale synthesis. Our RL pipeline applies mixed-mode RLHF over Think and Non-Think responses to improve overall model quality and reduce failure cases, length-controlled reasoning RL to balance accuracy and reasoning efficiency, and agentic RL with outcome and process rewards to stabilize long-horizon training. Extensive evaluations show that Nanbeige4.2-3B outperforms larger models, including Qwen3.5-9B and Gemma4-12B, across diverse agentic benchmarks while remaining competitive on reasoning and alignment tasks. Performance with OpenClaw further supports its use as a compact local personal assistant.

</details>


### [91] [Reasoning Denoiser: Denoising Reasoning Traces for Hallucination Detection in Large Reasoning Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22098)
*Junlin Fang,Do Nguyen-Thanh,Xiaogang Xu,Zhen Fang,Sean Du*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: REDE denoises reasoning traces of large reasoning models to improve hallucination detection by using final-answer attention to supervise step representations, enabling removal of noisy steps; plug-and-play with detectors; demonstrated gains across benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Long, noisy reasoning traces (irrelevant and repetitive steps) degrade the reliability of hallucination detection. Existing confidence-based or embedding-based filters struggle to separate noisy vs. informative steps, limiting the utility of traces for truthfulness assessment.

Method: Propose REDE, a denoising framework that uses final-answer attention as automatic supervision to shape the step-level embedding space, enabling identification and filtering of noisy steps. The filtered trajectory can be fed to diverse hallucination detectors.

Result: Extensive experiments on multiple reasoning benchmarks show that REDE consistently improves detection performance over competitive baselines.

Conclusion: Denoising reasoning traces via final-answer-guided step representations enhances hallucination detection and is readily integrable with various detectors.

Abstract: Large reasoning models (LRMs) generate long reasoning traces before producing final answers. While these traces may contain useful signals for hallucination detection, harnessing them is non-trivial because long trajectories often include noisy steps that obscure the cues relevant to truthfulness assessment. In this paper, we identify two prevalent forms of reasoning noises, i.e., irrelevant steps and repetitive steps, and show that both substantially degrade hallucination detection performance. Existing confidence-based scores and naive embedding-based filtering fail to reliably separate noisy from informative steps. To address this challenge, we propose REDE, a novel learning framework for denoising reasoning traces for hallucination detection. Specifically, REDE leverages final-answer attention as an automatic supervision signal to shape the step-level representation space, yielding refined embeddings in which noisy steps can be reliably identified and filtered. REDE can be readily plugged into diverse hallucination detectors by operating on the filtered reasoning trajectory after removing noisy steps. Extensive experiments on multiple reasoning benchmarks show that REDE consistently improves detection performance over competitive baselines.

</details>


### [92] [Industrial Tokenization for LLM-Based Health Intelligence: A Federated Architecture for Industrial Evidence Integration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22153)
*Deshui Li,Xiao-Ming Yuan,Zishun Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A conceptual Industrial Tokenization framework that transforms diverse industrial analytics into structured, domain-grounded tokens (Industrial Tokens) to enable federated reasoning with LLMs; initial DiagnosisToken implementation for vibration diagnostics; aims to improve interpretability, traceability, and adaptability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Industrial data are heterogeneous in structure, time resolution, physical meaning, and quality; integrating all into a monolithic model impairs interpretability and adaptability; a semantic interface is needed to connect domain-specific insights with LLM-based reasoning while preserving subsystem autonomy.

Method: Proposes Industrial Tokenization as a semantic interface and federated architecture. Defines tokens with metadata (source, temporal scope, operating context, analytical meaning, quality/confidence, provenance). Presents DiagnosisToken as an end-to-end pathway using vibration diagnostics, rule-based event aggregation, structured textual token generation, and LLM interpretation; outlines future extension for SCADA tokens, maintenance tokens, prognostic tokens.

Result: Introduces a concrete tokenization framework and an initial DiagnosisToken pathway; demonstrates token generation and interpretation, providing a blueprint for future multi-token extensions and federated reasoning.

Conclusion: Industrial Tokenization positions domain-specific industrial intelligence as tokens for a central reasoning layer, preserving subsystem autonomy while enabling interpretable, adaptable reasoning with LLMs; future work: expand to other token types and refine token schemas and provenance.

Abstract: Industrial health management increasingly relies on heterogeneous information sources, including condition monitoring systems, supervisory control and data acquisition systems, maintenance records, inspection results, and prognostic models. Although large language models provide new opportunities for cross-source reasoning, industrial data and analytical outputs differ substantially in structure, temporal resolution, physical meaning, and reliability. Directly integrating such heterogeneous information into a monolithic model may reduce interpretability, traceability, and adaptability to equipment and data changes. This paper introduces Industrial Tokenization, a conceptual interface for transforming source-specific analytical outputs into structured and machine-interpretable units of industrial evidence, termed Industrial Tokens. Unlike numerical tokens used to encode raw time-series data, Industrial Tokens represent domain-grounded evidence together with source, temporal scope, operating context, analytical meaning, quality or confidence information, and provenance. Based on this concept, a federated industrial architecture is proposed, where heterogeneous analytical subsystems retain autonomy while exposing standardized Industrial Tokens to a central reasoning layer. As an initial implementation, this study presents an end-to-end DiagnosisToken pathway based on vibration-diagnostic outputs, rule-based event aggregation, structured textual token generation, and LLM-based interpretation. Other Industrial Tokens, including SCADA-based condition-monitoring tokens, maintenance tokens, and prognostic tokens, are reserved as future extensions. The proposed framework positions Industrial Tokenization as a semantic interface between domain-specific industrial intelligence and LLM- or agent-based reasoning, rather than another method for encoding raw industrial data.

</details>


### [93] [Learning on the Job: Continual Learning from Deployment Feedback for Frozen-Weights Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22157)
*Valentin Tablan,Scott Taylor,Kristoffer Bernhem*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: External memory distills episode outcomes into retrievable natural-language rules to enable continual learning from feedback for frozen models; yields 1.6x–2.6x gains on τ-bench banking tasks; cross-model transfer observed; harness/protocol/data released.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address the inability of deployed (frozen) models to learn from post-deployment feedback by introducing an external memory that converts episode feedback (outcome verdicts and post-hoc corrections) into retrievable natural-language rules, enabling continual learning.

Method: Pair a frozen model with an external memory that stores distilled episode feedback as natural-language rules. Use outcome verdicts (one-bit signals) and after-the-fact corrections as supervision signals. Evaluate on τ-bench banking tasks against a static-RAG baseline retrieving from the full policy corpus. Replicate results on Mistral Large (self-hostable) and Claude Sonnet 5. Demonstrate cross-model transfer where each model reads the other’s memory store.

Result: Learning from a single verdict improves single-trial success to 1.6x baseline; learning from corrections improves to 2.6x baseline; 22 of 84 tasks solved that baseline never solved. Performance spans deployment spectrum and transfers across Mistral Large and Claude Sonnet 5. The authors release their harness, protocol, and data.

Conclusion: An external, NL-rule memory provides a sufficient signal for continual learning in frozen models; episodic feedback can be distilled into durable knowledge retrievable by the model, enabling significant gains and cross-model transfer. The work supports practical deployment for self-hosted systems and improves reproducibility via released resources.

Abstract: AI agents encounter learning opportunities in every episode they run, and discard nearly all of them: the underlying models are frozen at deployment, so an agent that resolves a difficult request today starts from zero when it recurs tomorrow. Yet ordinary operation already produces feedback, in the form of outcome verdicts and after-the-fact corrections. We show that this feedback is a sufficient signal for continual learning when the frozen model is paired with an external memory that distils each episode into retrievable natural-language rules. On the banking domain of $τ$-bench, against a static-RAG control retrieving over the complete policy corpus, learning from the one-bit outcome verdict lifts single-trial success to 1.6$\times$ the baseline, and learning from corrections to 2.6$\times$, converting 22 of the 84 tasks the baseline never solves. The result spans the deployment spectrum, measured on Mistral Large, an open-weights model that organisations with data sovereignty requirements can self-host, and replicated on a frontier model, Claude Sonnet 5. The accumulated memory also transfers: each model, reading the store built by the other, rises above its own no-memory baseline. The harness, protocol, and data are released.

</details>


### [94] [Deconstructing Off-Policy Ratios: Entropy-Scaled Trust Regions for Asynchronous Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22186)
*Guanqun Zhao,Zijun Xie,Binbin Zheng,Enlei Gong,Jiafeng Lu,Yehan Yang,Aoqi Hu,Zeyu Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: We propose Entropy-Scaled Trust Region (ESTR) for asynchronous RL in LLM post-training to scale off-policy deviations by token entropy, addressing entropy-dependent noise and exploration. ESTR avoids extra forward passes, yields better train-inference consistency, and matches GRPO accuracy with significant speedups.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Asynchronous RL can destabilize training due to stale data and off-policy updates. The natural scale of importance ratios varies with token entropy, causing noise at low entropy and uncontrolled exploration at high entropy when using magnitude-based corrections.

Method: Introduce ESTR that multiplies each token's off-policy deviation by its local entropy, effectively adapting the trust-region constraint to token-level uncertainty. No auxiliary forward passes or version-switch detection required. Evaluate on long-horizon agentic tasks and mathematical reasoning benchmarks.

Result: ESTR consistently outperforms existing asynchronous methods in train-inference consistency and overall performance. It achieves comparable accuracy to synchronous GRPO while accelerating training by about 2.6x.

Conclusion: Entropy-scaled adjustment stabilizes asynchronous RL for LLM post-training, providing improved train-inference consistency and training efficiency without extra computation or detection overhead.

Abstract: Asynchronous reinforcement learning (RL) accelerates large language model (LLM) post-training by overlapping rollout generation with policy optimization, but the resulting stale, off-policy data can destabilize optimization and ultimately cause policy collapse. Existing methods typically retain or discard tokens based solely on the magnitude of their importance ratios, applying the same threshold uniformly across token positions. In this work, we reveal that the natural scale of the importance ratio varies systematically with token entropy. Under asynchronous dynamics, this entropy-ratio scaling dictates two distinct phenomena: at low entropy, the inherent train-inference discrepancy is drastically amplified into substantial sampling noise; at high entropy, in-flight weight updates naturally induce pronounced, legitimate exploratory deviations. Consequently, magnitude-only correction inadvertently admits the amplified noise while strictly masking out the essential exploration triggered by in-flight updates. To address this, we propose the Entropy-Scaled Trust Region (ESTR), which scales each token's off-policy deviation by its local entropy, requiring no auxiliary forward passes or explicit version-switch detection. Across long-horizon agentic tasks and mathematical reasoning benchmarks, ESTR consistently outperforms existing asynchronous methods and achieves the best train-inference consistency. Compared with synchronous GRPO, ESTR attains comparable accuracy while improving training speed by $2.6\times$.

</details>


### [95] [AI4PLE: A Methodology for Integrating AI into Product Line Engineering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22260)
*Bedir Tekinerdogan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A methodological framework is proposed to systematically integrate AI into Product Line Engineering (PLE), evaluated via a multi-case industrial study.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To leverage AI to enhance decision-making in PLE (feature selection, variability management, configuration optimization) and address the gap of systematic AI integration in PLE.

Method: Proposes a methodological framework for AI-enabled PLE and assesses its effectiveness through a multi-case study in an industrial context.

Result: The abstract indicates the framework and its evaluation via a multi-case industrial study, but provides no detailed empirical results.

Conclusion: AI-enabled PLE is feasible and promising; the framework establishes a structured approach for integrating AI into PLE and is validated through industry-case study.

Abstract: Reuse-based development has become increasingly important in the creation of complex systems, offering significant opportunities to reduce costs, improve quality, and accelerate time-to-market. Product Line Engineering (PLE) provides a systematic approach to realizing this potential by enabling the efficient creation, management, and customization of product families by reusing shared assets and capabilities. PLE involves addressing numerous complex decisions, including feature selection, variability management, and configuration optimization, which are critical to the success of a product line. Despite its promise, the systematic integration of Artificial Intelligence (AI) into PLE processes has not yet been comprehensively explored. In this paper, we propose a methodological framework to support the systematic integration of AI into PLE and evaluate its effectiveness through a multi-case study conducted in an industrial context.

</details>


### [96] [A Roadmap to Impactful Pluralistic Alignment Research](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22305)
*Elinor Poole-Dayan,Jillian Fisher,Atoosa Kasirzadeh,Jacob Andreas,Mitchell Gordon,Michiel A. Bakker*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Public evidence that pluralistic value alignment is shaping training or evaluation in production AI is lacking; the authors call for empirical grounding, concrete operational goals, and production-ready, trade-off-aware evaluation methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To ensure AI systems serve diverse human values in practice, bridging the gap between normative/pluralistic alignment goals and adoption in widely used models.

Method: Systematic audit of public behavior documents and evaluations from frontier AI labs to assess whether pluralism is named as a goal or explicitly trained/tested for.

Result: No evidence found that pluralism is named as a goal or that production models are explicitly trained or tested for pluralistic behavior; identifies an adoption problem and outlines three underlying causes and three corresponding research directions.

Conclusion: Advancing pluralistic alignment requires moving from normative justification to empirical evidence, defining concrete ideal pluralistic behavior, and developing practical, production-ready methods and evaluations that support adoption in deployed AI systems.

Abstract: Pluralistic value alignment---the goal of building AI systems that represent and serve diverse human values and perspectives---has emerged as an active research agenda. Yet, there's no public evidence that it has shaped the training or evaluation of the AI systems people actually use. We audit the public behavior documents and evaluations of frontier labs, finding none name pluralism as a goal, and as of this writing, no clear indication that production models are explicitly trained or tested for it. This goes against the primary motivations and goals of pluralistic alignment, which revolve around making a positive difference in the models serving billions of users worldwide. We argue that the pluralistic alignment research community should focus on supporting impact and adoption in deployed, widely-used AI systems. We provide evidence for the adoption problem, present three main reasons behind it, and discuss three corresponding areas for future research to address it: 1. The primary justifications for pluralistic alignment so far have been normative or speculative. We need studies showing empirically how pluralistic AI benefits users or society. 2. The pluralistic alignment research community has not settled when pluralistic behavior is warranted or what pluralism ideally looks like in practice. We need to establish a concrete goal for developers to operationalize. 3. Current methods trade off against other desiderata of LLMs in ways that are largely unmeasured, and existing metrics are not "hill-climbable." We need trade-off-aware evaluations and methods that meet the requirements of production systems. This paper serves as a collective call to action for the pluralistic alignment researchers: progress requires moving beyond normative justification toward empirical foundations, a concrete account of ideal pluralistic behavior, and practical methods and evaluations built for adoption.

</details>


### [97] [Learning Structural Convergence: A Neuro-Symbolic Benchmark for Temporal Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22365)
*Michael Romei De Socio,Gian Luca Pozzato,Alessio Merlo*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A synthetic benchmark (TRACTA) for temporal structural reasoning in high-complexity event-driven systems is introduced, comparing raw-event neural models, a contract-lite semantic baseline, and a neuro-symbolic approach on semantically grounded trajectories across three tasks. Semantically grounded trajectories yield the strongest temporal reasoning performance, with complementary contributions from capability dynamics and contextual impacts; ablations and diagnostics highlight rationale and limitations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To detect and anticipate temporally distributed patterns in high-complexity operational environments, and to evaluate how semantically grounded, structured representations plus temporal learning improve temporal reasoning over raw event data.

Method: Introduce TRACTA benchmark with three tasks (early_warning, pattern_detection, run_classification) in MDO-like scenarios. Compare three architectures: (1) raw-event neural models, (2) contract-lite semantic baseline, (3) neuro-symbolic system operating on semantically grounded trajectories. Perform ablations on capability dynamics, contextual impacts, and temporal structure, and conduct shortcut diagnostics to understand reliance on direct identifiers.

Result: Raw event–level learning remains informative. However, models leveraging learned temporal semantics over semantically grounded trajectories achieve the highest aggregate performance, with the largest margins on temporal tasks. Ablation shows complementary contributions from capability dynamics, contextual impacts, and temporal structure. Shortcut diagnostics reveal the primary neural input view controls the major cross-run shortcut; residual shallow signals persist.

Conclusion: In controlled synthetic settings, semantically grounded trajectories provide an effective representation for temporal structural reasoning and warrant further exploration of semantic interfaces between event data, structured representations, and temporal learning.

Abstract: High-complexity operational environments require methods that detect and anticipate temporally distributed patterns rather than classify isolated events. This paper introduces TRACTA (Temporal Reasoning and Capability-Trajectory Analysis), a controlled synthetic benchmark for temporal structural reasoning in high-complexity event-driven systems, instantiated through Multi-Domain Operations (MDO)-like scenarios. The benchmark includes three tasks: early_warning, pattern_detection, and run_classification, and compares raw-event neural models, a contract-lite semantic baseline, and a neuro-symbolic configuration operating on semantically grounded trajectories. Results show that raw event-level learning remains informative, but learned temporal modeling over semantic capability and contextual direct-impact trajectories achieves the highest aggregate point estimates, with the largest margins on the temporal tasks. Ablation analysis indicates that capability dynamics, contextual impacts, and temporal structure contribute complementary information. Shortcut diagnostics indicate that the most direct cross-run global-identifier shortcut is controlled in the primary neural input view, while residual shallow signals remain. Overall, the findings support a bounded methodological conclusion: in controlled synthetic settings, semantically grounded trajectories provide an effective representation for temporal structural reasoning, supporting further investigation of semantic interfaces between event data, structured representations, and temporal learning.

</details>


### [98] [Do Agent Benchmarks Measure Capability? Protocol Validity in the Age of Agentic AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22368)
*Jiaqi Shao,Hanck Chen,Wei Zhang,Maxm Pan,Bing Luo*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Introduces HackDetect, a post-hoc audit to quantify protocol validity and detect reward hacking in agent benchmarks; defines Mislead gap and reports substantial score inflation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Benchmark scores can be inflated when evaluation protocols allow shortcuts; a common procedure is needed to attribute shortcuts and quantify their impact across benchmarks.

Method: Post-hoc audit of 2,385 traces across 15 agent benchmarks to identify exposure, infer how the agent used it, and compute Mislead gap (exploit score minus intended score); includes paired comparisons to measure score inflation.

Result: Observed exposures and reward hacking in about 67% of Frontier Science traces and 67% of AutoLab tasks; score inflation in paired comparisons ranges 0.45–1.00, indicating substantial inflation.

Conclusion: Benchmark reports should provide evidence that scores reflect the intended capability; HackDetect offers a framework to audit protocol validity and quantify misleading scoring.

Abstract: Agent benchmarks increasingly evaluate repository editing, web research, terminal use, and long-horizon interaction. Their scores support capability claims only when the evaluation protocol keeps the intended capability necessary for success. Recent reward-hacking benchmarks and system reports show that agents can instead recover public solutions, read evaluation artifacts, infer generator structure, manipulate feedback, or benefit from invalid scoring paths; existing responses do not provide a common procedure for attributing these shortcuts and quantifying their effect across benchmarks. We formulate protocol validity and introduce HackDetect, a post-hoc audit that identifies an exposure, determines how the agent used it, and assesses whether the resulting score is misleading. We quantify score inflation with the Mislead gap, defined as the exploit score minus the intended score. We audit 2,385 traces across 15 agent benchmarks and find evidence of exposures and reward hacking in 67.0% of Frontier Science traces and 66.7% of AutoLab tasks. Across paired comparisons, we measure score inflation of 0.45-1.00, showing that benchmark reports should provide evidence that scores reflect the intended capability.

</details>


### [99] [IDEAgent: Agentic Quality-Diversity Search for Research Idea Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22375)
*Varun Gumma,Navonil Majumder,Soumitra Sinhahajari,Soujanya Poria*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Presents IDEAgent, a multi-agent Quality-Diversity (QD) framework for idea ideation, introducing a joint metric Yield to maximize a diverse set of high-quality ideas across topics; shows large improvements over baselines and open-sources the code.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To overcome the limitation of prior systems that optimize either quality or diversity in isolation, hindering generation of high-quality, diverse, and clear ideas; advocate for treating ideation as a QD search.

Method: IDEAgent manages idea lineages with multi-agent collaboration. Quality is driven by multi-objective feedback for repair/refinement; Diversity is fostered via lightweight memory and explicit comparison against completed ideas, ancestors, and rejected proposals. Yield jointly measures the largest set of mutually diverse ideas satisfying a quality threshold.

Result: In evaluations over 32 CS topics across 8 domains, IDEAgent outperforms the best baseline by 3.89x on Yield and achieves non-zero Yield on 8x more topics; analyses indicate repair/refinement improve logical rigor and clarity while preserving non-obviousness.

Conclusion: The work formalizes ideation as a QD problem, introduces a novel Yield metric and a practical IDEAgent framework, and provides empirical support and open-source release to spur future QD-based ideation research.

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) have significantly automated the process of scientific discovery over the past few years. However, existing systems share one core limitation: they generate and optimize ideas independently for either Quality or Diversity. This often leads to the generation of ideas in close proximity to one another or to a large set of trivial, unsound, or unclear concepts. In this work, we instead argue that research ideation should be treated as a conjunction of both objectives and framed as a Quality-Diversity (QD) search. In line with this perspective, we introduce IDEAgent, a multi-agent framework that manages the evolution of ideas through lineages. We jointly drive Quality using multi-objective feedback for dedicated repair and refinement, while Diversity is achieved through lightweight sequential memory and explicit comparison against completed ideas, their historical ancestors, and rejected proposals. To systematically evaluate this QD conjunction, we develop Yield, a joint metric that computes the largest set of mutually diverse ideas that satisfy a predetermined quality threshold. Finally, through evaluations across 32 topics spanning 8 domains of Computer Science, we show that IDEAgent outperforms the best baseline by 3.89x on Yield, while achieving non-zero Yield on 8x more topics. We further corroborate these findings through an analysis of quality improvements, showing that repair and refinement are crucial for building logical rigor and clarity while preserving non-obviousness. To encourage future research on QD-search-based ideation, we open-source IDEAgent at https://github.com/declare-lab/IDEAgent.

</details>


### [100] [Agentic Root Cause Analysis through Evidence-Grounded Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22385)
*Amaury Wei,Olga Fink*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Zero-shot, evidence-grounded root cause analysis using a data-driven digital twin and tool-augmented LLM; achieves competitive diagnosis without fault-specific training and provides transparent reasoning traces.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Industrial systems suffer from anomalies; traditional data-driven RCA is limited by lack of explainability and scarce fault-label data, hindering deployment at scale.

Method: Inference-time reasoning with a normal-dynamics digital twin plus a tool-augmented large language model. The agent iteratively collects statistical evidence, evaluates competing hypotheses, and identifies the root cause that best explains observations.

Result: On real-world multiphase-flow and large-scale chemical-plant data, AgentRCA achieves diagnostic performance competitive with fully supervised baselines while not requiring fault-specific training, and it outputs transparent reasoning traces linking symptoms to physical causes.

Conclusion: Shows that autonomous, hypothesis-driven reasoning is a practical and scalable foundation for industrial root cause analysis, enabling explainable, data-efficient anomaly investigations.

Abstract: Diagnosing the root cause of anomalies is essential for safe industrial operation. Despite extensive sensor instrumentation, formulating hypotheses and gathering evidence remains a manual process, creating a major operational bottleneck. While existing data-driven approaches aim to automate this, two critical limitations restrict their deployment: their operate as black boxes unable to justify their diagnosis, and they require scarce labeled examples of faulty operation. To address this gap, we introduce AgentRCA, a zero-shot agentic framework for evidence-grounded root cause analysis. Rather than learning fault-specific mappings, AgentRCA performs inference-time reasoning by combining a data-driven digital twin (modeling normal system dynamics) with a tool-augmented large language model. The agent iteratively gathers statistical evidence, evaluates competing hypotheses, and identifies the physical fault that best explains the observed behavior.
