Evaluated on a real-world multiphase-flow facility and a large-scale chemical plant, AgentRCA achieves diagnostic performance competitive with fully supervised baselines without relying on fault-specific training. Crucially, it produces transparent reasoning traces that explicitly link observed symptoms to their underlying physical causes. These results establish autonomous hypothesis-driven reasoning as a practical foundation for scalable industrial root cause analysis.

</details>


### [101] [SceneActBench: Can Agents Act on the 3D Scenes They See?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22393)
*Yifei Zhao,Xiangxin Zhou,Wenhao Yang,Jiaqi Tang,Pu Jian,Huanjin Yao,Jiarui Yao,Haowei Lin,Chunchao Guo,Zhuo Chen,Wenkai Lyu,Jianzhu Ma,Xueqian Wang,Wenxi Zhu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A new SceneActBench benchmark evaluates visually conditioned agent actions on complete multi-object 3D scenes across five tasks, producing 520 task cases from 210 source instances. Across 11 proprietary VLM configurations, overall scores range 38.6–50.2; no model is consistently strong, revealing task-specific weaknesses and failure modes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing 3D benchmarks mostly evaluate textual responses or single-object operations and do not assess agents acting in full multi-object 3D environments. A unified agent–environment loop with geometry-grounded evaluation is needed to fairly compare VLM agents performing actions in 3D scenes.

Method: The authors construct SceneActBench with five tasks derived from 210 source instances, yielding 520 task cases (including paired inputs). Each task runs through a fixed agent loop. Inputs include PNG images or sampled video frames and, when applicable, supplied 3D assets. Outputs are evaluated against hidden ground truth using task-specific geometric metrics. The benchmark evaluates 11 proprietary VLM configurations.

Result: Scores across configurations range from 38.6 to 50.2, with none achieving consistent performance across all tasks. The authors also analyze where and how failures manifest across tasks.

Conclusion: SceneActBench exposes substantial cross-task variability and generalization gaps in current VLM agents operating on 3D scenes. The fixed-loop, geometry-aware evaluation framework enables fair comparison and yields actionable insights into prevalent failure modes to guide future improvements.

Abstract: Vision-language model (VLM) agents increasingly use tools to act on 3D scenes rather than only describe them. Existing 3D benchmarks score textual responses or single-object operations, leaving agent action on complete multi-object 3D scenes under evaluated. We present SceneActBench, a benchmark for visually conditioned action across five 3D tasks under a unified agent-environment loop. Given PNG images or sampled video frames and, where applicable, supplied 3D assets, an agent acts on a 3D environment. We evaluate each final output against hidden ground truth with task-specific geometric metrics. SceneActBench comprises five tasks built from 210 source instances, yielding 520 task cases including paired input conditions. Every task runs through one fixed agent loop to keep the comparison fair. Across eleven proprietary VLM configurations, Overall scores span 38.6-50.2, and none performs consistently well across tasks. We further analyse where and how failures manifest.

</details>


### [102] [Dynamic Capability Scoping for Enterprise AI Agents: A Synthetic Dataset and Three-Source Permission Architecture](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22445)
*Halil Burak Noyan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Proposes a dynamic, multi-source capability-scoping architecture for enterprise AI agents to enforce least-privilege, validated with a synthetic 600-prompt dataset and high inter-annotator agreement, showing large reductions in policy ceiling violations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Static, over-privileged credential sets expand attack surface and risk misalignment of LLM agents. The work argues for prevention-first dynamic scoping (capabilities exist only when needed) and a three-source architecture to harden enterprise AI agents with observe-only and enforce modes.

Method: Three-source architecture: (1) role-based ceilings; (2) task-context classifier; (3) policy-derived combination prohibitions, forming a layered proactive defense. A two-pass dataset pipeline separates prompt generation from permission labeling. Evaluation uses a 60-record/688 decisions human-reviewed sample (Cohen’s κ = 0.917 pre-review, 0.967 post-review) and reports a 93% reduction in ceiling violations (46 to 3). The dataset, environment spec, and generation pipeline are released.

Result: Synthetic dataset of 600 enterprise task prompts with a 15-permission taxonomy mapping to credentials/guardrails. High inter-annotator agreement and substantial reduction in ceiling violations demonstrate viability of dynamic scoping. Deployment modes include enforce and observe-only with the latter yielding behavioral signals for misalignment research.

Conclusion: Dynamic permission scoping can meaningfully reduce privilege leakage and misalignment risk; the multi-source architecture supports proactive defense and policy refinement via synthetic prompt generation. Release of dataset/env/pipeline enables broader evaluation of dynamic scoping mechanisms.

Abstract: Enterprise AI agents are typically granted static credential sets at configuration time, holding every tool the role might need for every task they perform. This persistent over-privilege expands the attack surface. We argue that capability scoping must follow a dynamic least-privilege principle and be treated as a prevention mechanism before a detection one. A credential that does not exist in an agent's context cannot be misused regardless of the agent's reasoning or evasion sophistication. We outline a three-source architecture instantiating this principle: role-based ceilings, a task-context classifier, and policy-derived combination prohibitions creating a layered proactive defense against LLM agent misalignment and misuse cases. The architecture supports both enforcing and observe-only deployment; the latter records agent permission requests inconsistent with task context, producing a behavioral signal usable in misalignment research.
  As a first step toward evaluating this architecture, we contribute a synthetic dataset of 600 enterprise task prompts grounded in a multi-department company policy, labeled with minimum required permissions across a 15-permission tool-based taxonomy that maps directly to deployable credentials or enforceable guardrails. The dataset is constructed via a two-pass pipeline that separates prompt generation from permission labeling to avoid circularity, and is validated against a 60-record/688 decisions human-reviewed sample (Cohen's $κ= 0.917$ pre-review and $κ= 0.967$ post-review). Iterating between dataset and policy reduced ceiling violations from 46 to 3, a 93% reduction. This shows that synthetic prompt generation can drive policy refinement when the two are developed together. The dataset, environment specification, and generation pipeline are released to support evaluation of dynamic scoping mechanisms.

</details>


### [103] [TRACE-ROUTER: Task-Consistent and Adaptive Online Routing for Agentic AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22465)
*Ritik Raj,Souvik Kundu,Sarbartha Banerjee,Dheemanth Joshi,Ishita Vohra,Tushar Krishna*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: TRACE-Router is a task-level routing framework that assigns each task to a model using a contextual bandit, pins subsequent LLM calls to that model, and optimizes for accuracy-latency using delayed task rewards, achieving Pareto improvements over baselines on tau2-Bench and Terminal-Bench.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In agentic, long-horizon workflows, per-call routers misalign with the unit of supervision (the task) and cannot attribute feedback to routing decisions; a task-level routing mechanism is needed to improve overall outcome quality.

Method: Propose TRACE-Router, which selects a backend at task admission via a contextual bandit, fixes that choice for the task, and updates its policy from the task’s terminal reward that combines accuracy and latency; leverages delayed feedback to learn workload-adaptive routing without explicit task complexity estimation.

Result: TRACE-Router yields consistently better accuracy-latency trades, producing non-dominated Pareto frontier points; on tau2-Bench it surpasses latency-matched model interpolation by 7–8 accuracy points; on Terminal-Bench it achieves 7.1 accuracy points more than the strongest single model with 36% lower latency.

Conclusion: Task-level routing with delayed feedback effectively aligns routing decisions with the unit of supervision in agentic workloads, enabling superior efficiency and accuracy trade-offs across multiple benchmarks.

Abstract: Routing to select large language models (LLMs) with different cost-quality trade-offs has become a fundamental deployment feature of enterprise AI. Existing routers, primarily make independent routing decisions for each LLM call. However, agentic applications execute as long-horizon workflows whose quality is determined only by a delayed, task-level outcome. This mismatch prevents per-call routers from correctly attributing feedback to individual routing decisions. Towards mitigating this, we present TRACE-Router, a task-level routing framework that aligns routing with the unit of supervision. TRACE-Router assigns each task to a model once at admission using a contextual bandit, pins all subsequent LLM calls to the selected backend, and updates its policy using the task's terminal reward, jointly accounting for accuracy and latency. By leveraging delayed task feedback, TRACE-Router learns routing policies that adapt to the workload while avoiding explicit task-complexity estimation. Across three agentic benchmarks, TRACE-Router consistently improves the accuracy-latency trade-off, achieving non-dominated Pareto frontier points. On tau2-Bench, it outperforms latency-matched interpolation between individual models by 7-8 accuracy points, while on Terminal-Bench it achieves 7.1 higher accuracy points than the strongest single model baseline with 36% lower latency.

</details>


### [104] [The Regression Tax: Decomposing Why Skills Help and Hurt LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22520)
*Darshan Tank,Baran Nama*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Procedural skills in LLM agents must be evaluated by decomposing net effects into gains and regressions rather than aggregate improvements.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand how introducing procedural skills affects performance beyond overall success rates and to uncover error modes caused by skills.

Method: Compare agents with and without skills across roughly 6,000 runs on two office-automation benchmarks and three model harness stacks; classify outcomes into regressions and residual failures; analyze regression causes (osmosis, grounding displacement, verification displacement); examine failure persists via traces; adjust evaluation to emphasize grounding and verification.

Result: Regressions are substantial and often explain why top performers look good (less regression rather than more gains); three regression causes identified; many persistent failures share the same causes; correcting evaluation artifacts reveals recoverable regressions with better grounding/verification; importance shifts from skill choice to grounding/verification.

Conclusion: Decompose procedural-skill impact into gains and regressions; avoid regression-prone modes; emphasize grounding and verification to improve reliability; evaluation framework should extend beyond aggregate task success.

Abstract: Adding procedural skills to an LLM agent is typically evaluated by average improvement in task success. However, this metric hides an important cost: skills can also make agents worse. We measure both sides by comparing agents with and without skills across nearly 6,000 runs spanning two office automation benchmarks and three model harness stacks. This allows us to distinguish two outcomes. A regression is a task solved without skills but failed after skills are added. A residual failure is a task that fails both with and without skills. We find that regressions are substantial enough that the best performing skills outperform others primarily by regressing less, not by gaining more. We identify three causes of regression: (i) skill description osmosis, a skill changes an agent's behavior simply by being present in context, even when it is never invoked; (ii) grounding displacement, a skill's prescribed procedure overrides how the agent interprets its inputs; and (iii) verification displacement, where the procedure suppresses checks the agent would otherwise perform on its outputs. Analysing persistent failures reveals the same underlying pattern. Existing skills overemphasize procedural guidance the stage least often responsible for failure while under supporting grounding and verification, the dominant sources of remaining errors. After correcting evaluation artifacts and studying traces, we find many regressions and persistent failures recoverable through better grounding and verification. Procedural skills should be evaluated by decomposing their net effect into gains and regressions, not by aggregate improvement alone. We identify three regression modes skills should avoid, and find that reliability depends more on grounding and verification than on procedural skill choice.

</details>


### [105] [Explainable Reinforcement Learning for assisting Air Traffic Controllers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22525)
*Anduel Mehmeti,Gabriella Gigante,Salvatore Venticinque*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Examines explainability for reinforcement learning in air traffic control, using a simplified environment and saliency maps to reveal input feature influence on routing decisions; a preliminary step toward trustworthy AI in safety-critical domains.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address trust in AI within high-stakes settings (healthcare, autonomous driving, aviation) by enhancing explainability, particularly for deep RL in safety-critical ATC tasks.

Method: Train a reinforcement learning agent in a simplified ATC environment to select alternative flight routes avoiding no-fly zones; apply saliency maps to identify input features that most influence decisions.

Result: Saliency maps provide initial insights into which input features drive the agent’s decisions, demonstrating feasibility of a saliency-based explainability approach for RL in ATC as a first step.

Conclusion: Saliency-based explanations are a preliminary, promising step toward trustworthy RL in ATC; further work is needed to develop more robust, domain-specific explanations and evaluate their effectiveness in real-world safety-critical contexts.

Abstract: To effectively integrate AI into high-stakes, critical environments such as healthcare, autonomous driving, and aviation--and to advance toward higher levels of automation and seamless human-AI collaboration--building trust in AI-driven solutions is essential. Trust, in turn, is closely linked to the explainability of AI systems. The rapid advancements in AI across various domains have underscored the challenges of establishing trust, raising increasing interest in AI explainability even more when applied to deep learning. In this context, the present work aims to explore the application of explainability techniques to Reinforcement Learning (RL) algorithms, specifically within the safety-critical domain of Air Traffic Control (ATC). Using a simplified ATC environment as an initial testbed, an intelligent agent is trained with a reinforcement learning algorithm to make decisions on alternative flight routes that avoid no-fly zones. As a preliminary explainability approach, a saliency map is employed, providing insights into the input features that most significantly influence the agent's decision-making process.

</details>


<div id='cs.RO'></div>

# cs.RO [[Back]](#toc)

### [106] [Learning Diverse Humanoid Tasks via Synthetic Video Scenarios without Real World Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21648)
*Yun-Hao Tsai,Cong-Thanh Vu,Yen-Chen Liu*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Text-to-motion synthetic demonstrations via Generative AI to enable diverse task demonstrations for humanoid robots, reducing data collection needs and improving robustness.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address high cost and limited diversity of real-demonstration data in Learning-from-Demonstrations; capture of multiple human execution styles for a single task.

Method: Propose a framework that uses Generative AI to convert textual prompts into realistic and diverse sequences of human body movements; generate synthetic demonstrations across four simulation scenarios for training.

Result: Robot completes tasks successfully and exhibits strong adaptability to motion variations across the simulated scenarios.

Conclusion: Synthetic demonstrations produced from textual prompts can augment LfD, enabling scalable, diverse training and improved robustness with reduced dependence on real human demonstrations.

Abstract: The human-like morphology of humanoid robots grants them exceptional potential for agile and versatile motor capabilities, but it also introduces significant challenges in acquiring complex skills. Traditional Learning-from-Demonstrations methods are often constrained by the high cost of collecting real-world data, the difficulty of capturing motion-specific behaviors, and the limited diversity of demonstrations across individuals. Moreover, even for the same task, humans may execute the motion in multiple distinct ways. In this paper, we propose a new framework that leverages the power of Generative AI to convert textual prompts into realistic and diverse sequences of human body movements, enabling the robot to observe multiple variations of how a single task can be performed. These synthetic demonstrations are then used as a training resource, allowing the robot to learn a broad range of task-execution styles without requiring direct human intervention. We evaluate the proposed method across four simulation scenarios. Experimental results show that the robot not only completes the tasks successfully but also demonstrates strong adaptability to complex variations in motion.

</details>


### [107] [Progress Reward Modeling for Robotic Learning: A Comprehensive Survey](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21655)
*Jianshu Zhang,Keliang Wu,Haoran Lu,Anbang Liu,Ce Zhang,Weijie Yin,Chengxuan Qian,Xiyuan Yang,Zhenyu Pan,Guo Ye,Han Liu*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A survey that unifies progress reward modeling for robotic learning by dissecting interface, construction, and data/evaluation, and outlining limitations and future directions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Progress rewards provide intermediate feedback in dynamic, high‑dimensional tasks; lack of a shared framework makes cross-study comparison and validation difficult.

Method: Organizes the field into three connected steps: (1) interface of a progress model, (2) internal methods to construct the progress signal, (3) data and benchmarks for supervision and evaluation.

Result: Offers a consolidated taxonomy and synthesis of existing approaches, clarifying assumptions and measurement criteria; highlights standardization needs and practical guidelines for evaluation.

Conclusion: Identifies main limitations and proposes future research directions toward unified frameworks, better benchmarks, and robust progress signals.

Abstract: Robotic learning takes place in dynamic environments with large behavior spaces. A terminal success signal only tells the robot whether the task is completed. It does not explain whether the current behavior is making progress, remaining unchanged, or undoing earlier progress. For this reason, recent studies have increasingly explored progress rewards that provide feedback during task execution. However, the current literature lacks a shared framework. Existing methods use different observations, goal specifications, output signals, supervision sources, and evaluation protocols. This makes it difficult to compare them and understand what their results actually validate. In this survey, we provide a unified view of progress reward modeling for robotic learning. We organize the field in three connected steps. We first study the interface of a progress model. This defines the problem from the outside by asking what information the model receives and what form of progress signal it produces. We then move inside the model and study the methods used to construct this signal. This reveals the different assumptions and mechanisms behind progress estimation and reward generation. Finally, we examine the data and benchmarks that support these methods. This shows how progress supervision is obtained and what different evaluations actually measure. Together, these three perspectives connect what a progress model is, how it is built, and how its quality is validated. We further summarize the main limitations of current approaches and discuss future research directions.

</details>


### [108] [GRACE: Gradient-Free Robot Action Generation via Combined Diffusion-MPPI Posterior Mean Estimation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21661)
*Leesai Park,Jiho HOng,Sanghyun Kim*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Gradient-free guidance of diffusion policies using MPPI to handle non-differentiable deployment constraints; improves success and safety on real robot.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Deployment-time constraints (binary collisions, joint limits, black-box costs) are often non-differentiable, which limits traditional gradient-guided diffusion policy methods.

Method: At each reverse step of diffusion, construct a cost-conditioned guidance posterior and estimate its mean via a single MPPI update centered at the diffusion's reverse mean; yields a gradient-free adjustment; for differentiable costs, reduces to conventional gradient guidance via first-order, matched-covariance approximation.

Result: Simulation shows GRACE outperforms diffusion-based and sampling-based baselines; real 7-DoF manipulator demonstrates obstacle avoidance where the unguided prior collides; code and videos provided.

Conclusion: GRACE enables gradient-free integration of arbitrary costs into diffusion policies, broadening applicability to safety-critical constraints and black-box costs; practical deployment with non-differentiable constraints is feasible.

Abstract: Diffusion policies generate multimodal robot action sequences from demonstrations, but steering them toward deployment-time constraints typically relies on differentiable guidance costs. This excludes many practical safety constraints, such as binary collision checks, joint limits, and black-box rollout costs that are nondifferentiable. We propose Gradient-free Robot Action generation via Combined diffusion-MPPI posterior mean Estimation (GRACE), which guides a pretrained diffusion policy with Model Predictive Path Integral (MPPI) control using only forward cost evaluations. Building on the common score-ascent structure of diffusion and MPPI, GRACE constructs a cost-conditioned guidance posterior at each reverse step and estimates its mean with a single MPPI update centered at the diffusion reverse mean. For differentiable costs, GRACE recovers conventional gradient guidance under a first-order, matched-covariance approximation. GRACE attains higher success rates than diffusion-based and sampling-based baselines in simulation. On a real 7-DoF manipulator, GRACE avoids a deployment-time obstacle that the unguided prior collides with in every trial. Code and experiment videos are available at https://anonymous.4open.science/w/grace-70BB/.

</details>


### [109] [Ordered Action Tokens for Visuomotor Policy Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21670)
*Chaoqi Liu,Yue Zhao,Haonan Chen,Xiaoshen Han,Jiawei Gao,Ehsan Adeli,Yilun Du*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Introduces Ordered Action Tokenization (OAT): a transformer-based action tokenizer that achieves high compression, total decodability, and an ordered token space by using registers, finite scalar quantization, and ordering-inducing training; enabling anytime inference. Validates on autoregressive and token-co-training policies across 60 tasks, five benchmarks, and real-world settings with strong policy performance and flexible inference cost.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing action tokenization either relies on coarse analytical discretization leading to long sequences or on opaque latent tokenizers lacking structure. There is a need for compact, decodable, and ordered action tokens that play well with downstream visuomotor policies and allow flexible inference.

Method: Propose OAT, a learned tokenizer that discretizes action chunks into an ordered token sequence. It uses a transformer augmented with registers, finite scalar quantization, and ordering-inducing training to structure tokens. Each token prefix is trained to decode to a valid action chunk, placing coarse information in early tokens and refining detail in later tokens. This yields an anytime tradeoff between inference cost and fidelity. Evaluated in two policy paradigms: autoregressive token generation and token co-training with a flow-based action expert; tested across multiple backbones, tasks, and benchmarks.

Result: OAT delivers strong policy performance across two main uses, three policy backbones, and 60+ tasks across five simulation benchmarks and real-world settings, while offering significantly greater inference-time flexibility due to its ordered, progressively decodable token sequence.

Conclusion: OAT fulfills the three desiderata (high compression, total decodability, ordered token space), enabling anytime control fidelity and better compatibility with downstream policies. The approach demonstrates broad applicability to autoregressive and token-co-training paradigms and suggests potential for further efficiency gains and integration with vision-language flow-based policies.

Abstract: Action tokenization maps continuous robot action chunks to discrete tokens and has become an important interface for modern visuomotor policies. Existing approaches either rely on analytical discretization methods that produce prohibitively long token sequences or learned latent tokenizers that lack structure, limiting their compatibility with downstream policies. In this work, we identify three desiderata for action tokenization - high compression, total decodability, and an ordered token space - and introduce Ordered Action Tokenization (OAT), a learned action tokenizer that satisfies all three. OAT discretizes action chunks into an ordered sequence of tokens using a transformer with registers, finite scalar quantization, and ordering-inducing training mechanisms. By training each token prefix to decode into a valid action chunk, OAT places coarse control information in early tokens and uses later tokens to refine residual detail, yielding an anytime tradeoff between inference cost and action fidelity. We validate OAT in two prevailing uses of action tokens: autoregressive policies that generate tokens for control, and token co-training policies that use token losses to shape the vision-language model context consumed by a flow-based action expert. Across three policy backbones and more than 60 tasks spanning five simulation benchmarks and real-world settings, OAT consistently delivers strong policy performance while offering significantly greater flexibility at inference time.

</details>


### [110] [Addressing the Orchestration Gap in Generalist Robots via Physical Agency](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21725)
*Liane Galanti,Dhruv Shah,Tri Dao*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A modular agent architecture with a high-level orchestrator (Pigey) that plans, decomposes goals into subgoals, commands low-level policies, tracks outcomes, and recovers from failures, enabling effective real-world manipulation by reusing existing VLA policies without extra data collection.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To overcome reliance on monolithic end-to-end pretraining for general-purpose robots by decomposing reasoning, planning, and control into specialized components governed by a language-conditioned policy and a high-level agent manager.

Method: Introduce Physical Agency orchestrator (Pigey) that can control existing vision-language-action policies and parametrized skills in a closed-loop system. It performs high-level planning, goal decomposition, low-level command issuance, outcome verification from observations, and failure recovery. Demonstrated across simulations and real robots, without additional data collection or post-training.

Result: On LIBERO-PRO, Pigey achieves state-of-the-art improvements >4x (12.8% → 53.3%) with no task-specific fine-tuning. On a real robot, the approach boosts a frozen policy from near-zero to over 90% on reasoning-limited tasks.

Conclusion: The paper introduces the orchestration gap—the performance boost gained when frozen motor skills are integrated inside an agentic loop through a high-level orchestrator. This modular, language-conditioned control framework enables robust, data-efficient real-world manipulation without extra data collection.

Abstract: General-purpose robots need to reason about their actions, combining perception, world knowledge, planning, success detection, recovery, and low-level control. Today's state-of-the-art models attempt to combine all these capabilities into the learned policy via large-scale pre-training. Instead, we show that these capabilities can be decomposed into a general language-conditioned policy/control agent and a high-level agent manager/orchestrator. Rather than training policies to reason via pre-training, we build a closed-loop physical agent orchestrator that can do high-level planning, decompose the goal into achievable subgoals, command low-level motor commands, track and verify the outcome from low-level observations, and recover from failures. Our Physical Agency orchestrator (Pigey) can control existing vision-language-action (VLA) policies as well as parametrized skills to solve complex reasoning tasks in the real world, without any additional data collection or post-training. We evaluate Pigey extensively across simulation benchmarks and challenging real-world robotic manipulation tasks, and demonstrate significant performance improvements over existing generalist policies. On LIBERO-PRO, Pigey advances the state-of-the-art by over 4x (12.8% -> 53.3%) with no task-specific fine-tuning. On a real robot, Pigey lifts the frozen policy from near-zero to over 90% on reasoning-limited tasks. We call the difference between what frozen motor skills achieve alone and inside the agentic loop the orchestration gap.

</details>


### [111] [StARS: Socially Appropriate Robot Actions via a Recommender System-Driven Approach](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21802)
*Erencem Ozbey,Fethiye Irmak Dogan,Jin Huang,Hatice Gunes*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: StARS is a model-agnostic, recommender-inspired framework that personalizes social-action appropriateness in human-robot interaction by combining collaborative filtering with learnable scene representations; evaluated on MannersDB+ and SocNav1; outperforms baselines and is robust to sparse feedback; code released.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Social judgments of robot actions are subjective and vary across individuals, requiring models that capture inter-subject variability to select actions aligned with user norms in HRI.

Method: StARS treats annotators as users, contexts/scenes as items, and appropriateness scores over candidate robot actions as targets. It is model-agnostic and can be integrated with various scene encoders/backbones, enabling personalized action scoring without redesigning the underlying model. It combines collaborative filtering with learnable scene representations to generate user-specific appropriateness scores.

Result: Across two datasets (MannersDB+ and SocNav1) and multiple backbones, StARS consistently improves performance and agreement with annotators, demonstrating robustness under sparse preference feedback.

Conclusion: StARS provides a flexible, plug-in approach to personalize HRI action selection by modeling user-specific preferences; it enables personalized, norm-aligned action recommendations without requiring changes to the core model, and its code is publicly available.

Abstract: Social appropriateness in human-robot interaction (HRI) is not universal: different people can judge the same robot action differently in the same situation. To capture this inter-subject variability, we reformulate socially appropriate action generation as a preference modelling problem inspired by recommender systems, treating annotators as users, contexts/scenes as items, and appropriateness scores over a set of candidate robot actions as targets. We propose StARS, a novel model-agnostic framework that integrates collaborative filtering with learnable scene representations to generate user-specific appropriateness scores over candidate robot actions. StARS is model-agnostic: it can be integrated with various scene encoders and backbones, enabling personalisation without redesigning the underlying model. We evaluate StARS on two socially aware robotics datasets, MannersDB+ and SocNav1, and analyse robustness under sparse preference feedback. Across datasets and backbones, StARS consistently improves performance and agreement with annotators, supporting personalised action selection aligned with user norms. Our code is publicly available at https://github.com/Cambridge-AFAR/StARS.git.

</details>


### [112] [Action-Conditioned World Model for Goal Plane Probe Guidance in Robotic Ultrasound](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21918)
*Siqi Fan,Mingcong Chen,Ran Liu,Zixuan Yang,Xiaoyu Fu,Xiaoqing Gao,Yunhui Liu,Hongbin Liu*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A two-stage model-based framework for goal-directed robotic ultrasound navigation using a latent diffusion world model plus a goal-conditioned transformer; achieves 70% carotid and 65% thyroid success in real-world closed-loop scans.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Autonomous ultrasound requires extensive probe-motion data for training, and explicit simulators are hard to build due to tissue deformation, contact-dependent appearance, and view-dependent artifacts.

Method: Stage 1: a latent conditional diffusion model predicts future ultrasound observations from recent context frames, probe motions, and temporal offset. Stage 2: a goal-conditioned temporal transformer predicts an ordered sequence of probe motions; it is fine-tuned using rewards from the frozen world model.

Result: The world model preserves action-dependent anatomical structure on target-directed scans; real-world closed-loop tests report 70.0% success for carotid guidance and 65.0% for thyroid guidance; dataset is self-collected.

Conclusion: Demonstrates the viability of learned ultrasound dynamics to train goal-directed robotic probe navigation and reduce the burden of collecting demonstrations; indicates potential for broader application in ultrasound-guided robotic tasks.

Abstract: We present an action-conditioned world model framework for goal plane probe guidance in robotic ultrasound, with a focus on neck ultrasound scanning. Autonomous ultrasound tasks often require large numbers of probe-motion trajectories for training, but collecting high-quality demonstrations is labor-intensive and explicit simulators are difficult to build because ultrasound appearance depends on contact, tissue deformation, and view-dependent acoustic artifacts. We address this problem with a two-stage model-based learning pipeline. First, a latent conditional diffusion world model predicts future ultrasound observations from recent context frames, probe motions and temporal offset. Second, a goal-conditioned temporal transformer predicts ordered probe motions and is fine-tuned using rewards from the frozen world model. Experiments on the self-collected dataset show that the world model preserves action-dependent anatomical structure on target-directed scans. In real-world closed loop experiments, the framework achieves success rates of 70.0\% for carotid guidance and 65.0\% for thyroid guidance. These results demonstrate the potential of learned ultrasound dynamics for training goal-directed robotic probe navigation.

</details>


### [113] [Adaptive Undulatory Locomotion of Snake-like Robots in Dynamic Viscous Environments via Deep Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21960)
*Tsuyoshi Kimoto,Akio Yamano,Kohei Honda,Takashi Iwasa*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: DRL with teacher-student privileged information distillation enables adaptive, non-sinusoidal gaits for snake-like robots in variable-viscosity fluids, surpassing classical controllers.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address performance limits of predefined, sinusoidal-based locomotion in dynamically changing fluid environments and lack of direct fluid-property sensing; enable robust, efficient propulsion through learning.

Method: Formulate as partially observable MDP; use asymmetric actor-critic DRL; train a teacher policy with privileged simulator information; distill into a student policy relying on proprioception; evaluate across a wide viscosity range (10^-7 to 10^-2 m^2/s) in simulation.

Result: The DRL agent discovers non-sinusoidal adaptive gaits, achieving higher propulsion velocity and transport efficiency; these gaits outperform conventional sinusoidal/kinematic controls and demonstrate effective learning of implicit environment inference via privileged information distillation.

Conclusion: Privileged information distillation for asymmetric actor-critic DRL provides an effective route to implicit environment inference, enabling robust locomotion under unpredictable fluid dynamics beyond classical model constraints.

Abstract: This paper demonstrates how deep reinforcement learning (DRL) enables adaptive locomotion of snake-like robots in dynamically changing viscous environments, overcoming the inherent performance limitations of classical predefined control methods. The lack of direct onboard sensors for fluid properties necessitates formulating this task as a partially observable Markov decision process. By employing an asymmetric actor-critic framework, a teacher policy trained using privileged information available only in the physics simulator distills its knowledge into a student policy that relies solely on proprioceptive sensor information. Simulation results across a wide range of dynamic viscosity changes ($10^{-7}$ to $10^{-2} m^2/s$) reveal that the DRL agent autonomously acquires non-sinusoidal adaptive gaits. These gaits improve propulsion velocity and transport efficiency, breaking the inherent limits of conventional sinusoidal and kinematic control. The findings establish that implicit environment inference via privileged information distillation is an effective approach to bypass the constraints of classical models under unpredictable fluid dynamics.

</details>


### [114] [ACME: A Multi-Cultural, Multi-Embodiment Social-Navigation Dataset](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21964)
*Shashank Rao Marpally,Allan Wang,Atharva Ghotavadekar,Renato Alexandre Ribeiro,Nhat Le,Pilar Bachiller-Burgos,Pranav Goyal,Subham Agrawal,Yasuhiro Nitta,Howard Ziyu Han,Daeun Song,Masaki Kuribayashi,Kohei Uehara,Xiyue Wang,Yangzhe Kong,Duc M. Nguyen,Amirreza Payandeh,Gerardo Pérez-González,Alejandro Torrejón-Harto,Jeeho Ahn,Tisha Jain,Andrew Stratton,Elvin Yang,Jorge de Heuvel,Nico Ostermann-Myrau,Sai Anudeep Sajja,Mithilya Raj,Daisuke Sato,Gaston Rouquette,Nikolas Martelaro,Maki Sugimoto,Hironobu Takagi,Chieko Asakawa,Maren Bennewitz,Aaron Steinfeld,Xuesu Xiao,Christoforos Mavrogiannis,Harold Soh*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: ACME is a cross-cultural, multi-embodiment dataset for social navigation, spanning 8 sites across 5 countries with 7 robot embodiments. It provides 29.35 hours of onboard robot data and 43.5 hours of overhead pedestrian tracking, plus 3D/2D scene features, odometry, interaction data, and annotated pedestrian trajectories, including robot speech. The dataset aims to enable learning of navigation policies and trajectory prediction and claims to cover more challenging scenarios and a broader pedestrian behavior distribution than prior datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing social navigation datasets lack sufficient cultural, geographic, and interaction-factor diversity, limiting generalization of learned navigation policies. ACME addresses this gap by capturing cross-cultural and cross-embodiment interactions.

Method: Large-scale data collection across 8 sites in 5 countries using 7 robot embodiments. Multimodal data collection includes onboard and overhead sensors, explicit robot-crowd interaction via robot speech, and annotations of pedestrian trajectories. Data is provided in both human-readable and raw binary formats, with 3D/2D scene features and odometry.

Result: ACME yields a larger, more diverse dataset with more challenging scenarios and a broader distribution of pedestrian behaviors than prior datasets, facilitating robust learning and evaluation of social navigation policies and trajectory prediction models.

Conclusion: ACME advances social navigation research by enabling cross-cultural and cross-embodiment studies, improving generalization of navigation policies, and enabling more accurate pedestrian trajectory prediction in shared-space environments.

Abstract: Understanding how robots and humans move in shared spaces is essential for designing effective social robot navigation policies and predicting human behavior. However, existing datasets often lack the diversity needed to capture differences in culture, geography, and human-robot interaction-factors that strongly shape appropriate social behavior. To address this gap, we introduce ACME: A Cross-cultural, Multi-Embodiment dataset for social navigation. A large-scale data collection effort across 8 sites in 5 countries, using 7 robot embodiments, ACME is a large and diverse multi-modal dataset aimed at advancing social navigation research, providing 29.35 hours of onboard robot data and 43.5 hours of overhead pedestrian tracking data. Unlike prior datasets, it focuses on capturing goal-driven social navigation behavior in complex social scenarios with explicit robot-crowd interaction through robot speech. To facilitate learning navigation policies and predicting pedestrian trajectories, ACME provides 3D and 2D scene features, odometry, interaction information, and human-annotated pedestrian trajectory labels. We make ACME easy to use by providing both human-readable data for each sensor modality as well as raw binary data. Our qualitative and quantitative analyses show that our dataset captures more challenging scenarios and a broader distribution of pedestrian behavior than previous datasets.

</details>


### [115] [Mag4D-SLAM Dataset: A Repeated-Traversal Multi-Modal 4D Geomagnetic Dataset for Localization and Mapping](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21986)
*Bibhutibhusan Nayak,Hyoseok Ju,Giseop Kim*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: First large-scale outdoor geomagnetic SLAM dataset (Mag4D-SLAM) with 14 synchronized sequences (>18 km) and ground-truth SE(3) poses, enabling study of yaw drift, magnetic loop closure, and cross-session place recognition using LiDAR, camera, IMU, magnetometer, and GNSS.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing geomagnetic SLAM datasets are indoor, small-scale, and lack synchronized multimodal sensing and high-precision ground truth, hindering systematic outdoor SLAM research with geomagnetics.

Method: Data collection along campus trajectories under day and night conditions, forward and reverse passes, with repeated traversals to assess magnetic repeatability, heading estimation, and location-discriminative magnetic signatures; comprehensive ground truth SE(3) trajectories provided for SLAM evaluation.

Result: Introduces Mag4D-SLAM as the first large-scale outdoor geomagnetic SLAM dataset; enables yaw drift mitigation, magnetic loop closure, and long-term localization research; highlights the complementary role of geomagnetic sensing to visual/LiDAR modalities and robustness under GNSS denial.

Conclusion: Dataset opens new research directions for integrating geomagnetic sensing into SLAM, particularly for yaw drift reduction, loop closure, and multi-session localization, and prompts exploration of magnetic signatures for place recognition and fallback cues during illumination changes and GNSS-denied operation.

Abstract: Geomagnetic sensing offers an infrastructure-free, absolute orientation reference that is robust to GNSS denial and visual degradation, yet no large-scale outdoor robotics dataset supports its systematic study in SLAM. Existing magnetic datasets are confined to small-scale indoor environments and lack the synchronized multi-modal sensing, repeated-traversal structure, and high-precision 6-DoF ground truth required for geomagnetic SLAM research. We present Mag4D-SLAM, the first large-scale outdoor geomagnetic SLAM dataset. It comprises 14 sequences totaling over 18 km of synchronized LiDAR, camera, IMU, tri-axis magnetometer, and GNSS measurements with SE(3) ground-truth poses, collected along structured campus trajectories under paired day/night conditions in both forward and reverse directions. Through repeated-traversal experiments, we analyze three core properties: magnetic field repeatability across different recording sessions (daytime and nighttime), drift-free global heading estimation, and location-discriminative magnetic signatures for cross-session place recognition. Mag4D-SLAM is designed to support research on yaw drift mitigation, magnetic loop closure, and long-term localization and to open new research questions on how geomagnetic sensing can complement visual and LiDAR modalities or provide a fallback cue under illumination changes, structural repetition, and GNSS-denied long-term operation.

</details>


### [116] [Embodying Multi-Hand Manipulation Policies by Searching the Assignment and Null Spaces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22020)
*Yorai Shaoul,Jiaoyang Li,Maxim Likhachev*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A complete, search-based grounding framework that maps multi-hand policy trajectories to physical multi-arm robots by jointly solving assignment (which hand/arm handles which trajectory) and null-space motion (using Jacobian redundancy) to avoid collisions and respect kinematics, built on Conflict-Based Search; yielding a safe, feasible, scalable planner.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Learned manipulation policies predict abstract end-effector motions but lack guaranteed feasibility and safety when executed on multi-arm robots. There is a need for a principled, complete method to assign trajectories to arms and realize corresponding joint motions with collision avoidance and kinematic limits.

Method: Extend Conflict-Based Search to jointly explore discrete trajectory-to-arm assignments and continuous null-space motions for redundant manipulators. The planner uses redundancy to avoid inter-arm collisions while tracking the prescribed end-effector trajectories, yielding a unified treatment of assignment and null-space optimization.

Result: A practically efficient planner that safely realizes coordinated manipulation-policy outputs on multi-arm robots, with theoretical completeness for grounding policy trajectories to physical systems.

Conclusion: Bridges the gap between learned, abstract multi-hand policies and executable multi-arm manipulation by providing a complete, safe grounding algorithm that accounts for assignment, redundancy, and collision avoidance.

Abstract: Learned manipulation policies increasingly predict motions for abstract "hands" and are attractive in practice because they rely on easily collected demonstrations and transfer across robot platforms. Executing these trajectories on multi-arm robots, however, is not trivial. Multi-hand policy outputs must be assigned to physical arms, each arm must realize a configuration-space motion that tracks its prescribed end-effector trajectory, and all arms must respect kinematic limits and avoid collisions. In the absence of algorithms that directly address this problem, practitioners typically extend single-arm inverse-kinematics (IK) pipelines in an ad hoc way, with no guarantees of feasibility or safety. In this work, we close this execution gap with a search-based framework that is theoretically complete for grounding policy-generated multi-hand trajectories onto physical multi-arm systems. Building on Conflict-Based Search, our method explicitly searches over both the discrete assignment of trajectories to arms and the continuous Jacobian null spaces of redundant manipulators, using redundancy to avoid inter-arm collisions while tracking the prescribed motions. This unified treatment of assignment and null-space motion yields a practically efficient planner that safely realizes coordinated manipulation-policy outputs on multi-arm robots. See omcbsa.github.io for more.

</details>


### [117] [Impedance Control of Ship-Borne Manipulators via Optimization-based Task-Space Inverse Dynamics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22030)
*Lingxiao Meng,Bi-Ke Zhu,Xuheng Gao,Zhe Zhang,Jiankun Yang,Jiankun Wang,Haibo Lu,Max Q. -H. Meng*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Torque-level optimization-based TSID with an error-state Kalman filter enables high-precision trajectory tracking and compliant manipulation for ship-borne manipulators under base motion, achieving >25% tracking improvement, 1 mm peg-in-hole clearance, and 45% reduction in contact forces.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Maritime manipulation suffers stochastic wave-induced base motions that induce kinematic disturbances and dynamic coupling, degrading tracking accuracy and complicating safe, contact-rich manipulation.

Method: Task-space inverse dynamics (TSID) formulated as quadratic programming to explicitly compensate for base-motion-induced dynamic coupling; error-state Kalman filter (ESKF) fuses inertial measurements with end-effector pose to estimate the base state for feedforward compensation; validated on a 7-DOF manipulator mounted on a 6-DOF Stewart platform in both simulation and real-world experiments.

Result: End-effector position tracking error reduced by 25.7% compared with the best baseline; enables dynamic peg-in-hole insertion with 1 mm clearance under base motion; increased insertion success rate and reduced average contact forces by 45%.

Conclusion: The torque-level TSID with ESKF framework achieves precise, compliant manipulation under base motion in contact-rich tasks, validating its potential for robust ship-borne manipulation in marine environments.

Abstract: Ship-borne manipulators operating in maritime environments are subject to stochastic wave-induced base motions that introduce kinematic disturbances and dynamic coupling, degrading trajectory tracking accuracy and complicating safe, contact-rich manipulation. This paper proposes a torque-level optimization-based control framework that integrates high-precision trajectory tracking with task-space impedance for ship-borne manipulators. The controller is formulated using task-space inverse dynamics (TSID) and solved via quadratic programming to explicitly compensate for the dynamic coupling introduced by base motion. To enable accurate feedforward compensation, an error-state Kalman filter (ESKF) is developed to estimate the base state by fusing inertial measurements with end-effector pose feedback. The framework is validated in simulation and real-world experiments using a 7-DOF manipulator mounted on a 6-DOF Stewart platform. The proposed method reduces real-world end-effector position tracking error by over 25.7% compared with the best baseline. Furthermore, the controller enables dynamic peg-in-hole insertion with 1~mm clearance under base motion, increasing the success rate while reducing average contact forces by 45%, demonstrating precise and compliant manipulation in contact-rich environments.

</details>


### [118] [One Hand Watches The Other: Dynamic Multi-Agent Cooperation for Sample-Efficient Bimanual Manipulation in Dynamic Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22119)
*Jan Ole von Hartz,Abhinav Valada,Joschka Boedecker*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: DynaMAC introduces a lightweight, policy-agnostic framework for dynamic and bimanual manipulation by treating the opposing arm as a dynamic task parameter, enabling zero-shot generalization from static demonstrations and a new benchmark (DynaBench). It achieves large data-efficiency gains and strong performance against baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multi-stream manipulation often relies on exogenous reference frames, but in dynamic settings where arms influence each other, this assumption breaks down. A framework that models inter-arm dynamics without explicit leader-follower roles is needed to preserve sample efficiency and flexibility.

Method: DynaMAC formulates the other arm as a dynamic task parameter within a policy-agnostic, lightweight architecture, enabling unified handling of dynamic manipulation and bimanual coordination without a prespecified hierarchy. The evaluation is conducted via the proposed DynaBench benchmark.

Result: Empirical results show DynaMAC outperforming leading probabilistic and generative baselines by over 35 percentage points and requiring ~20× fewer samples. It also demonstrates zero-shot generalization from static demonstrations to dynamic environments.

Conclusion: DynaMAC provides a cohesive framework bridging static and dynamic manipulation, preserving sample efficiency and speed while enabling dynamic, coordinated control. The accompanying DynaBench offers a new testbed for dynamic manipulation and bimanual coordination, with potential to facilitate human-robot collaboration.

Abstract: Multi-stream robot manipulation policies achieve unparalleled sample efficiency and generalization by modeling actions relative to environmental reference frames. However, existing approaches typically assume these frames to be strictly exogenous. This causal assumption collapses in dynamic settings, such as when a single robot arm manipulates a moving object or when two arms coordinate, where each arm effectively becomes part of the dynamic environment of the other. We propose DynaMAC, a lightweight, policy-agnostic framework that resolves this causal limitation while preserving the sample efficiency, computational speed, and flexibility of multi-stream policies, DynaMAC treats the opposite arm as a dynamic task parameter, thereby providing a unified formulation for dynamic manipulation and bimanual coordination without requiring an explicit leader-follower relationship. To rigorously evaluate these capabilities, we introduce DynaBench, a novel benchmark for robot manipulation in dynamic environments. Across both dynamic environments and bimanual manipulation tasks, DynaMAC outperforms leading probabilistic and generative baselines by over 35 percentage points while requiring 20 times fewer samples. Crucially, DynaMAC generalizes zero-shot from static demonstrations to dynamic environments, substantially simplifying data collection and establishing an elegant bridge toward human-robot collaboration.

</details>


### [119] [DB-VIO: Dual-Branch Visual Inertial Odometry with Enhanced Visual-Inertial Representation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22123)
*Ziyu Wan,Lin Zhao*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A dual-branch VIO (DB-VIO) decouples rotation and translation with depth cues and an explicit attitude prior, achieving state-of-the-art results across KITTI and EuRoC, notably large gains in rotation accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To overcome limitations of unified visual--inertial representations and single temporal models that struggle to capture heterogeneous rotation and translation dynamics; to leverage geometric structure in monocular visuals and explicit rotational kinematics in IMU data.

Method: A dual-branch architecture that (1) incorporates depth cues into monocular vision, (2) injects an explicit integrated-attitude prior to strengthen rotation-aware inertial features, and (3) decouples pose estimation into separate rotational and translational branches with motion-specific temporal modeling.

Result: Achieves state-of-the-art performance; +20% improvement over baselines on KITTI and +33% on EuRoC; rotation metric on EuRoC improved by 65.7%; demonstrates strong generalization across platforms and motion patterns.

Conclusion: DB-VIO provides a more faithful visual--inertial representation by separating rotation and translation, leveraging depth and attitude priors, leading to robust, generalizable 6-DoF VIO across diverse settings.

Abstract: Visual inertial odometry (VIO) is essential for accurate 6-DoF motion estimation in mobile robotic systems. Recent learning-based VIO methods have shown promising progress, but they often rely on unified visual--inertial representations and a single temporal model for full-pose estimation, limiting their ability to capture the heterogeneous dynamics of rotation and translation. Moreover, monocular visual features often lack explicit geometric structure, while raw inertial encoding leaves the underlying rotational kinematics implicit, weakening the rotation-related cues in IMU features. To address these issues, we propose DB-VIO, a dual-branch visual inertial odometry framework with enhanced visual--inertial representation. DB-VIO incorporates depth cues to improve monocular visual perception, injects an explicit integrated-attitude prior to strengthen rotation-aware inertial representation, and decouples pose estimation into dedicated rotational and translational branches for motion-specific temporal modeling. Experiments on autonomous driving and aerial robot benchmarks show that DB-VIO achieves state-of-the-art performance, improving the corresponding baselines by 20\% on KITTI and 33\% on EuRoC. Notably, under the more agile motion patterns of EuRoC, DB-VIO improves the rotational metric by 65.7\% over prior methods. These results demonstrate the effectiveness and generalization of DB-VIO across different platforms and motion scenarios.

</details>


### [120] [Flight-Ready LiDAR-Inertial Odometry for Embedded Drone Platforms](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22145)
*Alvaro J. Gaona,David Perez-Saura,Francisco J. Anguita,Pascual Campoy*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Incremental LIO improvements for real-time UAV control: raise odometry rate to IMU (200 Hz), supply full Twist at each IMU sample, reduce locking bottlenecks and race conditions via dual executors and synchronization, SLERP smoothing, IMU-forward propagation, and direct velocity publishing; validated on Livox Mid-360 with Pixhawk 4 Mini; applicable to FAST-LIO2-derived systems since estimator unchanged.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Real-time closed-loop aerial control requires high-rate, continuous odometry. Existing LIO implementations often publish at the LiDAR rate (~10 Hz), causing latency and control issues, and may lose continuity during LiDAR drop. Enhancing tempo and robustness is essential for UAV performance.

Method: Identify five architectural deficiencies in a representative tightly coupled IESKF-based LIO: odometry publishing tied to LiDAR rate (10 Hz), missing velocity outputs, execution bottlenecks blocking IMU processing, mutex contention, and synchronization race conditions. Introduce corresponding modifications: IMU-rate forward propagation, direct body-frame velocity publishing, SLERP-based smoothing, dual-executor isolation, and explicit synchronization protection. Preserve the underlying estimator (IESKF + ikd-Tree) while applying these changes.

Result: Odometry output increases from ~10 Hz to a stable 200 Hz; a complete Twist state is available at every IMU sample; continuity is preserved during transient LiDAR loss. Experimental validation on a Livox Mid-360 / Pixhawk 4 Mini autonomous UAV with motion-capture ground truth. The approach is applicable to FAST-LIO2-derived implementations since the estimator remains unchanged.

Conclusion: The improvements are architectural and transferable to similar LIO stacks; they enable real-time closed-loop UAV control by ensuring high-rate, continuous, and robust odometry outputs. Future work could assess broader sensor configurations, scalability, and integration in diverse flight conditions.

Abstract: Open-source LiDAR-inertial odometry (LIO) systems have achieved remarkable benchmark accuracy, yet current state-of-the-art implementations are primarily optimized for evaluation performance rather than the requirements of real-time closed-loop aerial control. When deployed onboard UAVs, this can introduce limitations that degrade flight performance. In this work, we identify five architectural deficiencies in a representative tightly coupled IESKF-based LIO implementation: odometry publishing tied to the LiDAR rate (10 Hz instead of the IMU's 200 Hz), missing velocity outputs, execution bottlenecks that block IMU processing, mutex contention, and synchronization race conditions. We introduce corresponding modifications including IMU-rate forward propagation, direct body-frame velocity publishing, SLERP-based smoothing, dual-executor isolation, and explicit synchronization protection. The resulting system increases odometry output from ~10 Hz to a stable 200 Hz, provides a complete Twist state at every IMU sample, and preserves continuity during transient LiDAR loss. Experiments on a Livox Mid-360 / Pixhawk 4 Mini autonomous UAV with motion-capture ground truth validate the approach. Since the underlying estimator (IESKF + ikd-Tree) remains unchanged, the proposed improvements can be directly applied to FAST-LIO2-derived implementations.

</details>


### [121] [Learning Spatiotemporal Decision Priors for Efficient Path Planning under Partial Observability](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22166)
*Yi Liu,Hongda Zhang,Leyao Zou,Chunlei Meng,Ziqing Zhou,Yuning Chen,Zhuo Zou,Lida Xu,Zhongxue Gan,Chun Ouyang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: ImiPath distills reusable spatiotemporal decision priors from demonstrations to guide planners under partial observability, improving search efficiency while maintaining path quality, validated in simulation and on a magnetic microrobot platform.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Under partial observability, classical planners solve each instance from scratch and miss transferable experience. Exploiting history-based directional preferences can reduce redundant search and improve long-horizon navigation.

Method: Construct a local spatiotemporal observation representation; use SpatioTemporal-Attention Policy Network (STAPNet) to convert it into decision priors; integrate priors into heterogeneous planners as directional biases to steer search toward promising regions; train from demonstration trajectories.

Result: ImiPath achieves competitive path quality and notably improves search efficiency by reducing redundant node expansions under local observability; physical experiments on a magnetic microrobot validate adaptability and deployment potential.

Conclusion: ImiPath provides transferable, experience-guided priors to bias planning in partially observable settings, offering practical improvements and showing promise for real-world deployment across heterogeneous planners and domains.

Abstract: Path planning under partial observability remains challenging because an agent must make long-horizon navigation decisions from only locally bounded observations. Nevertheless, historical trajectories contain reusable experience-guided directional preferences. Classical planners, however, typically solve each instance from scratch and lack an explicit mechanism to exploit such transferable decision knowledge, often leading to redundant node expansions and locally myopic search behaviors. Motivated by this limitation, this paper proposes ImiPath, a prior-guided learning framework that distills reusable spatiotemporal decision priors from demonstration trajectories and uses them as experience-informed directional guidance to bias planners toward reliable and promising search directions under partial observability. Specifically, ImiPath first constructs a local spatiotemporal observation representation, which encodes the spatial information of the local environment and the temporal information of historical trajectories. The SpatioTemporal-Attention Policy Network (STAPNet) then transforms this representation into dicision priors. These priors are further incorporated into heterogeneous planners as directional guidance, biasing the search toward locally promising regions. Extensive experiments demonstrate that ImiPath achieves competitive path quality and improves search efficiency by reducing redundant node expansions under local observability. Additional physical experiments on a magnetic microrobot platform further validate the adaptability and practical deployment potential of the proposed framework.

</details>


### [122] [Safe Learning Predictive Control for Ego-World Robotic Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22225)
*Davide Valenti,Giuseppe Notarstefano*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: SOWL-MPC enables safe autonomous navigation by learning a latent world policy with SVGPs and online conditioning, integrated in an MPC loop to anticipate and react to other robots' behavior.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Anticipating latent, unknown policies of surrounding robots is essential for safety; traditional controllers struggle with uncertainty and nonstationary neighbor behavior.

Method: Online Sparse Variational Gaussian Processes to infer a posterior over the latent world policy from noisy observations; Online Variational Conditioning to update with streaming data; approximate moment propagation through nonlinear world dynamics; uncertainty-aware MPC for safe control.

Result: Shows real-time feasibility and safety guarantees via Monte Carlo simulations in ROS 2 and validation on real hardware in an indoor arena.

Conclusion: Provides a viable learning-based predictive control framework for ego-world scenarios, enabling robust anticipation and safe maneuvers under latent policy uncertainty.

Abstract: Safe autonomous navigation in shared environments requires the ability to anticipate and react to the latent behaviors of surrounding robots. In this paper, we propose SOWL-MPC, a safe learning-based predictive control strategy for a novel scenario, which we name ego-world robotic framework. In this setting, the control policy of the world robot is unknown and the ego exploits data to learn it and perform safe maneuvers. The proposed architecture combines an online learning mechanism based on Sparse Variational Gaussian Processes (SVGPs) with a receding-horizon control scheme. Relying solely on noisy state measurements, our approach infers a posterior distribution over the latent world policy, which is updated on streaming data via Online Variational Conditioning (OVC). The learned policy is propagated through the nonlinear world dynamics using an approximate moment propagation scheme, and fed to an uncertainty-aware Model Predictive Control (MPC), thus enabling safe maneuvering of the ego robot. The real-time feasibility and safety guarantees of SOWL-MPC are demonstrated through extensive Monte Carlo virtual experiments in ROS 2, and validated on real-world robotic hardware in an indoor arena.

</details>


### [123] [Offline Vision-Language Navigation with Geometric Goal Localization for Outdoor Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22226)
*Ali Salmasi,Xianjia Yu,Tomi Westerlund*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Onboard VLN with edge SLMs benchmarked against cloud APIs; a lightweight hybrid semantic-geometric localization enables metric goals; integrated into Edge-BehAV for cloud-independent navigation; strong outdoor results (31/32 trials).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Cloud dependence and unreliable metric grounding limit fully onboard visual-language navigation; there is a need to systematically evaluate edge-deployable language models for instruction decomposition and develop onboard localization capable of metric goal estimation.

Method: Benchmark 17 edge-deployable SLMs against 4 cloud APIs for navigation instruction decomposition across three platforms using human-annotated instructions; develop a lightweight hybrid goal localization combining open-vocabulary object detection, prompted segmentation, and LiDAR geometry; integrate into Edge-BehAV as a fully onboard navigation system; evaluate on outdoor trials.

Result: Best offline SLM matches the strongest cloud API for instruction decomposition while running ~9x faster without connectivity; goal localization reduces mean distance error from 2.05 m to 0.20 m and lowers computational cost; complete system succeeds in 31 of 32 closed-loop outdoor trials.

Conclusion: The work demonstrates the viability of fully onboard VLN in outdoor environments, offers practical guidance for selecting onboard LMs, and shows effective onboard localization enabling cloud-independent robot navigation.

Abstract: Foundation-model-based vision-language navigation (VLN) has advanced autonomous robot navigation by enabling robots to interpret natural-language instructions, identify semantic goals, and follow user-specified behavioral rules. However, existing VLN systems rely heavily on cloud-hosted foundation models for language understanding and semantic grounding, limiting their applicability where network connectivity is unavailable and reliable metric goal localization is required. Although recent small language models (SLMs) enable fully onboard inference, their suitability for navigation instruction decomposition has not been systematically evaluated. This paper makes three contributions toward fully onboard VLN for outdoor environments. First, we present the first systematic benchmark of 17 edge-deployable SLMs against 4 online APIs for robotic navigation instruction decomposition, evaluating accuracy and latency on human-annotated instructions across three computing platforms and providing practical guidance for selecting onboard language models. Second, we propose a lightweight hybrid semantic-geometric goal localization framework that combines open-vocabulary object detection, prompted segmentation, and LiDAR geometry to estimate metric goals, while maintaining visual bearing guidance when reliable geometric observations are unavailable. Third, we integrate these advances into Edge-BehAV, a fully onboard extension of the BehAV architecture that enables cloud-independent behavior-guided navigation. Experimental results show that the best offline SLM matches the instruction decomposition performance of the strongest cloud API while running approximately 9x faster and without network connectivity. The proposed goal localization framework reduces mean goal-distance error from 2.05 m to 0.20 m at lower computational cost, and the complete system succeeds in 31 of 32 closed-loop outdoor trials.

</details>


### [124] [Design and Human Evaluation of Tactile Withdrawal Reflexes for a Skin-Covered Robot Arm](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22249)
*Laura Babayeva,Lukas Rustler,Matej Hoffmann*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A nociception-inspired reflex pipeline was developed for a robot arm with tactile sensing, mapping skin pressure to a pain gain and comparing three withdrawal strategies. A user study found that a uniform reflex (fixed joint moves) was perceived as more natural and safe, while Cartesian-space withdrawal was seen as the most appropriate reaction to touch; the bio-inspired, location-dependent reflex underperformed, likely due to embodiment gaps and expectation misalignment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Evaluate how artificial nociception and different withdrawal strategies affect human-robot interaction, particularly usability and acceptance metrics.

Method: Implement a pipeline converting skin pressure to a scalar pain gain via a nonlinear model. Compare three reflexes: (i) uniform joint-space withdrawal (fixed joint displacements), (ii) location-dependent joint-space withdrawal based on human reflexes, (iii) Cartesian-space withdrawal along the skin surface normal. Integrate these into a reflex controller that interrupts tasks, executes withdrawal, and resumes. Conduct a user study with 15 participants using Godspeed scales, custom naturalness/safety items, forced-choice comparisons, and qualitative feedback.

Result: Uniform reflex achieved higher ratings on anthropomorphism, animacy, likeability, naturalness, and felt safety; Cartesian reflex deemed most appropriate for touch; bio-inspired, location-dependent reflex failed to improve evaluated metrics, possibly due to embodiment gap and differing expectations between humans and robot manipulations.

Conclusion: Predictability of robotic withdrawal behavior enhances user acceptance. Simple, consistent strategies may outperform more sophisticated, biologically inspired ones given current embodiment; future work should address embodiment alignment and user expectations to unlock benefits of bio-inspired nociception.

Abstract: Nociception is a protective biological mechanism that links harmful stimulation to a reaction. This paper investigates artificial nociception for a robotic arm with whole-body tactile sensing. We present a complete pipeline that maps pressure changes from sensitive skin on a robot manipulator to bio-inspired withdrawal motions. The system first converts skin pressure into a scalar pain gain using a nonlinear continuous model. We compare three reflexes: (i) uniform reflex moves four robot joints by a fixed amount, whereby the withdrawal is approximated by a movement of the arm "toward the base", independent of where the robot was touched; (ii) biologically motivated location-dependent joint-space withdrawal derived from human withdrawal reflex characteristics; (iii) Cartesian space withdrawal along the surface normal of the contacted skin pad. All behaviors are integrated in a reflex controller that interrupts the task, executes the withdrawal, and returns to a pre-contact pose. A user study with 15 participants compared the strategies using Godspeed questionnaire subscales, custom perceived-naturalness and safety items, forced-choice comparisons, and qualitative feedback. Interestingly, participants rated more highly the uniform reflex behavior over one or both competitors on the anthropomorphism, animacy, and likeability Godspeed subscales and on the Naturalness and Realism custom scale. When asked to compare the conditions, the uniform reflex was scored best in "felt safest", "most human-like", and "most natural". This suggests that predictability of the robot behavior is key for user acceptance. The Cartesian reflex was judged the most appropriate reaction to touch. The bio-inspired reflex did not lead any evaluated measure. This may be partly attributed to the embodiment gap between the robot arm and human arm and participants having different expectations from a robot manipulator.

</details>


### [125] [A Monolithic Hand with Asymmetric Origami Bending and Dual-chamber Actuators](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22320)
*Nan Huang,Yuming Zhu,Zicong Zhang,Jianhui Liu,Xiaohuang Liu,Dihan Liu,Jiansheng Dai,Sicong Liu*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Proposes asymmetric origami bending (AOB) and asymmetric dual-chamber (ADC) designs to create a monolithic, origami-inspired soft hand (OSOR) with finger and palm actuators; enables safe, versatile grasping and simple manufacturing via selective laser sintering of TPU; experimental validation shows substantial bending (finger 203°, palm 40°) and forces (6.3 N finger, 16 N palm) enabling tissue pinch and bottle grasp.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Soft robotic hands offer safe, compliant interaction but often suffer from manufacturing complexity and bulky structures that limit performance. A lightweight, monolithic, origami-based actuator paradigm could reconcile high output with easy fabrication.

Method: Design of AOB-S (finger) and AOB-D (palm) units forming the OSOR hand with an asymmetric origami pattern and an asymmetric dual-chamber layout. Characterization via analytical models of AOB/ADC using an asymmetric ratio, complemented by Finite Element Analysis. Fabrication via Selective Laser Sintering of a single TPU material to enable monolithic assembly. Experimental validation of bending ranges and grasping capabilities across tissue pinch, bottle grasp, palm-only grasp, and power grasps.

Result: The OSOR hand achieves finger bending up to 203° and palm bending up to 40°, with output forces of 6.3 N (finger) and 16 N (palm). Demonstrates practical grasps including tissue pinching, stable water-bottle grasp with two fingers, palm-only grasp, and power grasps within a compact, manufacturable monolithic design.

Conclusion: The asymmetric origami approach enables integrated manufacturing and adequate actuation performance in a soft robotic hand, offering a promising route toward safer, versatile manipulation with simplified fabrication. Further work would extend dynamic performance, durability, and control under real-world tasks.

Abstract: The passive adaptability inherent in soft robotic hands affords them advantages in applications that require safe and compliant interaction. However, existing soft robotic hands often struggle to simultaneously achieve adequate output performance and easy manufacturing due to their complicated structures. In this paper, we introduce the asymmetric origami bending (AOB) pattern for generating bending motion and the asymmetric dual-chamber (ADC) design for obtaining multifunction capability. The AOB single (AOB-S) chamber and AOB dual-chamber (AOB-D) units are designed and constitute the finger and palm actuators of the proposed Origami-inspired SOft Robotic (OSOR) hand. The OSOR hand achieves bio-inspired fingers-palm motions and adequate output performance within a monolithic structure that significantly simplifies the manufacturing process. By defining the asymmetric ratio to characterize the geometric asymmetry of the unit, the analytical models of the AOB and ADC structures are proposed. The Finite Element Analysis tool for the design of AOB actuators is obtained by geometric analysis. The asymmetric origami design grants the integrated manufacturing of the OSOR hand through a Selective Laser Sintering printing process with a single thermoplastic polyurethane material. The model and simulations are validated by experimental results. Experiments show the finger and palm maximum bending motion range of 203° and 40°, respectively, with output forces of 6.3 N and 16 N.
