The OSOR hand is capable of pinching a piece of tissue, stably grasping water bottles with two fingers, palm-only grasping, and completing the power grasps in the taxonomy of manufacturing grasps. The compactness, performance, and easy manufacturing of the proposed hand benefit the development of the soft robotic hand with new possibilities.

</details>


### [126] [Teachy Mini: Development and Preliminary Evaluation of a Knowledge-Based Generative Social Robot for Higher Education](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22345)
*Stephan Vonschallen,Karim Kaufmann,Dominique Oberle,Friederike Eyssel,Theresa Schmiedel*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Implemented Teachy Mini by operationalizing knowledge-based design (KBD) for generative social robots in higher education. Found improved alignment with responsible tutoring but no robust differences in acceptance, motivation, or learning; potential learning gains when accounting for learner preferences.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Assess how enforcing KBD principles in GSR tutoring affects responsible behavior and learning outcomes in higher education, addressing risks of misinformation, transparency, and reinforcement of incorrect responses.

Method: Operationalized selected KBD requirements in Reachy Mini via system prompting, retrieval-augmented generation, and stateful prompt orchestration. Developed Teachy Mini and conducted a preliminary evaluation (N=24) comparing Teachy Mini to a control without KBD.

Result: Teachy Mini was perceived as more aligned with responsible tutoring behavior. Manipulation checks showed Teachy Mini used personalization, slide-grounded explanations, Socratic questioning, affective support, and learner-anchored feedback more consistently than the control. No significant between-condition differences in system acceptance, intrinsic motivation, or learning effectiveness; exploratory analyses suggested potential learning gains when accounting for learner preferences.

Conclusion: Provides an initial implementation and empirical evaluation of KBD for GSR tutoring, indicating KBD can shape responsible robot behavior and may enhance learning effectiveness in robot-supported education.

Abstract: Generative social robots (GSRs) powered by large language models offer new possibilities for personalized tutoring in higher education, but also introduce risks related to misinformation, missing transparency, or reinforcing incorrect student responses. Prior work identified knowledge-based design (KBD) requirements that define the informational prerequisites for GSRs to manifest responsible and effective tutoring behavior in higher education. In this paper, we operationalized selected KBD requirements in the Reachy Mini robot platform through system prompting, retrieval-augmented generation, and stateful prompt orchestration. As a result, we present Teachy Mini, a GSR tutoring system that was developed using KBD. To test the system, we conducted a preliminary evaluation study. Participants (N = 24) completed a robot-guided learning session about research methodologies. They learned either with Teachy Mini or with a control version that did not follow KBD principles. Teachy Mini was perceived as significantly more aligned with responsible tutoring behavior than the control robot. Moreover, a manipulation check illustrated that Teachy Mini used personalization, slide-grounded explanations, Socratic questioning, affective support, and learner-anchored feedback more consistently than the control robot. No significant between-condition differences were found in system acceptance, intrinsic motivation, or learning effectiveness, although exploratory analyses suggested a positive effect of KBD on objective learning gains when accounting for learner preferences. Overall, the study offered an initial implementation and preliminary evaluation of KBD for GSR tutoring, indicating that KBD can shape responsible robot behavior and potentially increase learning effectiveness in robot-supported learning.

</details>


### [127] [Conformal Constraint Tightening for Chance-Constrained Motion Planning with Unknown Dynamics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22409)
*Shubham Natraj,Bruno Sinopoli,Yiannis Kantaros*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A planner-agnostic, probabilistic constraint-tightening framework using conformal prediction to guarantee task completion on true dynamics even when dynamics differ from the nominal model.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Dynamic-model mismatch undermines task-completion guarantees; need planner-agnostic methods that provide probabilistic success guarantees under unknown dynamics while leveraging an approximate nominal model.

Method: Compute a probabilistic bound on nominal-to-true trajectory deviation via conformal prediction across planning problems; tighten planning constraints with this bound; solve tightened problem on nominal model; this yields a sufficient condition for success on true system with prescribed probability.

Result: Theoretically guaranteed probabilistic task completion; empirical validation showing substantially improved task completion relative to planning with the nominal model alone.

Conclusion: Constraint-tightening via conformal prediction enables planner-agnostic probabilistic guarantees under unknown dynamics, with practical improvements across planning paradigms.

Abstract: Motion planning algorithms compute control sequences that drive autonomous robots to goal regions while avoiding unsafe states. Existing methods, from sampling-based planning to deep reinforcement learning, typically provide task-completion guarantees only with respect to a nominal model or simulator, which may be invalidated when the true dynamics are unknown or difficult to model accurately. This letter addresses this limitation for systems with unknown dynamics and an available approximate nominal model, contributing a planner-agnostic constraint-tightening procedure that equips existing planners with a probabilistic task-completion guarantee on the true system. We leverage conformal prediction to provide a probabilistic bound on the nominal-to-true trajectory deviation over a distribution of planning problems. We tighten the planning constraints using that bound, and show that solving the tightened problem under the nominal model is a sufficient condition for solving the original problem on the true system with a prescribed probability. We validate the theoretical guarantees empirically and demonstrate substantially improved task completion relative to nominal-model planning.

</details>


### [128] [Robot Learning to Communicate through Projected Visual Abstractions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22434)
*Danyang Yan,Boyuan Wang,Jiaxun Liu,Boyuan Chen*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Robot uses a soft, shadow-enabled hand and a learned shadow self-model to express shadows; gradient-based optimization with simulation refinement enables dynamic shadow-based communication (sign language, puppetry, animal motion imitation) in simulation and on hardware.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To expand robot expressivity by leveraging projected visual abstractions (shadows) that emerge from embodiment, enabling intuitive human--robot communication and visual storytelling.

Method: A 21-DoF dexterous hand with soft skin reduces light leakage to create continuous silhouettes. A differentiable shadow self-model is learned from task-agnostic self-exploration to map hand configurations to projected shadows. Given a target shadow image/video, the system performs gradient-based optimization over the learned self-model and refines the motion via collision-aware simulation to ensure physical feasibility. For dynamic shadows, expressive-region objectives, temporal smoothness regularization, and keyframe-based optimization preserve important motion cues while reducing optimization complexity. Experiments include sign-language gestures, hand-shadow puppetry, and animal motion imitation in both simulation and physical experiments.

Result: Demonstrates robotic shadow expression across multiple domains in both simulation and hardware, showing a pathway to manipulate projected visual abstractions for communication and storytelling and providing a reusable framework for learning and optimizing shadow-based motions.

Conclusion: The work establishes a framework for enabling robots to communicate and tell stories through projected shadows, extending robot expressivity beyond direct body motion to level of light-field abstractions.

Abstract: Humans routinely communicate through abstractions of their bodies, including shadows, silhouettes, and reflections. Yet robots remain largely confined to expressing themselves through their physical morphology. Enabling robots to communicate through such projected visual abstractions requires reasoning not only about bodily motion but also about how that motion is transformed into an external representation perceived by an observer. Among these abstractions, shadows provide a particularly compelling example because they emerge directly from the robot's embodiment while remaining visually distinct from the body itself. Here, we present a robotic system capable of dynamic shadow expression using a 21-degree-of-freedom dexterous hand with compliant soft skin and a learned shadow self-model. The soft-skinned embodiment reduces light leakage to produce visually continuous silhouettes, while the differentiable self-model learns the mapping between hand configurations and projected shadow appearance through task-agnostic self-exploration. Given a target shadow image or video, the robot optimizes its hand configurations through gradient-based search over 1 the learned self-model and refines the solution through collision-aware simulation to obtain physically feasible motions. For dynamic shadow performance, we further introduce expressive-region objectives, temporal smoothness regularization, and keyframe-based optimization to preserve visually important motion cues while reducing optimization complexity. We demonstrate robotic shadow expression across sign-language gestures, hand-shadow puppetry, and animal motion imitation in both simulation and physical experiments. These results establish a framework for enabling robots to manipulate projected visual abstractions of themselves for communication and visual storytelling.

</details>


### [129] [Plug, Play, and Comply: A Modular Framework for Online Variable Impedance with Arbitrarily Oriented Compliance Axes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22483)
*Mihael Simonič,Xiaocong Li*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A robot-agnostic, plugin-based compliant-control framework extends ROS control with standardized joint and Cartesian interfaces, enabling cross-manipulator compliant-control deployment via runtime-loaded control-law plugins and URDF/Pinocchio-based kinematics; supports task-dependent impedance and is open-source with real-robot and simulated demonstrations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To overcome the lack of reusable infrastructure for implementing compliant-control algorithms across different manipulators while preserving a common interface for high-level applications.

Method: A plugin-based architecture separates controller infrastructure from control laws. Generic wrappers interface with hardware abstractions; runtime-loaded plugins implement only the control law. Command interfaces cover joint- and Cartesian-space references, stiffness and damping gains, nullspace targets, and feedforward terms. Kinematics and dynamics are computed from URDF models using Pinocchio.

Result: The framework enables deployment of the same compliant-control implementation across platforms. It supports task-dependent compliance by online rotation of stiffness and damping aligned with local task geometry. Real-robot experiments demonstrate task-dependent compliance in contact-rich manipulation; simulations show portability across manipulators with different kinematic/dynamic properties.

Conclusion: An open-source complete framework with reference controllers, high-level task interfaces, and example configurations for various manipulators, facilitating development of compliant-control strategies and cross-platform portability.

Abstract: The paper proposes a robot-agnostic compliant-control framework that extends the ROS control ecosystem with standardized joint and Cartesian command interfaces. It addresses a key limitation of existing control software: no reusable infrastructure for implementing compliant-control algorithms across different manipulators while preserving a common interface to higher-level applications. A plugin-based architecture separates controller infrastructure from control-law implementation. Generic wrappers use existing hardware abstractions to interface with different manipulators, while runtime-loaded plugins implement only the control law. Command interfaces support joint- and Cartesian-space references, stiffness and damping gains, nullspace targets, and feedforward terms, enabling variable impedance and diverse compliant-control formulations. Robot kinematics and dynamics are computed from URDF models using Pinocchio. The architecture facilitates the development of compliant-control strategies and enables the same implementation to be deployed across platforms unchanged. The complete framework, including reference controllers, high-level task interfaces, and example configurations for various manipulators, is open-sourced. The reference Cartesian impedance controller supports task-dependent compliance by rotating translational and rotational stiffness and damping, allowing the principal compliance directions to be updated online according to local task geometry rather than remaining fixed in the robot base or TCP frame. This is particularly important in contact-rich manipulation, where the desired directions of motion, constraints, and compliance directions may vary throughout task execution. Real-robot experiments demonstrate task-dependent compliance in contact-rich manipulation, while simulations show portability across manipulators with distinct kinematic and dynamic characteristics.

</details>


### [130] [ViTacWorld: Scaling Visuo-Tactile World Models for Contact-Rich Robot Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22530)
*Yunao Huang,Shiyu Sang,Haotao Lu,Suting Ni,Shijie Wu,Ziyang Guo,Ye Shi,Jingya Wang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: ViTacWorld is a scalable visuo-tactile-action world model for contact-rich robot manipulation that pretrains on real and simulated tactile data and finetunes with real rollouts to synthesize visuo-tactile rollouts and evaluate policies.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Visuo-tactile sensing is crucial for contact-rich tasks, but real tactile data collection is costly and limited in diversity; a scalable model is needed to leverage tactile grounding and reduce sim-to-real gaps.

Method: Construct a world model using public tactile datasets and a simulated environment, pretrain on large-scale visuo-tactile trajectories (real and simulated), then finetune with real policy rollouts. Given actions, predict temporally aligned visual observations and tactile feedback to generate visuo-tactile-action rollouts for data augmentation and policy evaluation.

Result: Generates physically meaningful visuo-tactile rollouts, improves policy performance through scalable data augmentation, and enables action-conditioned policy evaluation.

Conclusion: ViTacWorld is the first framework to use a world model for visuo-tactile-action trajectory generation and policy evaluation in contact-rich manipulation.

Abstract: Contact-rich robot manipulation requires physical interaction cues that are often invisible to cameras, making tactile sensing essential for robust control. However, scaling visuo-tactile robot learning remains difficult because real tactile interaction data are expensive to collect, hardware-dependent, and limited in task and scene diversity. We present ViTacWorld, an action-conditioned visuo-tactile world model for scalable contact-rich robot manipulation. ViTacWorld leverages public real tactile datasets and a constructed simulation environment to scale visuo-tactile-action data, exploiting the fact that tactile signals are directly grounded in physical contact and can exhibit a smaller simulation-to-real gap than purely visual observations. The model is first pretrained with large-scale real and simulated visuo-tactile trajectories, and then finetuned with real-world policy rollouts to better match downstream manipulation behaviors. Given robot actions, ViTacWorld predicts temporally aligned visual observations and tactile feedback, enabling visuo-tactile-action rollout generation. To the best of our knowledge, ViTacWorld is the first framework that uses a world model for robot visuo-tactile-action trajectory generation and policy evaluation. It serves two roles: synthesizing rollouts to improve downstream tactile policies, and evaluating policies by predicting action-conditioned visuo-tactile outcomes under controlled action sequences. Experiments on contact-rich manipulation tasks show that ViTacWorld generates physically meaningful rollouts, improves policy performance through scalable data augmentation, and enables action-conditioned policy evaluation. Project page: https://vitacworld.github.io/

</details>


### [131] [Robot-Factored World Models via Robot Rendering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22535)
*Byungjun Kim,Taeksoo Kim,Hyunsoo Cha,Hanbyul Joo*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Robot-factored world models separate action realization and robot rendering to predict scene responses more reliably, improving cross-embodiment generalization and enabling retargeted demonstrations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Action conditioning in video world models conflates two distinct processes: (1) how commands are realized into robot motion, and (2) how the scene responds upon contact. Conditioning on actions leaks future states and hampers generalization across embodiments. A modular approach isolates the robot's influence.

Method: Two robot-specific factors are moved outside the world model: (1) action realization via the robot's controller/kinematics to produce a nominal trajectory, and (2) robot rendering via the URDF to render explicit robot geometry. Depth cues are paired (end-effector + scene depth) to resolve depth ambiguity. A camera-aware RGB/depth context plus rendered geometry forms a shared visual interface that remains consistent across viewpoints and embodiments, enabling the world model to learn object responses to visible robot geometry rather than raw commands.

Result: The rendered interface outperforms vector-conditioned baselines and generalizes to unseen robot embodiments at inference. The approach can also generate robot manipulation videos from human demonstrations by retargeting and rendering hand motion as robot geometry.

Conclusion: Separating action realization and robot rendering yields a robust, embodiment-generalizable visual world model that respects viewpoint consistency and can translate human demonstrations into robot-performing sequences.

Abstract: Action-conditioned video world models predict future observations from an initial observation and an action signal. In robotics, actions influence future observations through two distinct processes: they are first realized into robot motion by the robot body and controller, and the scene then responds through contact and object motion. Conditioning directly on action commands asks the world model to learn the realization process itself, while conditioning on logged future states leaks the interaction outcomes it is meant to predict. We propose robot-factored world models, which move two robot-specific factors outside the world model. First, action realization: each command is rolled through the robot's own controller and kinematics into a deployment-available nominal trajectory, a middle signal that avoids both action-realization learning and future-state leakage. Second, robot rendering: this nominal trajectory is rendered through the robot URDF, factoring the robot's geometry, kinematics, and appearance out of the model and into explicit rendered robot geometry. To resolve depth ambiguity, we pair end-effector depth with scene depth, giving geometric cues for contact and occlusion beyond image-plane overlap. Together, camera-aware static RGB/depth context and rendered robot geometry form a shared visual world-model interface that stays consistent across viewpoints and robot embodiments, so the model sees the action only as visible robot geometry and learns how objects respond to it. Our experiments show that the rendered interface outperforms vector-conditioned baselines and generalizes to unseen robot embodiments at inference. We further demonstrate that our model generates robot manipulation videos from human demonstrations by retargeting and rendering the hand motion as robot geometry.

</details>


<div id='cs.LG'></div>

# cs.LG [[Back]](#toc)

### [132] [Cloud-Native Evaluation-as-a-Service: A Microservices Architecture for Scalable AI Monitoring with Conformal Guarantees](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21623)
*Lei Yang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A cloud-native platform (EaaS) implements six stateless Kubernetes microservices for AI evaluation (conformal predictions, calibration, drift, fairness, DAG-based orchestration, storage); evaluated on coverage guarantees, MMLU token predictions, drift detection, and fairness on UCI Adult, with favorable latency and claimed novelty in combining conformal-prediction-as-a-service with microservice and DAG orchestration.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Enable scalable, reproducible, and governance-aware AI evaluation by deploying evaluation primitives as modular, cloud-native microservices with orchestration and storage, validated across multiple evaluation dimensions.

Method: Design and implement EaaS as six stateless Kubernetes microservices: conformal prediction with finite-sample-corrected Adaptive Prediction Sets, calibration assessment, drift detection via RFF-approximated MMD, fairness monitoring with bootstrap CIs, a DAG-based pipeline orchestrator, and a result storage API. Validate empirical coverage across 50 random calibration/test splits; assess language-model token predictions on MMLU with top-k logprobs and optional imputation; evaluate drift detection power and Type I error via RFF-MMD across drift severities; assess demographic fairness on UCI Adult; measure latency (p99) for prediction/calibration, and drift monitoring latency; compare with four open-source tools for novelty.

Result: Conformal/prediction and calibration services achieve sub-2ms p99 latency at batch size 100; RFF-MMD detects drift with 100% power for mild/severe drift at median bandwidth (Type I error 5–8.5%); on UCI Adult, DP gap=0.33 with stable alerts; MMLU tokens appear in top-20 logprobs with 0% imputation; simulated 10% imputation causes <1.5% coverage impact. Platform claims novelty in combining conformal-prediction-as-a-service, microservice decomposition, and DAG-based orchestration; benchmarked against four open-source tools.

Conclusion: EaaS presents a cohesive, cloud-native evaluation stack integrating conformal prediction, calibration, drift detection, fairness monitoring, orchestration, and storage, offering end-to-end evaluation capabilities and competitive latency, with demonstrated empirical guarantees and novel architectural integration.

Abstract: We present EaaS, a cloud-native reference architecture that operationalizes AI evaluation methods as six stateless Kubernetes microservices: conformal prediction with finite-sample-corrected Adaptive Prediction Sets, calibration assessment, drift detection via RFF-approximated Maximum Mean Discrepancy, fairness monitoring with bootstrap confidence intervals, a DAG-based pipeline orchestrator, and a result storage API. We validate four key methodological concerns. First, empirical coverage is consistent with the marginal conformal guarantee across K=50 random calibration/test splits, with mean coverage within 1.4 percentage points of the nominal target. Second, all four MMLU answer tokens appear in the top-20 logprobs with 0% imputation needed, and simulated imputation at 10% produces less than 1.5% coverage impact. Third, RFF-MMD achieves 100% detection power for mild and severe drift at the median heuristic bandwidth, with Type I error between 5-8.5%. Fourth, fairness monitoring on the UCI Adult Income dataset reveals significant demographic parity disparities by race (DP gap=0.33) with stable alerts across sequential batches. Conformal prediction and calibration services achieve sub-2ms p99 latency at batch size 100; RFF-MMD requires ~500ms suited for periodic batch monitoring. A comparison with four open-source tools suggests that, to the best of our knowledge, no current platform combines conformal-prediction-as-a-service, microservice decomposition, and DAG-based orchestration.

</details>


### [133] [On the Depth Scalability of Logic Gate Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21633)
*Taegun An,Dohun kim,Haebeom Lee,Changhee Joo*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: IALGNs enable scalable depth in logic gate networks by anchoring every layer to the input, preserving a computational spine while conditioning on the original input. This yields depth-dependent, input-conditioned computation with empirical gains on standard benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Classical LGNs saturate or degrade with depth due to optimization collapse and topology-induced limitations. To sustain deep computation, LGNs need both stable optimization and a mechanism to access input information across layers.

Method: Introduce Input-Anchored Logic Gate Networks (IALGNs) where each gate combines an evolving hidden feature with a direct input anchor. This preserves a computational spine and conditions every layer on the original input. An explicit depth-D path can depend on up to D+1 input bits, establishing a strict path-wise depth hierarchy. Random-k anchor relaxation is used to improve anchor selection without compromising the spine.

Result: IALGNs achieve consistent fixed-width depth-accuracy gains beyond 100 layers on MNIST, CIFAR-10, and CIFAR-100, whereas alternative LGN topologies saturate or degrade. Layer-wise probes, topology ablations, and effective-depth analyses show that input anchoring yields progressively more informative representations and preserves longer computational paths.

Conclusion: Sustainable depth in LGNs requires both stable optimization and an information-access pattern that supports input-conditioned refinement. Input anchoring provides this pattern, enabling depth to translate into meaningful computation across diverse vision datasets.

Abstract: Logic Gate Networks (LGNs) implement computation through compositions of Boolean operations, yet unlike classical Boolean circuits, existing LGNs do not reliably benefit from increased depth. We identify two distinct causes: optimization collapse in deep relaxed LGNs and a topology-induced limitation that persists even when skip-biased initialization and straight-through estimation stabilize training. Thus, trainability alone is insufficient; deeper layers must also receive information that supports useful computation.
  We introduce Input-Anchored Logic Gate Networks (IALGNs), in which each gate combines an evolving hidden feature with a direct input anchor. This topology preserves a computational spine while conditioning every layer on the original input. We show that a depth-D path can depend on up to D+1 input bits and establish a strict path-wise depth hierarchy. Random-k anchor relaxation further improves anchor selection without relaxing the spine.
  Across MNIST, CIFAR-10, and CIFAR-100, IALGNs achieve consistent fixed-width depth--accuracy improvements beyond 100 layers, whereas alternative LGN topologies saturate or degrade. Layer-wise probes, topology ablations, and effective-depth analysis show that input anchoring produces progressively more informative representations and preserves longer computational paths. These results demonstrate that scalable depth in LGNs requires both stable optimization and an information-access pattern that supports input-conditioned refinement.

</details>


### [134] [MotifRole-Diff: Risk-Optimal Role-Aware Corruption for Masked Molecular Graph Diffusion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21634)
*Tasfia Nuzhat Ornee,Elias Hossain,Ivan Garibay,Niloofar Yousef*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: MotifRole-Diff introduces role-aware masking for molecular graph diffusion, allocating masking budget across token roles to match their denoising difficulty and perturbation impact, improving validity and reducing FCD vs uniform schedules under matched compute.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Uniform corruption across heterogeneous molecular graph tokens neglects varying denoising difficulty and influence on decoded molecules; exploiting role information can improve generation quality.

Method: Define token roles in the molecular graph; measure denoising difficulty and perturbation impact per role; design a risk-optimal budget allocation across roles; implement MotifRole-Diff with the same architecture and lossless decoder; provide a theorem characterizing optimality of the role-weighted residual risk; conduct empirical evaluation on QM9 and MOSES.

Result: Under matched architecture/budget, QM9 validity up from 0.905 to 0.944; FCD from 1.701 to 1.609; MOSES validity from 0.920 to 0.938; FCD from 2.125 to 1.850; role-wise diagnostics show improved reconstruction across token categories.

Conclusion: Structurally informed, role-aware corruption is more effective than uniform masking for serialized molecular graph diffusion, improving generation fidelity while preserving architecture and decoder; provides a principled scheduling framework.

Abstract: Masked discrete diffusion for molecular graph generation typically applies a uniform corruption schedule to all tokens in a lossless graph-to-sequence representation, implicitly treating structurally heterogeneous molecular components as equally difficult and equally important to reconstruct. However, different molecular graph token roles exhibit substantial variation in denoising difficulty and their influence on the decoded molecule, motivating role-specific corruption strategies. We introduce MotifRole-Diff, a role-aware corruption process that allocates masking rates according to empirically measured denoising difficulty and graph-level perturbation impact while preserving the model architecture, clean sequence space, and lossless molecular-graph decoder. We formulate schedule selection as the risk-optimal allocation of a fixed masking budget across token roles. Our theorem characterizes optimality for the modeled role-weighted residual risk, while downstream generation performance is evaluated empirically. Under matched architecture, training budget, and sampling compute, MotifRole-Diff improves validity on QM9 from 0.905 to 0.944 while reducing FCD from 1.701 to 1.609, and on MOSES improves validity from 0.920 to 0.938 while reducing FCD from 2.125 to 1.850. Role-wise diagnostics further show improved reconstruction across molecular graph token categories. Together, these matched-compute results indicate that structurally informed corruption is a more effective masking strategy than uniform schedules for serialized molecular graph diffusion.

</details>


### [135] [Toward User-Conditioned Evaluation of Personal LLM Agents under Temporal Interventions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21635)
*Pin Qian,Su Wang,Yihang Chen,Qiaolin Yu,Xiaoyuan Wang,Zhitong Guo,Zhicheng Wang,Junxian You*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Proposes a four-condition protocol for evaluating personal agents under persistent, user-conditioned states; audits benchmarks for gaps; offers a minimal benchmark design and metrics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current benchmarks evaluate isolated capabilities (tools, memory, safety) but fail to capture cross-component failures propagating through persistent user-conditioned states; a unified, temporal evaluation protocol is needed for personal agents.

Method: Formalizes four conditions (explicit temporal intervention, persistent state across intervention, induced cross-dimensional effects, variation in user-conditioned state), audits public benchmarks for these conditions, identifies gaps, and proposes a minimal benchmark design with reporting metrics.

Result: No audited protocol satisfies all four conditions; identifies a focused gap; provides a concrete design requirement for future evaluation and candidate metrics for reporting user-conditioned adaptation.

Conclusion: Advocates a minimal, forward-looking benchmark design focused on evaluating how personal agents adapt to user-conditioned states and how failures propagate across components.

Abstract: Personal agents maintain memories, learned skills, tool configurations, and policy state that evolve with each user. Existing agent benchmarks often evaluate these capabilities in isolation: tool benchmarks test invocation under fixed APIs, memory benchmarks test recall or forgetting, and safety benchmarks test static policy compliance. We argue that personal-agent evaluation requires a different protocol: replaying the same temporal intervention across different persistent user-conditioned states and measuring how failures propagate across agent components. We formalize this requirement as four conditions: explicit temporal intervention, persistent state across the intervention, induced cross-dimensional effects, and variation in user-conditioned state. A focused audit of public benchmark protocols selected by explicit inclusion criteria identifies several close cases. Under our explicitly narrow operationalization, we did not find a protocol in that audited set satisfying all four conditions. This claim is scoped as a focused gap analysis with bounded literature coverage. This position paper proposes a minimal benchmark design and candidate reporting metrics for user-conditioned adaptation. The result is a concrete design requirement for future personal-agent evaluation, with metrics used as reporting tools for that requirement.

</details>


### [136] [Measuring the Dependency Gap: Diagnosing Inter-Column Fidelity in Tabular Generative Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21636)
*Jie Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Dependency-aware fidelity diagnostic reveals hidden inter-column dependencies in synthetic tabular data missed by standard metrics; fixing dependencies is not achieved by capacity scaling or simple post-hoc corrections.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard tabular-synthetic-data metrics largely ignore cross-column dependencies, risking overestimation of fidelity and minority-class utility in imbalanced domains.

Method: Introduce a dependency-aware decomposition of XGB-C2ST into marginal, dependency, and numerical-categorical cross components, using a worst-case fully-factorized reference and a best-case real-data oracle; apply to TabbyFlow/EF-VFM; analyze with respect to minority-class utility.

Result: A real dependency gap exists that standard metrics miss; destroying dependency collapses minority-class utility; the residual gap persists despite a 16x increase in model capacity; no cheap in-model dependency mechanism or post-hoc copula correction closes it; the gap is due to lack of explicit dependency supervision.

Conclusion: Caution against relying on conventional fidelity metrics; while mean-field objectives can be asymptotically exact, they do not address inter-column dependencies; dependency-aware supervision (or metrics) is needed, as capacity growth alone is insufficient.

Abstract: Synthetic tabular data is valued for preserving not only each column's marginal distribution but the dependencies between columns -- structure that carries much of the discriminative signal for minority classes in imbalanced domains such as fraud and clinical risk. Yet the metrics most commonly used to certify synthetic tabular data are, we show, largely blind to inter-column dependency: a baseline that models every column independently (and therefore destroys all dependency) is judged indistinguishable from real data by the logistic-regression C2ST, and the pairwise Trend score is only partially sensitive. We introduce a dependency-aware fidelity diagnostic that decomposes a strong classifier two-sample test (XGB-C2ST) into marginal, dependency, and numerical-categorical cross components, anchored between a worst-case fully-factorized reference (all dependency destroyed) and a best-case real-data oracle. Applying it to a state-of-the-art flow-matching generator (TabbyFlow/EF-VFM), we find a real dependency gap that standard metrics miss; destroying dependency outright collapses minority-class utility, and the generator's residual gap carries a smaller, consistent utility cost. We then ask whether this gap reflects a structural limitation of mean-field generative objectives. It does not: consistent with recent recovery results for variational flow matching, the objective is asymptotically exact. Yet the gap is stubborn -- a 16x increase in model capacity does not close it -- pointing to the absence of direct dependency supervision rather than a capacity or structural limit. Consistent with this, and because the residual gap is higher-order, no cheap intervention closes it: not an in-model dependency mechanism, not post-hoc copula correction, and not the 16x capacity increase -- a caution for a field that assumes such fixes help.

</details>


### [137] [Quasi-Monte Carlo Initialization for Meta-Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21637)
*Julian G. Soltes*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Quasi-Monte Carlo (QMC) meta-priors accelerate training convergence for meta-RL in similar continuous-control tasks, outperforming SB3 defaults; however, for dissimilar tasks, orthogonal initialization yields better unbiased search performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Improve meta-reinforcement learning initialization to enhance convergence and generalization by leveraging low-discrepancy sampling (QMC) to construct priors from a baseline task set, and to compare against standard orthogonal initializations in SB3.

Method: Apply quasi-Monte Carlo sampling to bound a population-based search and aggregate an optimal prior from a baseline set of tasks; compare QMC-derived meta-priors to SB3 orthogonal defaults across similar unseen and dissimilar continuous control environments.

Result: QMC meta-priors yield improved training convergence over SB3 defaults in similar unseen environments; in dissimilar tasks, orthogonal initialization is globally superior for unbiased search.

Conclusion: Task similarity governs the preferred initializer: QMC priors can accelerate learning when tasks are similar to those in the baseline set, while orthogonal initialization remains preferable for broad, unbiased exploration across heterogeneous tasks.

Abstract: This paper explores the efficacy of quasi-Monte Carlo (QMC) weight initialization for meta-reinforcement learning within modern benchmark environments. Various sampling methods are used to bound a population-based search and aggregate an optimal prior from a baseline set of tasks. The QMC meta-priors show improvements in training convergence compared to modern orthogonal (SB3) defaults when extrapolated to similar unseen continuous control environments. In dissimilar tasks, the orthogonal orientation was globally superior for an unbiased search.

</details>


### [138] [Toward Goal-Agnostic Joint-Embedding Predictive Control of Partial Differential Equations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21644)
*Jonathan Gallagher,Roberto Guglielmi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Goal-agnostic PDE control via a joint-embedding predictive architecture (JEPA) with a small 2D ViT encoder and action-conditioned latent dynamics, reused by an MPPI controller. Calibrated observables (kinetic energy) outperform latent-space L2 objectives, enabling accurate trajectory reproduction and improved Navier–Stokes control and stabilization, though latent probes are sensitive to measurement noise.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Control of PDE systems without task-specific rewards is challenging; aligning latent dynamics with physically meaningful, uniquely continuing observables can yield more robust state control than optimizing in latent space alone.

Method: Train offline, frozen JEPA: a compact 2D ViT encoder with action-conditioned latent dynamics; use MPPI for planning on the latent model. Compare two objectives: latent-L2 in latent space vs a kinetic-energy (KE) observable probe trained on latent rollouts. Evaluate on the PDE Control Gym 2D Navier–Stokes benchmark with native reward, RMSE metrics, and held-out targets; assess stabilization via direct KE regulation.

Result: KE-probe planning improves matched 50-episode native reward from -12.08±0.86 to -10.90±0.91 (95% CI) and reduces last-quarter velocity-field RMSE from 0.0765 to 0.0692. Across three withheld, dissimilar targets, KE planning lowers late field RMSE by 53% (0.0220 vs 0.0469), winning all 30 paired episodes. The same frozen model supports stabilization around a steady state with 2.7% mean relative error. Latent probes are brittle to measurement noise and missing pixels, yet results suggest latent dynamics can be dynamic and goal-agnostic while calibrated observables may be a better objective for state control.

Conclusion: Calibrated, unique-continuation observables can provide a superior objective for PDE-state control over latent-space goals; latent dynamics can remain expressive and goal-agnostic, but robust control benefits from physically meaningful observable-based objectives.

Abstract: We present a goal-agnostic control framework for partial differential equations (PDEs) built around a joint-embedding predictive architecture (JEPA). The small 2D ViT encoder and action-conditioned latent dynamics are trained offline without a reward or downstream goal, frozen, and reused by a model-predictive path integral (MPPI) controller. We find that when available, the control objective is better applied to an explicit physical observable (provided injectivity) than to minimizing raw Euclidean distance ($L^2$) in the learned latent space. For a learned linear kinetic-energy (KE) probe on frozen latent rollouts we can reproduce held-out trajectories with $R^2=0.989$, while requiring no change to the underlying world model. On the PDE Control Gym 2D Navier--Stokes benchmark, using KE-probe planning improves the matched 50-episode native reward from $-12.08\pm0.86$ for latent-$L^2$ planning to $-10.90\pm0.91$ (95\% CI), while lowering last-quarter velocity-field RMSE from $0.0765$ to $0.0692$. Across three intentionally withheld, dissimilar, aperiodic targets, KE planning lowers late field RMSE by $53\%$ relative to latent-$L^2$ planning ($0.0220$ versus $0.0469$), winning all 30 paired episodes. The same frozen model also supports controls targeting stabilization around a steady configuration via direct regulation of KE achieving $2.7\%$ mean relative error. While the latent probe is brittle to measurement noise and missing pixels, we believe the results support the claim that latent dynamics can remain both dynamic and goal-agnostic while calibrated observables (granted they guarantee unique continuation) may be a better objective for state control

</details>


### [139] [Multi-Horizon Consistency as Geometry: When Latent Dynamics Contract, and When They Do Not](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21645)
*Kavya Bhand,Aadi Joshi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper investigates how multi-horizon latent consistency (lambda) affects latent geometry in video predictors/world models, using L20 and E20 as diagnostics. It reports a domain-limited effect: increasing lambda tightens L20 and halves E20 on Moving-MNIST, but not on action-conditioned tasks or other videos; mediation indicates a strong association; a stochastic-forcing law L20 ~ 1.23 + 1.82 eta at lambda=0.8 emerges. The claim that soft consistency moves models toward a near-contractive band is domain-dependent; more randomized experiments and broader baselines are needed.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand what the hyperparameter lambda (weight on multi-step latent agreement) does to transition geometry in video predictors and world models, and whether it can be diagnosed via L20 and E20 across domains.

Method: Empirical study across multiple domains. Define and measure L20 (an expansion proxy) and horizon-20 prediction error E20. Systematically vary lambda (0 to 0.8) on Moving-MNIST with 6 seeds at a critical pair; perform paired t-tests and Wilcoxon tests for L20 and E20. Conduct an associational mediation analysis on MMNIST (r-hat=0.94). Conduct defensive checks with architectural baselines, exogenous stress tests, WorldTest, MPC, and scaling. Evaluate action-conditioned Pendulum-v1, CartPole-v1, and KTH Actions videos where E20 improves but L20 does not drop. Explore joint slices at lambda in {0.4, 1.2} across 30×30 cells, 5 eta × 3 seeds (eta_eff). Report lack of randomized lambda in MMNIST and omission of some baselines (DreamerV3/TD-MPC2).

Result: In Moving-MNIST, raising lambda from 0 to 0.8 reduces L20 from 4.96±2.01 to 1.01±0.06 (paired t p=0.005; Wilcoxon p=0.031); E20 halves from 0.365 to 0.177 (paired t p=1.1e-13); 4 of 6 seeds cross L<1 at lambda=0.8. The same loss does not drive L20<1 on action-conditioned Pendulum-v1, CartPole-v1, or KTH Actions even when E20 improves. Mediation analysis on MMNIST yields r-hat=0.94 (95% CI [0.88, 1.00], n=27; B=2000) though lambda was not randomized. Defensive checks broadly support a narrow claim: soft consistency nudges passive video toward a near-contractive band, but this band is domain-limited. A stochastic-forcing law L20 ≈ 1.23 + 1.82 η at λ=0.8 (bootstrap slope CI [1.73, 1.92], R^2=0.96) unifies control domains on the same η_eff curve. Joint slices at λ∈{0.4,1.2} show linear L20(η) slopes ≈1.69 and ≈2.00 (30/30 cells, 5 η × 3 seeds); no continuous (λ,η) surface was fit. The paper does not report DreamerV3 or TD-MPC2 results.

Conclusion: Soft multi-horizon latent consistency can push passive video toward a near-contractive band, but this effect is domain-limited rather than universal. The relationship between lambda, latent expansion (L20), and domain forcing (η) appears robust within Moving-MNIST but does not generalize across action-conditioned or different video domains. The evidence is limited by non-randomized lambda in MMNIST and by selective baselines; broader experiments with randomized design and additional baselines are needed to establish a universal claim.

Abstract: Multi-horizon latent consistency is a common training knob in video predictors and world models, but practitioners rarely know what it does to transition geometry. We treat lambda, the weight on multi-step latent agreement, as a diagnostic control and measure an empirical expansion proxy L20,q95 together with horizon-20 prediction error E20. On Moving-MNIST (n=6 seeds at the critical pair), raising lambda from 0 to 0.8 cuts L20 from 4.96 +/- 2.01 to 1.01 +/- 0.06 (paired t p=0.005, Wilcoxon p=0.031) and halves E20 (0.365 to 0.177, paired t p=1.1e-13). Four of six seeds cross L<1 at lambda=0.8. The same loss does not produce population L<1 on action-conditioned Pendulum-v1 or CartPole-v1, nor on KTH Actions video, even when E20 improves. An associational mediation analysis on MMNIST gives r-hat=0.94 (95% CI [0.88, 1.00], n=27, B=2000); lambda was not randomized. Defensive checks (architectural baselines, exogenous stress, WorldTest, MPC, scaling) mostly support a narrow claim: soft consistency can push passive video toward a near-contractive band, and that band is domain-limited. A stochastic-forcing law L20 ~ 1.23 + 1.82 eta at lambda=0.8 (bootstrap slope CI [1.73, 1.92], R^2=0.96) unifies control domains on the same curve via calibrated eta_eff. Complete joint slices at lambda in {0.4, 1.2} (30/30 cells, 5 eta x 3 seeds) show comparable linear L20(eta) slopes (~1.69 and ~2.00); we do not fit a continuous (lambda, eta) surface. We do not report DreamerV3 or TD-MPC2 returns.

</details>


### [140] [Adjustment Speed as a Safety Constraint for Nonstationary Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21646)
*Timothy Tomashevskiy*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Proposes adaptation-speed as a safety constraint in nonstationary RL; uses context forecasts and shielding to prevent unsafe actions when adaptation demand exceeds capacity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Nonstationary environments can cause transient unsafe behavior; existing safe RL often assumes stationarity and ignores adaptation speed, necessitating a safety framework that accounts for forecasted environmental change.

Method: Learned context representations with short-horizon context forecasts to estimate adaptation demand; compare demand with calibrated recovery capacity; when demand exceeds capacity, tighten admissible actions and activate an action-level shield.

Result: Reduces safety violations in short-horizon windows aligned with context changes; ablations show shielding conservatism for peak/tail risk while optimization-level adjustment reduces additional short-horizon switch-conditioned violations.

Conclusion: Adaptation feasibility is a practical safety principle for RL under nonstationarity; proactive intervention (shielding and action-level adjustment) can improve safety during environmental change.

Abstract: Ensuring safety in reinforcement learning under nonstationarity requires determining whether a learning system can safely adapt to forecasted environmental change within the required recovery horizon. Existing safe reinforcement learning methods typically assume stationary environments and do not explicitly consider adaptation speed as a safety concern. However, when environments evolve over time, delayed adaptation may result in transient unsafe behavior.
  This paper proposes adjustment speed as a safety constraint for nonstationary reinforcement learning. The central idea is to define safety in terms of adaptation feasibility: future states or regions may become unsafe when the adaptation required to remain safe exceeds the learning system's calibrated recovery capacity. The proposed framework uses learned context representations and short-horizon context forecasts to estimate adaptation demand and compare it with the agent's achievable adaptation capacity.
  When predicted adaptation demand exceeds the calibrated recovery capacity, the framework proactively tightens the admissible action set and activates an action-level shield to reduce unsafe behavior before violations occur.
  Experiments in a nonstationary driving environment show that the proposed approach primarily reduces safety violations in short-horizon windows aligned with context changes. Ablation studies further show that shielding is more conservative for peak- and tail-risk suppression, while optimization-level adjustment provides additional reductions in short-horizon switch-conditioned violations.
  These results support adaptation feasibility as a practical safety principle for reinforcement learning under nonstationarity and demonstrate that proactive intervention can improve safety during periods of environmental change.

</details>


### [141] [A Drift Stable Quantum Federated Learning for Intelligent Services](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21647)
*Shanika Iroshi Nanayakkara,Shiva Raj Pokhrel*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: DUQFL-Prox introduces a drift-stable quantum federated learning framework that uses adaptive unfolded SPSA updates with a proximal penalty and a learnable step-size controller, aiming to improve stability, generalization, and client fairness under heterogeneous data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Heterogeneous client data and noisy quantum optimization induce unstable local updates, client drift, and unfair performance across clients in privacy-preserving quantum federated learning, necessitating more reliable and fair distributed quantum learning for privacy-sensitive tasks.

Method: Each client performs adaptive unfolded SPSA updates; a proximal term keeps the local model close to the global model; a lightweight controller learns step-specific optimization parameters; evaluation on financial fraud and genomic classification tasks.

Result: The proposed method outperforms standard QFL baselines in stability, generalization, and client fairness across the tested tasks.

Conclusion: Deep-unfolded quantum federated learning can support more reliable and fair intelligent services in heterogeneous distributed environments.

Abstract: Quantum federated learning enables distributed clients to train quantum neural networks without sharing local data, making it promising for privacy-aware intelligent services. Intelligent services in this context refer to privacy-sensitive distributed decision systems, such as fraud detection and genomic classification, where reliable and fair client-level learning is as important as the accuracy of the aggregate model. However, heterogeneous client data and noisy quantum optimization often cause unstable local updates, client drift, and unfair performance between clients. This paper proposes DUQFL-Prox, a drift-stable quantum federated learning framework based on deep-unfolded local optimization. Instead of using a fixed local optimizer, each client performs adaptive unfolded SPSA updates, while a proximal term keeps the local model close to the global model. A lightweight controller learns step-specific optimization parameters to improve post-aggregation performance. Experiments on financial fraud and genomic classification tasks show that DUQFL-Prox improves stability, generalization, and client fairness compared with standard QFL baselines. The results suggest that deep-unfolded quantum federated learning can support more reliable and fair intelligent services in heterogeneous distributed environments.

</details>


### [142] [Shallower ReLU Network Representations via Exact Linear Algebra](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21651)
*Kilian Rueß,Gennadiy Averkov,Florestan Brunck,Moritz Grillo,Christoph Hertrich,Georg Loho,Jack Stade,Moritz Stargalla,Matthew Sun,Martin Winter*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Max(n real numbers) exactly representable by a ReLU network with two hidden layers for n ≤ 10; for larger n, depth scales as ceil(log_5(n/2))+1; extends to all continuous piecewise-linear functions via hinging-hyperplane; improves on prior bounds.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Clarify the depth-efficiency of ReLU networks for exact representation of CPWL functions, particularly the max function, using constructive, verifiable methods.

Method: Apply symmetry reduction to reduce cancellations to finite linear systems over Q; solve these systems exactly (computational verification); design a first hidden layer consisting of pairwise maxima; recursively substitute to build larger networks; use generalized hinging-hyperplane representation to extend bounds to all CPWL functions with d+1 in place of n.

Result: A two-hidden-layer construction for max_n with n≤10; a depth bound of ceil(log_5(n/2))+1 for n>10; the same bound applies to CPWL functions on R^d via hinging-hyperplane, giving two-hidden-layer realizations for d≤9; improvement over previous bounds (e.g., ceil(log_3(n-2))+1) and earlier results.

Conclusion: Shows exact representations can be achieved with shallow ReLU networks for small n and with logarithmic-depth for larger n; presents a constructive, verifiable methodology and extends to all CPWL functions in low dimensions; invites further tightening and generalization.

Abstract: We prove that the maximum of $n$ real numbers is exactly representable by a ReLU network with two hidden layers for every $n\le 10$. The constructions are obtained by reducing the problem to exact rational linear algebra: after a symmetry reduction, the necessary cancellations are encoded in finite linear systems over $\mathbb{Q}$, which we solve and verify computationally. The representation of $\max_{10}$ has a structured first hidden layer consisting only of pairwise maxima, a feature that allows it to be recursively substituted into larger networks. We use this to show that for every $n>10$, the maximum $\max_{n}$ can be exactly represented with $\lceil{\log_5 (n / 2)\rceil}+1 < \log_5(n) +1.5694$ hidden layers. Via the generalized hinging-hyperplane representation [Wang, Sun, IEEE Trans. Inf. Theory 2005], the same depth bound holds for all continuous piecewise-linear functions on $\mathbb{R}^d$, with $d+1$ in place of $n$. In particular, every continuous piecewise-linear function on $\mathbb{R}^d$ for $d\le 9$ admits a two-hidden-layer ReLU representation. Our results improve on [Bakaev, Brunck, Hertrich, Stade, Yehudayoff, STOC'26]. In that work, the authors established a two-hidden-layer representation for $\max_{5}$ and an upper bound of $\lceil{\log_3 (n-2)\rceil}+1$ hidden layers for $\max_{n}$.

</details>


### [143] [Molt: A Scalable PyTorch-Native Training Framework for Agentic Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21653)
*Jian Hu,Huiying Li,Hao Zhang,Binfeng Xu,Yifan Zhang,Shaokun Zhang,Hemil Desai,Michael Demoret,Pavlo Molchanov,Jan Kautz,Yi Dong*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Molt is a compact PyTorch-native reinforcement learning training framework designed to reduce researcher cost while achieving performance parity with a Megatron-based stack.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Agentic RL research suffers from frequent codebase changes across trainer, backend, and rollout layers, imposing high iteration costs; a lean, readable framework improves traceability and AI-assisted development.

Method: Develop Molt as a PyTorch-native framework with a fully asynchronous training loop; support multimodal and mixture-of-experts policies; ensure training only on tokens generated by the model, maintaining token, policy version, and model semantics consistency; benchmark against a Megatron-based stack under matched asynchronous protocol; provide open-source recipes and containers.

Result: Molt is statistically comparable to the state-of-the-art Megatron-based stack under a matched asynchronous protocol; leanness does not cost performance.

Conclusion: Molt delivers a lean, end-to-end traceable RL training framework without sacrificing performance, with open-source availability for researchers and AI-assisted tooling.

Abstract: Agentic reinforcement learning research is constant algorithm modification, new estimators, new pipeline stages, new rollout schemes, and in mainstream frameworks each change threads through layers of trainer, distributed backend, and rollout glue: the cost lands on the researcher at every iteration. Molt is a PyTorch-native training framework built to keep that cost small: a codebase compact and clean enough for a researcher to hold in their head, and for an AI coding assistant to read and reason about in its entirety, so the algorithm flow can be traced and changed end to end. The agent is an ordinary program, and one asynchronous loop trains multimodal and mixture-of-experts policies while never training on a token it did not generate, consistent in tokens, policy versions, and model semantics. Leanness does not cost performance: under a matched, fully asynchronous protocol, Molt is statistically comparable to a state-of-the-art Megatron-based stack. Molt is open source and provides recipes and containers at https://github.com/NVIDIA-NeMo/labs-molt.

</details>


### [144] [Physically Constrained Federated Additive Models for O-RAN SLA-Risk Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21665)
*Aubida A. Al-Hameed,Mohammed M. H. Qazzaz,Maryam Hafeez,Syed A. Zaidi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Monotone FedNAM is a federated additive model enforcing monotone constraints on physics-grounded KPIs to prevent physically invalid SLA risk predictions in O-RAN, while allowing unconstrained KPIs to vary; federated training preserves auditability and privacy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Need for auditable, privacy-preserving per-slice SLA risk inference across base stations without pooling commercially sensitive per-slice KPIs; existing NAMs exhibit physics-violating predictions under local and federated training, worsened by non-IID FedAvg.

Method: Introduce Monotone FedNAM: a federated additive model that uses monotone splines for KPIs with unambiguous physical direction; leave contestable KPIs unconstrained; aggregate via FedAvg; deploy as Non-RT RIC rApp and near-RT xApp.

Result: On ColO-RAN dataset, eliminates all monotonicity violations; constrained-shape consistency rises from 0.71 to 1.00; generalizes to unseen scheduling policy; reduces uplink traffic by 65%; incurs a modest AUC cost of 0.04–0.07.

Conclusion: Physically constrained federated additive models enable auditable SLA risk inference for multi-tenant O-RAN; Monotone FedNAM demonstrates improved physical validity, generalization, and traffic efficiency with small accuracy trade-offs.

Abstract: Proactive service assurance in O-RAN requires predicting per-slice SLA violations before they occur. The prediction model must be auditable by operators and must train across base stations without pooling per-slice KPIs, which are commercially sensitive because slices are leased to individual tenants. Neural additive models (NAMs) offer auditability because each KPI contributes through a visible shape function. However, visibility alone does not guarantee physical validity. On the ColO-RAN testbed dataset, unconstrained NAMs learn effects that contradict wireless physics, for example predicting higher risk when channel quality improves. This failure appears under both local and centralized training, and non-IID federated averaging worsens it. We present Monotone FedNAM, a federated additive model in which KPIs with unambiguous physical direction are represented as monotone splines whose constraints survive FedAvg aggregation by construction, while contestable KPIs remain unconstrained. The model trains and operates as a Non-RT RIC rApp and is compact enough for deployment as a Near-RT RIC xApp. Monotone FedNAM eliminates all monotonicity violations, raises constrained shape consistency from 0.71 to 1.00, generalizes to an unseen scheduling policy, and reduces uplink traffic by 65%, at a cost of 0.04 to 0.07 AUC. These results show that physically constrained federated additive models can support auditable SLA risk inference for multi-tenant O-RAN service assurance

</details>


### [145] [Neural Feature Governance: Extending Atom Prevalence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21671)
*Idris Karel Seunda Ekwe,Patrick Tenga Shako,Ernest Parfait Fokoué*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: NAP is a Bayesian framework for structured node-level sparsity and interpretability in feedforward nets, using a four-phase pipeline to identify neural atoms and produce sparse, well-calibrated models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address sparsity, interpretability, and uncertainty quantification in large neural networks by combining Bayesian model selection with a structured, unit-level view (neural atoms).

Method: Four-phase pipeline: (1) BLT identification via Iterative Magnitude Pruning (IMP); (2) soft variational training of Spike and Slab Independent Gaussian (SS-IG) model; (3) Poisson-Binomial (PB) optimal layer-size selection; (4) Bayesian fine-tuning to yield sparse, stable, interpretable models. Introduces neural atom as activation unit.

Result: Achieves state-of-the-art structural sparsity; active nodes reduced to ~8% of dense MNIST model; uncertainty attribution shows model ignorance only 3–4% of predictive variance; reliability diagrams show near-nominal interval coverage (93.4% observed vs 95% target).

Conclusion: NAP provides a reliable, theoretically grounded, computationally tractable approach for simultaneous sparsity, accuracy, interpretability, and uncertainty quantification in Bayesian neural networks.

Abstract: Neural network compression and interpretability remain open challenges in modern deep learn- ing, where billion-parameter architectures deliver impressive accuracy at the cost of trans- parency, computational efficiency, and reliable uncertainty quantification. This paper introduces Neural Atom Prevalence (NAP), a principled Bayesian framework for structured node-level model selection in feedforward neural networks. NAP introduces the neural atom (activation unit) and functions as a hybrid method operating through a four-phase pipeline: Bayesian Lottery Ticket (BLT) identification via Iterative Magnitude Pruning (IMP), soft variational training of the Spike and Slab Independent Gaussian (SS-IG) model, Poisson-Binomial (PB) optimal layer-size selection, and Bayesian fine-tuning to produce a sparse, stable, interpretable, and accurate model. Extensive empirical validation across simulated nonlinear regression, two UCI benchmark datasets (Concrete, YearPredictionMSD), and the MNIST image classification task demonstrates that NAP achieves state-of-the-art structural sparsity, reducing active nodes to as few as 8% of the original dense architecture on MNIST, while well-calibrated probabilisti- cally: the aleatoric-epistemic uncertainty decomposition reveals that model ignorance accounts for only 3 to 4% of total predictive variance across all experiments, and regression reliability diagrams confirm a near-nominal predictive interval coverage (93.4% observed against a 95% target). These results establish NAP as a reliable, theoretically grounded, and computation- ally tractable solution to the simultaneous pursuit of sparsity, accuracy, interpretability, and uncertainty quantification in Bayesian neural networks.

</details>


### [146] [Self-Poisoning in Adaptive Out-of-Distribution Detection: A Sharp-Threshold Theory and Certified Label-Free Calibration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21673)
*Vishnu Bindu Balachandran*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This work analyzes test-time adaptive, memory-bank based OOD detectors with unlabeled streams, modeling bank impurity as a generalized Polya urn to reveal a mean-field equilibrium and a reproduction-number-like slope. A near-critical regime (slope just below 1) leads to empirical collapse; a certified admission gate and static calibration (CDC) mitigate or remove the transition and restore nominal FPR, while a two-world impossibility theorem bounds fundamental performance under drift without labels.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Understand the dynamic failure modes of label-free, memory-based OOD detectors during streaming unlabeled data and assess robustness, including fundamental limits, of such adaptive systems.

Method: Model impurity as a generalized Polya urn; analyze almost-sure convergence to a mean-field equilibrium; derive a reproduction-number-like slope controlling stability; validate across 96 settings; propose a certified admission gate (frozen reserve) and CDC for drift; establish a two-world impossibility theorem contrasting drift and contamination without labels.

Result: The admission kernel is affine with a slope just below one across encoder families, indicating near-critical detectors; predicted threshold matches empirical collapse, with ungated dictionaries losing up to 0.163 AUROC. The certified gate severs feedback and eliminates the transition at all contamination rates, preserving label-free false-positive control. CDC restores nominal FPR on drift-affected data. A two-world impossibility theorem shows drift and contamination are indistinguishable without labels, yielding a closed-form power ceiling for label-free adaptive OOD detection.

Conclusion: The paper delivers a complete possibility/impossibility framework for label-free adaptive OOD detection, including design principles to prevent collapse and a formal bound on achievable performance under drift ambiguity.

Abstract: Test-time adaptive out-of-distribution (OOD) detectors update a memory bank from the unlabelled stream. We show this adaptation obeys a provable dynamical law. Modelling bank impurity as a generalized Pólya urn, we prove almost-sure convergence to a mean-field equilibrium whose slope acts as a reproduction number. Below one, impurity stays benign. Above one, the bank is fully poisoned and the detector collapses. The measured admission kernel is affine ($R^2 \ge 0.996$) with slope just below one in every encoder family (a protocol signature), so this detector class is near-critical by design, and across 96 settings the predicted threshold matches the empirical collapse, where ungated dictionaries lose up to $0.163$ AUROC. We then prove that a certified admission gate, reading only a frozen reserve, severs the feedback loop and removes the transition at every contamination rate, even adversarially, while controlling false positives label-free. For the complementary static-calibration failure under drift we give CDC, which restores nominal FPR label-free on all tested drift-affected cells. Finally we prove a two-world impossibility theorem. Drift and contamination are indistinguishable without labels, forcing a closed-form power ceiling our procedure approaches. Together these give a complete possibility/impossibility characterization of label-free adaptive OOD detection.

</details>


### [147] [Encoding Invisible Causation for Bridge Diagnostic Agents: Triple-Guided Retrieval-Augmented Fine-Tuning with QLoRA](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21680)
*Takato Yasuno*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A retrieval-augmented, parameter-efficient encoder classifies 10 bridge-damage causes from visible descriptions using retrieved causal triples; QLoRA offers equivalent accuracy to full-precision LoRA with faster inference and lower memory, plus a reusable Golden Testset for evaluation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Transform tacit, expert-knowledge-based damage diagnosis into an automated, data-driven pipeline that can run on edge hardware while maintaining high accuracy and generalization.

Method: (i) Knowledge Triple Extraction: LLM extracts (damage -> caused_by -> cause) triples from 15–35 diagnostic manuals and indexes them in FAISS. (ii) Retrieval-Augmented Context: for training/inference, retrieved causal triples C_i are concatenated with S_i to supply explicit encoder context. (iii) Systematic Fine-tuning Comparison: compare LoRA, QLoRA, QA-LoRA on a fixed Golden Testset (116 samples); evaluate generalization via a 100-sample diverse evaluation across 10 classes; introduce a stratified, deduplicated benchmark.

Result: QLoRA achieves identical test accuracy (87.07%) to full-precision LoRA, with 11% faster inference, 72% lower GPU memory, and better generalization on unseen inputs. On the 100-sample diverse evaluation, QLoRA outperforms LoRA by 13 percentage points across all 10 damage classes. A controlled Golden Testset is proposed as a reusable benchmark.

Conclusion: The approach enables memory-efficient, high-accuracy diagnostic agents suitable for edge deployment on consumer-grade hardware, demonstrating effective integration of retrieval-augmented context with parameter-efficient fine-tuning.

Abstract: Bridge infrastructure deteriorates gradually, yet its root causes---salt intrusion, freezing, fatigue cracking, and others---remain invisible to the naked eye. Expert diagnosis relies on tacit knowledge built over years of practice. We address the challenge of automating this latent causal reasoning by proposing a Damage Cause Encoder that classifies 10-class damage causes from visible damage descriptions $S_i$ for use in autonomous bridge diagnostic agents. Our approach chains three components: (i)Knowledge Triple Extraction---a large language model extracts causal triples of the form (damage $\xrightarrow{\mathtt{caused\_by}}$ cause) from 15--35 diagnostic PDF manuals and indexes them in a FAISS vector store; (ii)Retrieval-Augmented Context---at training and inference time, relevant causal triples $\mathcal{C}_i$ are retrieved and concatenated with $S_i$, converting implicit domain knowledge into explicit Encoder context; (iii)Systematic Fine-tuning Comparison---we conduct a rigorous comparison of LoRA, QLoRA, and QA-LoRA on a fixed Golden Testset (116 stratified samples), demonstrating that QLoRA achieves the optimal trade-off: identical test accuracy (87.07%) to full-precision LoRA, 11% faster inference, 72% lower GPU memory, and superior generalization across diverse unseen inputs. A controlled Golden Testset---stratified, deduplicated, and difficulty-tagged---is introduced as a reusable benchmark contribution. QLoRA further outperforms LoRA by 13 percentage points on a 100-sample diverse evaluation spanning all 10 damage cause classes.These findings enable memory-efficient, high-accuracy diagnostic agents on consumer-grade hardware for edge deployment.

</details>


### [148] [CARNet Cycle-Conditioned Core Aggregation and Redistribution for Multivariate Time Series Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21681)
*Awsaf Tausif Adib,Md. Shahria Sarker Shuvo,Md. Estehaar Ahmed Emon,Mustafa Kamal,Fuad Rahman,Shafin Rahman,Nabeel Mohammed*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: CARNet introduces a cycle-conditioned core aggregation approach that injects global periodic information into linear-complexity cross-variate interactions, outperforming transformer and non-attention baselines on multivariate forecasting across horizons.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Modeling cross-variate dependencies in multivariate time series with strong periodic patterns is challenging due to the quadratic complexity of attention and the lack of explicit leverages of global periodic structure in linear-core methods.

Method: Cycle-Conditioned Core Aggregation and Redistribution (CARNet) integrates global recurrent cycle information into efficient core-based interactions via a Multihead Core Aggregation mechanism to capture cross-variate dependencies with linear complexity.

Result: Empirical evaluations on multiple real-world multivariate forecasting benchmarks show CARNet consistently surpassing strong transformer and non-attention baselines across various prediction horizons while maintaining linear-complexity cross-variate modeling.

Conclusion: CARNet provides a scalable, attention-free framework that effectively leverages global periodic structure to improve multivariate forecasting performance without sacrificing linear-time complexity.

Abstract: Accurately modeling cross-variate dependencies remains a key challenge in multivariate time series forecasting, particularly in the presence of strong periodic patterns. Many existing approaches rely on attention-based mechanisms that incur quadratic complexity and scale poorly with increasing numbers of variates. Recent attention-free aggregation models address this issue through linear-complexity core-based interactions, but they do not explicitly leverage the global periodic structure present in the data. To overcome this limitation, we propose CARNet, a Cycle-Conditioned Core Aggregation and Redistribution framework that integrates global recurrent cycle information into efficient core based interaction modeling via Multihead Core Aggregation. Extensive experiments on multiple real-world multivariate forecasting benchmarks demonstrate that CARNet consistently outperforms strong transformer and non-attention baselines across diverse prediction horizons while preserving linear-complexity modeling of cross-variate dependencies.

</details>


### [149] [Learning What Matters: Supervising Sparse Attention Routing with Causal Evidence Sets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21692)
*Jim Allchin*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Attention patterns may not reflect causal dependence; distilling sparse selectors from a dense tutor often inherits mismatch, with teachers attending to outdated facts. Training selectors on causal evidence can match or outperform attention-based targets, even without annotations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Question the common assumption that attention distributions reveal what information a model uses to answer. Test this in retrieval tasks with exact evidence, to see whether attention aligns with causal dependence.

Method: Mask parts of context to test impact on answers; compare selectors trained to imitate attention vs. selectors trained on causal evidence. Use a two-step reference task to illustrate when attention overlooks earlier steps. Evaluate on frozen teachers and pretrained models (Qwen2.5-3B, Gemma-2-9B). Reconstruct evidence sets from masking without annotations and train selectors on them. Compare accuracy and alignment with teacher.

Result: Attention and causal dependence often disagree; distillation inherits the mismatch. Teachers may rely on outdated facts, and attention can vary across runs. In a two-step task, attention-based selector achieves 41% accuracy, while causal-evidence-based selector reaches 99% and matches the teacher. Evidence sets can be recovered without annotation. In pretrained models, Qwen2.5-3B attends more to outdated facts on 58% of conflicting examples; Gemma-2-9B accuracy improves from 56% to 99% when restricted to two relevant sentences. Overall, dependence can match or exceed attention as a training target.

Conclusion: Attention indicates where a model looks but not necessarily what it depends on. Causal evidence serves as a more reliable training target for selectors in sparse attention setups; reliance on attention alone can mislead about the actual evidence used.

Abstract: Sparse attention reduces the cost of long contexts by allowing each query to read only selected parts of the input. These selectors are often trained by distilling the attention patterns of a dense teacher, assuming that attention reveals which context the teacher actually uses. We test that assumption on retrieval tasks where the evidence for each answer is known exactly. By masking parts of the context and measuring whether the answer changes, we find that attention and causal dependence often disagree, and distilled selectors inherit the mismatch. Teachers attend to outdated facts they have learned to ignore, and their attention can vary across training runs even when they rely on the same evidence. In a two-step reference task, attention at the answer skips the intermediate step because it was resolved earlier in the forward pass: a selector trained on attention achieves 41% accuracy, while the same selector trained on causal evidence reaches 99% and matches the teacher. These evidence sets require no annotation: recovered from a frozen teacher by masking alone, they train selectors to the same accuracy. We find the same conflict in pretrained models: Qwen2.5-3B gives more attention to an outdated fact than the current one on 58% of conflicting-fact examples despite answering correctly, while Gemma-2-9B rises from 56% to 99% accuracy when restricted to the two relevant sentences.
