Attention shows where a model looks, not necessarily what its answer depends on; across the regimes we tested, that dependence matched or outperformed attention as a training target.

</details>


### [150] [An Introduction to Bayesian and Frequentist Simulation-Based Inference with Machine Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21702)
*Maximilian Dax,Theo Heimel,Gilles Louppe*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SBI with ML enables Bayesian and frequentist parameter inference for inverse problems, including detector effects and unfolding, using neural posterior and neural likelihood estimation; the framework is validated and its limitations are discussed.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address inverse problems in science and engineering by unifying Bayesian and frequentist SBI approaches and enabling efficient parameter inference and detector-effect correction via ML-based amortized inference.

Method: Provide a conceptual overview of SBI with machine learning, focusing on neural posterior estimation (NPE) and neural likelihood estimation (NLE); show how these methods can perform parameter estimation within Bayesian and frequentist frameworks and how they extend to Empirical Bayes and unfolding tasks; discuss validation strategies.

Result: The work offers a unified view of SBI methods (NPE/NLE) within Bayesian and frequentist paradigms, demonstrating applicability to parameter estimation, detector-effect inversion, and unfolding, and outlining practical validation procedures and noted limitations.

Conclusion: SBI with machine learning is a versatile tool for inverse problems, capable of handling parameter inference and unfolding, but requires careful validation, calibration, and awareness of model/method limitations.

Abstract: Simulation-based inference (SBI) with machine learning is an increasingly important tool for solving inverse problems in science and engineering, including parameter inference and the inversion of detector effects. We provide an overview of the Bayesian and frequentist statistical frameworks, describe how machine-learning-based SBI methods, such as neural posterior estimation and neural likelihood estimation, can be used for parameter estimation within these frameworks, and show that the same methods can also be applied to Empirical Bayes or unfolding tasks. We also discuss how to validate inference results and the limitations of SBI with machine learning.

</details>


### [151] [A Defense of the Quadratic Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21716)
*Alexandru Meterez,Pranav Ajit Nair,Depen Morwani,Cengiz Pehlevan,Sham Kakade,Alex Damian*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A simple quadratic model can predict pretraining dynamics of an LLM over meaningful training windows, with Hessian spectrum and stability analyses revealing structure dependent on batch size, preconditioner, and time; the quadratic proxy could be a tractable theory for optimization in large-scale pretraining.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address the gap between tractable theoretical models and the complex, realistic optimization dynamics of neural networks. Test whether a quadratic approximation around intermediate points can meaningfully describe LLM pretraining.

Method: Taylor-expand the model and loss around intermediate checkpoints; evaluate predictive accuracy for windows up to ~10% of training. Estimate the Hessian spectrum with deep Lanczos probes; analyze spectrum structure and eigenvectors with respect to batch size, preconditioning, and training time. Assess local linear stability at checkpoints and compare with theoretical predictions.

Result: The quadratic model accurately predicts optimization dynamics over sizeable training windows. The Hessian spectrum shows structured eigenvalues/eigenvectors influenced by batch size, preconditioner, and training time. Local stability analysis reveals optimization operates at a stochastic edge of stability, modulated by batch size.

Conclusion: A quadratic model can serve as a theoretically tractable proxy for pretraining optimization dynamics in LLMs, providing insights into stability and spectrum and potentially guiding theory and practice.

Abstract: Due to the complexity of neural network loss landscapes, optimization theory is forced to rely on idealized models, and there is generally a tradeoff between how theoretically tractable the model is, and how accurately it describes the true optimization dynamics. In this work, we stress test the simplest possible model of optimization -- the quadratic model -- and show that it can be surprisingly predictive in an LLM setting with 150M parameters and 3B training tokens. Specifically, we show that Taylor expanding the model and the loss function at intermediate checkpoints through training can accurately predict the optimization dynamics over windows that can last up to 10\% of training. Having established this agreement, we then turn to analyzing the structure of these local quadratic optimization problems through two lenses: the Hessian spectrum and local stability. Using Lanczos quadrature with extremely deep probes, we are able to estimate the Hessian spectrum deep into the tail, and we find a surprising amount of structure in both the eigenvalues and eigenvectors, which depends on the batch size, preconditioner, and training time. We also empirically test local linear stability at intermediate checkpoints and compare it to theoretical predictions to demonstrate that optimization in LLMs typically occurs at a stochastic edge of stability, whose nature is also determined by batch size. Our results indicate the quadratic model may be a theoretically tractable proxy for pretraining optimization dynamics.

</details>


### [152] [RED-PIM: Reducing Data Movement for Transformers using Processing-in-Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21731)
*Zahra Yousefijamarani,Alaa Alameldeen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: RED-PIM is an algorithm-architecture co-design that accelerates transformer inference on processing-in-memory by minimizing inter-bank data movement and shrinking attention matrices, achieving large latency reductions, especially on long sequences.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Transformers incur heavy data movement during attention; PIM mitigates by computing in memory, but baseline approaches suffer from inter-bank communication and limited bank capacity, hindering scalability.

Method: Reorganize matrix operations to perform computations locally, optimize data transfer, and reduce attention data movement from O(N^2) to O(N) and intermediate matrices from N x N to d x d; implement algorithm-architecture co-design (RED-PIM).

Result: Inference time reductions from 16.05% to 99.99% (geometric mean 66.42%), with largest gains on longer sequences; real-world data shows 99.60% improvement for long documents and 13.44% for shorter ones; accuracy maintained or improved.

Conclusion: RED-PIM demonstrates scalable, efficient transformer inference on PIM by alleviating inter-bank communication bottlenecks and reducing data movement, especially beneficial for long sequence inputs.

Abstract: Transformers are widely used across many domains, including natural language processing, computer vision, web search, and DNA sequence analysis. Given their broad applicability, improving the performance of transformer models is critical. However, the high volume of data movement between processing units and memory during attention operations significantly limits their efficiency. Processing-In-Memory (PIM) mitigates this issue by performing computations directly inside memory. While prior work has proposed PIM-based transformer implementations, they suffer from costly inter-bank communication, and struggle to scale due to the limited capacity of memory banks. As a result, attention-related data must be split across banks, diminishing the potential benefits of PIM.
  In this work, we propose RED-PIM, an algorithm-architecture co-design that reduces attention latency by minimizing inter-bank data movement from O(N^2) to O(N) and shrinking intermediate attention matrices from N x N to d x d. By reorganizing matrix operations, performing computations locally, and employing an optimized data transfer strategy, RED-PIM significantly reduces computation cost and interconnect traffic. Compared to baseline PIM implementation, RED-PIM achieves inference time reductions ranging from 16.05% to 99.99% (geometric mean of 66.42%), with the largest gains on longer sequences. On real-world datasets, RED-PIM improves performance by 99.60% for long documents and 13.44% for shorter ones, while maintaining or improving accuracy. These results demonstrate RED-PIM's effectiveness for scalable and efficient transformer inference.

</details>


### [153] [Parameter-free Adaptive Sparse Attention via Compression-Based Content Selection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21752)
*Debarshi Kundu,Swaroop Ghosh,Vasant Honavar*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Data-compression-based, no-parameter sparse attention masks outperform fixed patterns and dense attention on long sequences, delivering state-of-the-art BPB on PG-19 without extra parameters and with faster convergence.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reduce the cost of long-sequence attention without introducing learnable parameters or custom kernels, while surpassing existing adaptive sparse attention methods.

Method: Compute per-block gzip compression ratios to identify non-redundant blocks and route long-range attention through them, forming an input-dependent, parameter-free sparse mask.

Result: On PG-19 with 92M params and 8K context, the method achieves 1.71 BPB, outperforming dense attention (2.89), BigBird (2.34), Longformer (3.21), and SBM-Transformer (3.38); gains up to 1.67 BPB with no parameters; performance advantage grows with sequence length and convergence is 3.3× faster.

Conclusion: Compression-based masking yields effective, adaptable sparse attention without learned parameters, competitive with or superior to dense attention and structured sparsity patterns, with greater benefits for longer sequences.

Abstract: Data-adaptive sparse attention masks substantially outperform fixed patterns (e.g., BigBird and Longformer) and can even exceed dense attention on long sequences. Existing adaptive approaches---including SBM-Transformer, Dynamic Mask Attention, and NSA---typically require additional learnable parameters, custom gradient estimators, or specialized CUDA kernels.
  We show that classical data compression provides an effective masking signal with \textbf{no additional parameters}. By computing per-block gzip compression ratios, we identify non-redundant content blocks and route long-range attention selectively through them. Intuitively, blocks that gzip cannot compress contain information not predictable from local repetition, making them natural long-range attention targets. Because the compression profile is input-dependent, the resulting sparse mask adapts dynamically to content without learned parameters, auxiliary losses, or custom kernels.
  On PG-19 byte-level language modeling at 92M parameters with 8K context, our method achieves 1.71 bits-per-byte (BPB), outperforming dense attention (2.89), BigBird (2.34), Longformer (3.21), and a reimplemented SBM-Transformer (3.38)---the only learned-mask baseline---by up to 1.67 BPB while adding no parameters. The advantage grows with sequence length, with the gap over BigBird widening from 0.05 BPB at 4K context to 0.63 BPB at 8K, while convergence is 3.3$\times$ faster.

</details>


### [154] [Smart predict-then-robustly-optimize](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21773)
*Aakil Caunhye,Xuefei Lu,Belen Martin-Barragan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Proposes a robust variant of smart predict-then-optimize to hedge against covariate perturbations via a convex surrogate; provides theoretical guarantees (exponential decay of approximation error probability under sub-Gaussian, high-probability Fisher consistency) and conditions for superiority; empirical results show improved out-of-sample performance and training stability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Real-world predictive-prescriptive pipelines suffer from noisy/corrupted features; standard models assume perfect side information; robust optimization can mitigate worst-case perturbations to improve decision quality.

Method: Integrate robust optimization into the predict-then-optimize pipeline; derive a tractable convex surrogate loss (smart predict-then-robustly-optimize); analyze approximation error via sub-Gaussian concentration and Fisher consistency; derive necessary conditions for superiority and compatibility with regularized upstream predictions; provide numerical experiments.

Result: The surrogate is computationally tractable and convex; approximation error probability decays exponentially under sub-Gaussian assumptions; Fisher consistency holds with high probability; necessary conditions for outperformance established; empirical studies show significant improvements in out-of-sample performance and training stability over standard methods.

Conclusion: The robust framework improves resilience to feature perturbations and enhances training stability, with theoretical guarantees and empirical validation; under mild assumptions, it can outperform standard smart predict-then-optimize.

Abstract: In this paper, we propose and study a robust variant of the smart predict-then-optimize approach that accounts for prediction shifts due to disturbance in the covariate feature space. While traditional integrated-learning-and-optimization models assume that side information is perfectly revealed, empirical data-driven features are frequently corrupted or noisy at the time of decision-making, leading to fragile operational policies. To bridge this gap, we integrate principles of robust optimization directly into the predictive-prescriptive pipeline via a smart predict-then-robustly optimize loss and establish a computationally tractable convex surrogate, designed to hedge against worst-case feature perturbations. On the theoretical front, we formalize the structural validity of this surrogate by proving its approximation error probability decays exponentially according to a sub-Gaussian concentration profile. Furthermore, we establish that under mild assumptions, the surrogate is Fisher consistent with high probability. We also prove necessary conditions under which our framework outperforms standard smart predict-then-optimize and maintain its superiority even when the standard method is equipped with regularized upstream predictions. Numerical experiments validate that our robust framework consistently yields significant performance improvements over standard methods, both in out-of-sample terms and in training stability.

</details>


### [155] [Physiological Signals as a Forensic Modality for Talking-Face Deepfake Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21776)
*Othmane Harraq,Tamer Aldwairi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: TF deepfake detection using rPPG signals via RhythmFormer; lightweight classifiers detect real vs synthesized physiological signals with AUC 0.806, EER 27.8%; results show method- and generator-dependence across TF synthesis methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: TF talking-face synthesis lacks a real video timeline, making traditional forensic cues weak; remote photoplethysmography (rPPG) offers a modality-specific signal to discriminate real vs synthesized videos.

Method: Extract per-video rPPG waveforms with RhythmFormer; train a suite of lightweight classifiers; evaluate on the TF subset of Celeb-DF++ under a subject-independent protocol; report 1D ResNet AUC and EER; perform a controlled reproduction study of DeepFakesON-Phys; assess performance across seven TF generators.

Result: 1D ResNet achieves AUC 0.806 and EER 27.8% on TF Celeb-DF++; close to the top general detector (Effort) by 2.4 AUC points while using only physiological signals; DeepFakesON-Phys performance drops from AUC 0.999 on legacy data to 0.622 on TF Celeb-DF++; AUC across seven TF generators ranges 0.690–0.985 indicating strong method dependence on generator properties.

Conclusion: Physiological-signal-based detection is viable for TF deepfakes; generator-specific physiological cues drive detection performance, suggesting evaluation should account for generator variety; primary contribution is revealing interpretability of generator-dependent rPPG cues and reproduction-level analysis; suggests future work exploring multi-modal or enhanced rPPG features and robustness across generators.

Abstract: Talking-face (TF) deepfake generation synthesizes photore- alistic facial video from a static source image and an au- dio signal, producing forgeries that current image-based detectors consistently fail to identify. Unlike face-swap ma- nipulation, TF synthesis has no underlying real video from which to inherit physiological characteristics, making re- mote photoplethysmography (rPPG) a uniquely motivated detection modality for this forgery category. We propose a detection framework that extracts per-video rPPG wave- forms via RhythmFormer and trains a suite of lightweight classifiers to distinguish real from synthesized physiologi- cal signals. Evaluated on the TF subset of Celeb-DF++ un- der a strict subject-independent protocol, where test identi- ties are completely separated from training identities, our 1D ResNet achieves an AUC of 0.806 and EER of 27.8%, placing it within 2.4 points of the best published general- purpose detector (Effort, ICML 2025) while operating ex- clusively on the physiological channel. We document a con- trolled reproduction study of DeepFakesON-Phys, the rep- resentative prior rPPG detector, demonstrating degrada- tion from AUC 0.999 on legacy face-swap data to 0.622 on the TF subset of Celeb-DF++. We further show that detec- tion difficulty is strongly method-dependent: AUC ranges from 0.985 (Real3DPortrait) to 0.690 (IP-LAP) across the seven TF generators, with the ranking remaining perfectly stable across all evaluation protocols. This spread reflects an interpretable physiological property of each generator rather than evaluation noise, and constitutes the primary theoretical contribution of the work.

</details>


### [156] [Bounding the Causal Impact of ML-assisted Decision-Making via Counterfactual Correctness](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21806)
*Jonathan Zhang,Erik Skalnes,Jacob Chen,Michael Oberst*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Presents a partial-identification framework to bound the causal effect of a newly deployed ML model using prior RCT data and two monotonicity assumptions that link predictive accuracy to downstream outcomes, with simulation showing tighter bounds than previous work.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: High-stakes ML deployment in domains like healthcare/criminal justice requires causal impact assessment. Re-running RCTs for retrained models is often infeasible, motivating a method to bound effects with existing data.

Method: Leverages prior RCT data and two monotonicity assumptions: (1) individual-level counterfactual correctness where correct predictions do not worsen outcomes; and (2) subgroup predictive performance relates monotonically to outcomes (trust-related). Uses partial identification to construct bounds on the causal effect of a new model; demonstrates via simulation. 

Result: The approach yields more informative (tighter) bounds on the model's causal impact than prior work, as shown in simulations.

Conclusion: Incorporating monotone relationships between predictive accuracy and outcomes enables practical bounds on deployment effects for updated models, reducing the need for repeated trials and guiding decision-making in high-risk settings.

Abstract: Predictive machine learning (ML) models are increasingly used to aid human decision-makers across various high-risk domains such as healthcare and criminal justice. There is a growing recognition of the need to evaluate the causal impact of deploying these systems on downstream outcomes, such as patient survival or crime recidivism. Randomized control trials (RCTs) can provide high-quality evidence on the impact of a deployed model, but they run into a challenge: it is often infeasible to run repeated trials when models are updated or retrained to improve predictive performance. In this work, we present a partial-identification approach to using prior RCT data to construct bounds on the causal effect of a new model. The core innovation in our approach is to leverage assumptions relating fine-grained predictive accuracy to downstream outcomes. We do so via two monotonicity assumptions: first, on individual-level `counterfactual correctness' (all else being equal, a correct prediction leads to non-inferior outcomes); and second, on the relation between subgroup predictive performance and outcomes, interpretable as an assumption regarding trust in model outputs. We demonstrate our method with a simulation study, illustrating how incorporating this information can lead to more informative bounds compared to prior work.

</details>


### [157] [Data eccentricity, asymptotics of Gaussian RBF reproducing kernel Hilbert space, and kernel PCA](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21823)
*Sergio A. Alvarez*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Gaussian RBF RKHS becomes asymptotically isometric to Euclidean space as bandwidth grows; kernel-based metrics converge to linear-kernel equivalents; Gaussian CKA and kernel PCA converge to their linear counterparts; embeddings differ by a similarity transform with residual O((ρ/σ)^2).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Clarify how kernel methods with Gaussian RBF kernels relate to linear methods in the large-bandwidth limit, enabling interpretation of kernel-based metrics (CKA, PCA) and guiding bandwidth choices.

Method: Theoretical analysis of the Gaussian RBF RKHS under large bandwidth (σ→∞). Derives asymptotic isometry to Euclidean space up to isotropic scaling, and analyzes eigenstructure and embeddings for kernel PCA, plus an error term dependent on dataset geometry ρ (ratio of max to median pairwise distances). Introduces ρ as a measure of geometric eccentricity. Provides experimental validation across diverse datasets to relate ρ with convergence behavior.

Result: As σ→∞, Gaussian RBF RKHS and Euclidean space are asymptotically isometric (up to scaling). Kernel-based quantities like CKA and PCA eigenstructures converge to their linear counterparts. For a fixed data representation, RKHS embeddings and PCA eigenframes differ by a geometric similarity transformation, with a residual on the order of (ρ/σ)^2. Experiments show ρ predicts dataset-specific convergence rates in the top principal directions.

Conclusion: Large-bandwidth Gaussian kernels effectively emulate linear methods in terms of geometry and spectral structure, with convergence governed by dataset geometry via ρ. This provides a practical criterion for predicting convergence behavior and informs bandwidth selection and interpretation of kernel-based metrics.

Abstract: We show that, up to isotropic scaling, the Gaussian RBF reproducing kernel Hilbert space (RKHS) is asymptotically isometric to Euclidean space in the large bandwidth limit. This strongly suggests that kernel-based constructions reliant on metric properties of the RKHS will yield results for Gaussian RBF kernels that similarly approach those of linear kernels for large bandwidths. The asymptotic behavior of Gaussian CKA can be understood in this light. We further consider kernel PCA, showing that Gaussian RBF eigenvalues, eigenprojections, and principal components all converge to those of classical (linear) PCA as bandwidth $σ\rightarrow \infty$. For a given data representation, both the RKHS feature embeddings and the orthogonal PCA eigenframes of the two kernel types differ asymptotically by a geometric similarity transformation, up to a residual of size $O \left (\fracρσ \right )^2$, where $ρ$ is a measure of geometric eccentricity of the representation, equal to the ratio of maximum to median pairwise distance between data examples. Experiments over a diverse collection of data sets demonstrate that $ρ$ provides a simple and reliable predictor of dataset-specific convergence behavior in the top principal directions.

</details>


### [158] [A Graph-Based Control Interface for Traffic Signals on Heterogeneous Road Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21831)
*Bertil Braun*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: GNN-based interface assigns movement scores for traffic control; junctions map scores to fixed, nonlearned phase sets via incidence matrices; evaluated with PPO on synthetic grids and five city graphs; shows cross-geometry generalization within grids but sensitivity to signal-coverage shifts; single city-policy can generalize across multiple graphs with mixed results; feasibility evidence, not a universal transfer guarantee.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To achieve scalable, transferable traffic-signal control by decoupling learned action scores from the phase definitions and timings, enabling variable action counts per junction while preserving learned context.

Method: A shared graph neural network operates on a directed corridor graph with movement nodes (inputs/outputs) and junction nodes; typed mean aggregation yields one scalar per movement; each junction uses a deterministic incidence matrix to map movement scores to a variable-sized, legally defined set of signal phases; phase definitions and timing remain outside the learned network; PPO optimizes the network. Evaluations include unseen synthetic grid geometries, altered signal coverage, and five heterogeneous city graphs, with a single city-policy tested across all graphs.

Result: Policies retained performance across unseen geometries within the synthetic grid family; performance degraded when signal-coverage distribution shifted; a single trained city-policy applied to five city graphs yielded heterogeneous outcomes.

Conclusion: The work provides feasibility evidence for transferability of learned signals under some conditions but does not offer a general estimate of transfer to arbitrary road networks. The action-space decoupling from learned parameters improves scalability, but sensitivity to signal-coverage distribution suggests robustness gaps to distribution shifts.

Abstract: We present a traffic-signal control interface in which a shared graph neural network assigns scores to individual traffic movements. Each junction converts these scores into its own variable-sized set of legal signal phases using a deterministic incidence matrix. Directed corridor nodes provide traffic context, while movement nodes represent controlled input-to-output paths through junctions. Typed mean aggregation produces one scalar per movement; phase definitions and signal timing remain outside the learned network. This makes graph size and junction-specific action count independent of the learned parameter shapes. PPO experiments evaluate the interface on unseen synthetic grid geometries, altered signal coverage, and five heterogeneous city graphs. The policies retained performance across unseen geometries within the synthetic grid family, while changes in signal coverage exposed sensitivity to a signal-coverage distribution shift. A single trained city-policy instance executed across all five city graphs, with heterogeneous outcomes. These results provide feasibility evidence rather than a general estimate of transfer to arbitrary road networks.

</details>


### [159] [Searching the Space of Feed-Forward Neural-Network Weight-Update Rules with Fixed Depth Symbolic Regression](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21855)
*Charles Brum,Edward Finkelstein*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Symbolic regression discovers competitive neural network weight-update rules; 25/30 improvements; ~44.5% aggregate MSE reduction; rules are diverse yet share themes; promising but needs larger-scale validation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To determine whether symbolic regression can automatically discover explicit neural network weight-update rules that outperform hand-designed optimizers on small symbolic regression benchmarks.

Method: Represent candidate update rules as fixed-depth symbolic expressions over operands derived from common optimizers (gradient, momentum, adaptive-gradient, moment estimates); evaluate across 30 benchmark/NN combinations and compare against the best hyperparameter-tuned optimizer.

Result: An update rule outperformed the best hyperparameter-tuned optimizer in 25 of 30 cases; aggregate MSE reduction of 44.47% in the improved cases.

Conclusion: Symbolic regression can serve as a lightweight mechanism to discover compact optimizer variants, but larger-scale validation is needed.

Abstract: We investigate whether symbolic regression can discover explicit neural network weight-update rules that outperform standard hand-designed optimizers on small symbolic regression benchmarks. Candidate update rules are represented as fixed-depth symbolic expressions over operands derived from common optimizers, including gradient, momentum, adaptive-gradient, and moment-estimate quantities. Across 30 benchmark/neural network combinations, the symbolic regression procedure found an update rule outperforming the best hyperparameter-tuned established optimizer in 25 cases, with an aggregate MSE reduction of 44.47\% over the improved cases. The discovered rules do not all share a single common symbolic form, but many combine adaptive normalization, momentum-like quantities, nonlinear transformations, and rational expressions. These results suggest that symbolic regression can serve as a lightweight mechanism for discovering compact optimizer variants, while also highlighting the need for larger-scale validation.

</details>


### [160] [LeAct: Learning to Reason from Expert Actions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21856)
*Ziran Yang,Chengshuai Shi,Raj Ghugare,Benjamin Eysenbach,Karthik Narasimhan,Chi Jin*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: LeAct treats chain-of-thought (CoT) as a latent variable, learning to recover reasoning from expert actions by sampling candidate CoTs and retaining those that improve action recovery. It achieves strong results across imperfect-information games and robotics, suggesting expert systems can be valuable sources of reasoning supervision for foundation models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Expert systems like game engines, planners, and theorem provers generate near-optimal actions but do not reveal their internal reasoning. Recovering their CoT would distill expertise into models with better generalization, addressing data limitations from purely human annotations or LLM-based distillation.

Method: LeAct models CoT as a latent variable. For each expert action, the student samples candidate CoTs and keeps those that measurably increase the probability of recovering the action. This yields a training signal that aligns the model's reasoning with high-quality expert behavior across imperfect-information games and robotics benchmarks.

Result: In small enumerable games, LeAct reaches the solver’s numerical floor. At larger scales, it is about 5× closer to the solver than the strongest expert-iteration baseline. In Flop Hold’em (~10^9 infosets), LeAct outperforms the opponent by +60 mbb/g, and in the robotics probe it uniquely improves on direct imitation.

Conclusion: LeAct demonstrates that expert systems can serve as a new, general source of reasoning teachers for foundation models, enabling distillation of latent expert CoT into students that transfer beyond demonstrated actions.

Abstract: Modern reasoning models depend on reasoning data, today sourced from human annotations or distilled from stronger LLMs. However, a rich and largely untapped source of supervision lies in expert systems (e.g., game engines, classical planners, theorem provers), which routinely produce near-optimal actions across diverse domains. But these experts are silent: they commit to an action without writing down the chain of thought (CoT) behind it. Recovering that CoT as natural-language reasoning would distill expert knowledge into a student that generalizes beyond the demonstrated actions. We treat it as a latent variable and study how to recover it from the action alone. Our approach, LeAct (Learning to reason from Actions), optimizes this latent variable: the student samples candidate CoTs for each expert action, and we retain those that measurably improve its own probability of recovering the action. Across imperfect-information games at multiple scales and a simulated robotics benchmark, LeAct reaches the solver's numerical floor on small enumerable games. At larger scale, it is $5\times$ closer to the solver than the strongest expert-iteration baseline. At Flop Hold'em ($\sim 10^9$ infosets), LeAct wins head-to-head by $+60$ mbb/g, and on the robotics probe it is the only training recipe that improves on direct imitation. We present a principled framework and the result: expert systems become a categorically new source of reasoning teachers for foundation models.

</details>


### [161] [Scaling Laws for Classical Machine Learning on Tabular Data: A Benchmark Study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21866)
*Kaihua Ding*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Large-scale classroom-style replication shows that power-law learning curves fit across multiple datasets and model families, with substantial within-family exponent sharing; but replicability is sensitive to protocol details rather than seeds; the work provides aggregated curves, per-cell fits, and practical data requirements for achieving target error.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To extend prior small-scale analyses of learning curves to a distributed, classroom-scale setting, test the prevalence of power-law error curves across diverse models and datasets, and assess the extent of cross-dataset exponent sharing and protocol-driven variability.

Method: A distributed replication with 127 students following a fixed protocol on 3 datasets each (from 18 tabular datasets and 6 model families: Boosting, Random Forest, SVM, Linear/Logistic, Ridge, Lasso). Total: 11,536 training runs and 1,648 fitted power-law curves of the form error(N) = a N^(-b) + c. Analyses include: (i) fit quality (R^2) of power laws across cells; (ii) cross-dataset exponent sharing within model families using constrained/unconstrained fits and AIC; (iii) leave-one-dataset-out stability; (iv) replicate-implementation variance with fixed random_state assessing protocol-induced variability in the exponent b; (v) data release of aggregated curves, per-cell fits, and a data-requirement table for N* to reach target error 0.15.

Result: (1) Power laws fit: R^2 > 0.8 in 77.7% of cells; tree ensembles dominate full-data performance (Boosting ≈50% of datasets, RandomForest ≈33%), while linear models perform worse on classification. (2) Approximate exponent sharing: for 5/6 model families, a single family-level exponent predicts cross-dataset curves nearly as well as per-dataset exponents (R^2 gap < 0.011); however, AIC favors unconstrained fits and curve collapse is partial (32–58% of points within ±0.5 dex). Lasso fails; Ridge is fragile under leave-one-dataset-out. (3) Replicator variance: with fixed seed 42, independent re-implementations differ in mean CV(b) = 0.144 for the fitted exponent, driven by unconstrained protocol components (preprocessing, encoding, missing-value handling) rather than seed variance. The work provides aggregated curves, per-cell fits, and a practical data-requirement table for N* to reach target error 0.15.

Conclusion: The findings support approximate predictive compressibility of learning curves within model families rather than universal dataset-independent laws. Power-law fits are common but not universal; cross-dataset exponent sharing exists within families, enabling near-parallel predictive curves, yet full collapse is partial. Replicability is influenced by protocol choices, highlighting the importance of documenting preprocessing and data-handling steps. The authors release data to enable reuse and practical guidance for required data scale to reach target performance.

Abstract: Prior classical-ML learning-curve work fits power laws to tree, linear, and kernel models on tabular data, but at small scale: typically one curve, one team, a handful of cells. We present a distributed classroom-scale replication: 127 students each ran a fixed protocol on 3 assigned datasets, drawn from 18 tabular classification and regression datasets and 6 model families (Boosting, Random Forest, SVM, Linear/Logistic, Ridge, Lasso), yielding 11,536 training runs and 1,648 fitted power-law curves of the form error(N) = a N^(-b) + c. Three findings. (1) Power laws fit: R^2 > 0.8 on 77.7% of cells, with tree ensembles dominating at full data (Boosting 50% of datasets, RandomForest 33%; linear models underperform on classification). (2) Approximate shared exponents within a model family: for 5 of 6 families, a single family-level exponent predicts each family's cross-dataset curves nearly as well as per-dataset exponents (R^2 gap < 0.011), though AIC favors the unconstrained fit and curve collapse is partial (32-58% of points within +/-0.5 dex). We frame this as approximate predictive compressibility, not dataset-independent universality; Lasso fails outright (negative control) and Ridge is fragile under leave-one-dataset-out. (3) Replicator-implementation variance: with random_state=42 fixed, independent re-implementations of the same protocol still differ by mean CV(b) = 0.144 on the fitted exponent -- not seed variance, but the spread induced by unconstrained parts of the protocol (preprocessing, encoding, missing-value handling). We release the aggregated curves, per-cell fits, and a practical data-requirement table for N* to reach target error 0.15.

</details>


### [162] [Variance-Reduced Q-Learning over Static and Time-Varying Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21876)
*Sreejeet Maity,Feng Zhu,Aritra Mitra,Robert W. Heath*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Proposes VRDQ, an epoch-based distributed Q-learning algorithm for multi-agent RL on a shared MDP, achieving linear speedups with near-constant communication; provides high-probability finite-time convergence for static and time-varying networks, improving sample complexity with ~O~(1) communication.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Enable collaborative Q-learning among multiple agents with a shared MDP while reducing communication overhead and achieving fast convergence.

Method: Epoch-based distributed Q-learning (VRDQ): within each epoch, agents locally estimate the Bellman optimality operator and use a consensus-based diffusion to share information; applicable to both static and time-varying networks.

Result: High-probability finite-time convergence rates with linear speedups due to collaboration; sample complexity gains require only ~O(1) communication, outperforming prior work in communication cost.

Conclusion: VRDQ provides efficient decentralized RL with minimal communication overhead, enabling scalable multi-agent learning on shared MDPs.

Abstract: We investigate a decentralized reinforcement learning problem involving multiple agents that interact with the same Markov Decision Process (MDP). The agents can exchange information over a network to collectively learn the optimal state-action value function. For this setting, we introduce a novel epoch-based distributed $Q$-learning algorithm called VRDQ, where within each epoch, agents locally estimate the Bellman optimality operator and diffuse information using a consensus-based protocol. For both static and time-varying networks, we establish high-probability finite-time convergence rates for VRDQ that enjoy linear speedups from collaboration. Crucially, we prove that such speedups in sample-complexity require only $\tilde{O}(1)$ communication, substantially improving upon the communication costs in prior work.

</details>


### [163] [Remedying Coarsening-Based GNN Training under Heterophily via Adaptive Complementary Enhancement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21885)
*Guoming Li,Jian Yang,Xukun Wang,Zixiao Wang,Shangsong Liang,Yifan Chen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: ACE provides a plug-and-play enhancement for coarsening-based GNN training to handle heterophily by reconstructing discarded information and applying heterophily-aware regularization with uncertainty weighting.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Coarsening-based training scales GNNs to large graphs but inherently loses information; this loss hurts performance on heterophilic graphs. A model-agnostic method is needed to recover discarded information and adapt to local heterophily.

Method: Adaptive Complementary Enhancement (ACE) learns a projector to reconstruct original node features from coarsened representations. It applies anisotropic structural regularization to capture local heterophily and uses homoscedastic uncertainty weighting to balance the primary coarsened-graph loss with an auxiliary loss from reconstructed full-graph features.

Result: Experiments show consistent gains on heterophilic benchmarks with minimal overhead and competitive performance on homophilic graphs. The approach is plug-and-play and code is provided.

Conclusion: ACE is a versatile, model-agnostic strategy that mitigates information loss in coarsening-based GNN training, enhancing heterophily handling while preserving efficiency.

Abstract: Coarsening-based training for graph neural networks (GNNs), i.e.\ training on coarsened graphs rather than the original large ones, has become a promising direction for scaling GNNs to massive graphs. However, prior work has been evaluated almost exclusively on \textit{homophilic} graphs, leaving the more challenging \textit{heterophilic} settings underexplored. We show, both empirically and theoretically, that existing coarsening-based training methods suffer significant performance degradation on heterophilic graphs due to inevitable loss of graph information during coarsening. To address this, we propose {\bf A}daptive {\bf C}omplementary {\bf E}nhancement, a plug-and-play, model-agnostic strategy that reintegrates the information discarded in coarsening: ACE learns a projector for re-constructing original node features and applies \textit{anisotropic structural regularization} to embed local heterophily. We further adopt \textit{homoscedastic uncertainty weighting} to adaptively balance the combined training objective of primary coarsened-graph training loss and full-graph auxiliary loss with augmented node features re-constructed by the heterophily-aware projector. Extensive experiments show that ACE drives consistent gains on heterophilic benchmarks while preserving competitive results on homophilic graphs with minimal computational overhead. Code is available at the GitHub repository: https://github.com/vasile-paskardlgm/ACE.

</details>


### [164] [MissHyper: Restoring Clinical Synchronicity in Missingness-Guided Hypergraph Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21922)
*Mingyi Ma,Qingxiong Tan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: MissHyper restores co-timestamp context before message passing in event-centric hypergraph models, using a missingness-guided, density-aware fusion, to improve multi-step clinical forecasting across multiple datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Clinical irregular multivariate time series with event-centric models lose co-timestamp context too early, creating a pre-propagation bottleneck. Leveraging local patient-state context and missingness cues before propagation can enhance sparse forecasting without redesigning the propagation architecture.

Method: MissHyper augments each event with a local support-density cue, aggregates co-timestamp records to recover patient-state context, and uses a missingness-guided gate to adaptively fuse node-specific evidence with the recovered context, applied before standard message passing.

Result: Consistent improvements in multi-step forecasting across PhysioNet 2012, MIMIC-III, and MIMIC-IV; outperforms a strong hypergraph baseline; ablations indicate snapshot restoration, adaptive fusion, and support-density encoding each contribute to performance gains.

Conclusion: Event initialization is a critical design axis for sparse clinical forecasting; improving the initialization stage can yield substantial gains without altering downstream propagation mechanics.

Abstract: Clinical irregular multivariate time series are shaped not only by physiological dynamics but also by the measurement process that determines when and what to observe. In event-centric models, however, co-timestamp structure can be flattened too early: measurements acquired at the same timestamp are embedded as isolated nodes, leaving local patient-state context unavailable until later message-passing layers. We study this pre-propagation representation bottleneck and address it by restoring co-timestamp context before message passing begins. We propose MissHyper, a missingness-guided hypergraph forecasting model with pre-propagation synchronicity restoration. MissHyper augments each event with a local support-density cue, aggregates co-timestamp records to recover patient-state context, and uses a missingness-guided gate to adaptively fuse node-specific evidence with the recovered context. Across PhysioNet 2012, MIMIC-III, and MIMIC-IV, MissHyper achieves consistent gains in multi-step forecasting and outperforms a strong hypergraph baseline. These results suggest that improving event initialization can benefit sparse clinical forecasting without requiring a redesigned downstream propagation architecture. Ablations indicate that snapshot restoration, adaptive fusion, and support-density encoding all contribute, pointing to event initialization as a critical design axis for sparse clinical forecasting.

</details>


### [165] [RIS-Kernel: A Model-Agnostic Architecture for Long-Context LLM Inference via Sparse Attention](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21927)
*Anderson R. Santos*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: RIS reduces attention to O(N log N) with sparse stochastic geometry, enabling model-agnostic, CPU-only long-context inference; experiments on Qwen2-1.5B-Instruct show competitive accuracy and regularization effects at 32k–65k tokens.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Quadratic complexity of full self-attention limits long-context processing and requires GPUs; need a weights-unchanged, model-agnostic solution that runs on commodity hardware.

Method: Introduce RIS inference engine with RIS-Stochastic and RIS-Structural variants; uses sparse stochastic geometry to achieve O(N log N) complexity; evaluate on Qwen2-1.5B-Instruct across 32k and 65k tokens; CPU-only; 1% and 5% densities; multiple seeds.

Result: At 32,768 tokens: RIS-Stochastic at 1% density with 70 seeds achieves 75.00% vs 71.88% dense; 5% density matches baseline. RIS-Structural at 1% with 10 seeds yields 68.75%. At 65,536 tokens: RIS gains up to 14.06 pp over zero-context floor; p=0.078. All on CPU with 16–128 GB RAM.

Conclusion: Sparse attention acts as a regularizer and enables long-context LLM inference on commodity hardware; RIS demonstrates practical gains and retrieval improvements without modifying weights.

Abstract: Full self-attention in large language models scales as O(N^2), which limits long-context document analysis to 65,536 tokens and requires costly GPU clusters. The Reduced Interaction Sampling (RIS) inference engine addresses this constraint as a model-agnostic architecture. Without modifying weights, RIS reduces self-attention complexity to O(N log N) using sparse stochastic geometry that fits within commodity memory limits. We validate RIS on Qwen2-1.5B-Instruct across two regimes. In controlled evaluations at 32,768 tokens (where native dense attention serves as the upper bound), RIS-Stochastic at 1% density and 70 ensemble seeds achieves 75.00% accuracy, outperforming the native dense baseline (71.88%), while RIS-Stochastic at 5% density and 10 seeds matches it (71.88%). This demonstrates that sparse attention acts as a regularizer: low density (1%) over multiple seeds filters out sequence-level noise, whereas higher density (5%) reintroduces distractor noise. Under the tightest budget, RIS-Structural reaches 68.75% accuracy at 1% density with just 10 seeds, recovering 75% of the contextual gap relative to the zero-context floor (59.38%). At 65,536 tokens, where dense attention triggers out-of-memory faults, RIS yields retrieval gains of up to 14.06 percentage points over the zero-context floor (51.56%), which is confirmed as marginally significant under McNemar's paired test (p = 0.078 < 0.10). All evaluations run on commodity, unaccelerated CPU servers (16-128 GB of RAM), demonstrating that long-context LLM inference is feasible on standard academic hardware without GPU acceleration.

</details>


### [166] [LatentFlow: Visual Analytics for Latent Space Analysis in Molecular Graph Neural Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21941)
*Shiyi Liu,Jiaqing Chen,Nicholas Hadler,Rostyslav Hnatyshyn,Michael W. Mahoney,Talita Perciano,John F. Hartwig,Gunther H. Weber,Ross Maciejewski*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: LatentFlow is a visual analytics system for analyzing latent spaces of molecular Graph Neural Networks (GNNs) across layers and model states. It clusters embeddings, tracks cluster evolution with a modified Sankey diagram, links clusters to representative molecules and substructures, and enables user-driven domain knowledge integration. Evaluated with two case studies, it helps diagnose latent-space organization and interpret model behavior.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand how molecular GNN embeddings organize chemical information beyond predictive accuracy, including how latent spaces evolve across layers and different model states, which is difficult with existing methods.

Method: Introduce LatentFlow, a visual analytics system that clusters embeddings and tracks cluster changes across layers and model states using a modified Sankey diagram; connect clusters to representative molecules and shared substructures; allow user input of domain knowledge and comparison with discovered patterns; evaluated via two case studies with domain expert collaboration.

Result: LatentFlow enables users to observe latent-space evolution, identify meaningful molecular patterns, and improve interpretation of model behavior.

Conclusion: LatentFlow provides a practical tool for diagnosing and interpreting latent spaces in molecular GNNs across layers and model states, with demonstrated utility in case studies and potential for integrating domain knowledge.

Abstract: Chemists and materials scientists increasingly use machine learning models, such as graph neural networks (GNNs), to predict properties of molecules and the outcomes of their reactions. Beyond predictive performance, understanding how these models organize chemical information internally in their latent spaces, i.e., the embeddings of the molecules, is critical. Analyzing latent spaces helps diagnose model behavior and assess whether the learned embeddings are organized in ways that reflect meaningful chemical relationships. Unfortunately, existing methods provide limited support for analyzing latent spaces across layers and across different model states (e.g., training epochs, model configurations, and input data), making it difficult to understand how these latent spaces evolve throughout a model or relate to chemical concepts. We present LatentFlow, a visual analytics system developed in collaboration with a domain expert for analyzing latent spaces in molecular GNNs. LatentFlow groups embeddings into clusters and supports exploration of latent spaces by tracking how these clusters change across layers and model states using a modified Sankey diagram. To support interpretation, LatentFlow links these clusters to representative molecules and their shared substructures, and it allows scientists to introduce their own domain knowledge and compare it with the patterns found in the latent spaces. We evaluate LatentFlow through two case studies. The results show that LatentFlow helps scientists understand how latent spaces evolve, identify meaningful molecular patterns, and better interpret model behavior.

</details>


### [167] [Multi-Agent Debate and Visual Information Extraction for SeePhys Pro: A 1st-Place Technical Report from ICML 2026 AI4Math Track 3 Challenge](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21946)
*Jiseok Kwak,Suhyeon Jo,Taewoo Kim,Yeongmin Kim,Byeonghu Na,Il-chul Moon*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Two-stage framework to answer physics questions with figures: (1) extract visual content into solver-readable text, (2) orchestrate three heterogeneous solvers via multi-agent debate. The analysis shows gains stem from reliable answer selection, and benefits scale with image-embedded content; achieved 0.802 public accuracy and top leaderboard placements.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Bridge the modality gap in visual physics problems where decisive information resides in figures, which challenges LLMs that primarily process text.

Method: Stage 1: visual information extraction re-expresses figure content as solver-readable text. Stage 2: reasoning stage coordinates three heterogeneous solvers through multi-agent debate to select reliable answers.

Result: Accuracy improved from 0.643 (baseline) to 0.802 on the public split; won 1st place on both public and private leaderboards (private overall 0.743).

Conclusion: The approach effectively closes the modality gap and benefits from reliable answer selection enabled by solver orchestration; the value of figure aids increases with the portion of the problem depicted in the image.

Abstract: This technical report presents our approach to Challenge Track~3: SeePhys Pro at the 3rd AI for Math Workshop, where the task is to answer college-level physics questions whose statement and figure may be given partly or entirely as an image. Visual physics problems become substantially harder for large language models when the decisive information resides in a figure rather than in the text, and this modality gap widens as more of the problem migrates into the image. We address the task with a two-stage framework: a visual information extraction stage that re-expresses figure content as solver-readable text to close the modality gap, and a reasoning stage that orchestrates three heterogeneous solvers through multi-agent debate. Our analysis yields two findings: the gain from orchestration comes from reliable answer selection rather than from additional debate, and the value of a figure aid scales with how much of the problem is locked inside the image. The resulting pipeline improves overall accuracy over a single-agent baseline from 0.643 to 0.802 on the public split, and won 1st place on both the public and the private leaderboard (private overall 0.743).

</details>


### [168] [MA-DAR: Manifold-Aligned Dynamic Adaptive Routing for Continual Temporal Knowledge Graph Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21949)
*Xiangjun Shi,Chong Mu,Jinchuan Zhang,Lizong Zhang,Yuefeng He,Shang Liu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: MA-DAR is a lightweight plug-and-play framework that fuses replayed and current temporal knowledge graph representations by aligning them on a shared manifold and applying dynamic, dimension-wise gating with a polarization regularizer, improving continual TKG reasoning across benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address representation conflicts in replay-based continual TKG learning, specifically norm domination and semantic blurring that arise when naively integrating replayed and current representations.

Method: 1) Manifold alignment of replayed and current representations into a common latent space to reduce distribution discrepancy. 2) Dynamic gating that learns dimension-wise fusion weights to adaptively combine replayed and current representations. 3) Polarization regularizer to discourage ambiguous gate decisions, promoting decisive routing. 4) Lightweight, plug-and-play design that can be applied to existing TKG encoders and replay settings.

Result: Empirical evaluation on four public continual TKG benchmarks shows MA-DAR consistently improves the performance of representative TKG encoders across different replay settings. Ablation studies and visual analyses validate the effectiveness of manifold alignment and dynamic adaptive routing in mitigating representation conflicts and enhancing continual reasoning.

Conclusion: Manifold alignment plus dynamic, polarization-regularized gating provides a stable, effective mechanism to fuse replayed and current knowledge in continual TKGs, offering a practical, scalable enhancement for continual temporal reasoning.

Abstract: Continual temporal knowledge graph (TKG) reasoning aims to continuously incorporate newly emerging facts while preserving previously acquired knowledge. Replay-based continual learning has achieved promising performance by revisiting historical representations. However, existing methods primarily focus on what to replay, while largely overlooking how replayed representations should be integrated with current ones. Such direct integration often gives rise to two critical forms of representation conflict: \textit{norm domination} and \textit{semantic blurring}, ultimately degrading continual reasoning performance. To address these challenges, we propose MA-DAR (Manifold-Aligned Dynamic Adaptive Routing), a lightweight plug-and-play framework for replay representation fusion. MA-DAR first aligns replayed and current representations onto a shared manifold to alleviate distribution discrepancies. It then employs a dynamic gating mechanism to learn dimension-wise fusion weights, adaptively determining the contribution of replayed and current representations to the fused representation. Furthermore, a polarization regularizer encourages more decisive routing behaviors by discouraging ambiguous gating decisions, resulting in more stable and effective knowledge integration. Extensive experiments on four public continual TKG benchmarks demonstrate that MA-DAR consistently improves the performance of representative TKG encoders while remaining effective under different replay settings. Comprehensive ablation studies and visualization analyses further verify the effectiveness of manifold alignment and dynamic adaptive routing in mitigating representation conflicts and improving continual reasoning.

</details>


### [169] [Teaching LLMs to Self-Evolve: Cultivating Core Meta-Skills with Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21971)
*Shujin Wu,Cheng Qian,Xiusi Chen,Heng Ji*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: MetaEvolve is a framework to cultivate self-evolution meta-skills via data synthesis, RL with verifiable code-execution rewards, and inference-time evolutionary search, achieving strong gains on coding benchmarks and open-ended problems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional post-training approaches neglect meta-skills like self-reflection with environment feedback that enable multi-round refinement. The authors aim to induce transferable, domain-agnostic meta-skills by training on rich, execution-based signals and evolving trajectories.

Method: Construct evolution trajectories as training data (current program, fitness, history of attempts); train with reinforcement learning using verifiable rewards from test-case execution; synthesize data from large-scale code; perform inference-time evolutionary search; ground in coding to obtain continuous feedback.

Result: Across seven coding benchmarks, MetaEvolve yields 10.01% absolute improvement in-distribution, 24.12% absolute improvement out-of-distribution; 46.9% relative improvement on open-ended algorithm optimization problems outside training domain.

Conclusion: Explicitly cultivating self-evolution meta-skills is a principled path to more capable, autonomously self-evolving AI with broad transfer potential.

Abstract: Test-time scaling through iterative self-evolution with environment feedback, as demonstrated by AlphaEvolve, shows remarkable performance gains. We hypothesize that the success of such evolution frameworks hinges on meta-skills, such as self-reflection with environment feedback, that enable effective multi-round refinement, yet are largely neglected by traditional post-training. To bridge this gap, we present MetaEvolve, a framework designed to develop these meta-skills via a data synthesis pipeline, evolution-aware reinforcement learning (RL), and inference-time evolutionary search. Concretely, we ground MetaEvolve in coding, where program execution provides natural, continuous reward signals beyond binary correctness. Building on these signals, we synthesize evolution trajectories as training data, each containing a current program, its fitness score (combining correctness and efficiency), and a history of prior attempts, and train the model via RL with verifiable rewards derived from test case execution. By training on large-scale code data, we aim to inspire generalizable domain-agnostic meta-skills that can transfer broadly to open-ended problems where such rich training signals are scarce. Across seven coding benchmarks, MetaEvolve outperforms the strongest baseline by 10.01% absolute on in-distribution tasks and 24.12% on out-of-distribution tasks. On open-ended algorithm optimization problems entirely outside the training domain, it further achieves a 46.9% relative improvement. These results demonstrate that explicitly cultivating self-evolution meta-skills offers a principled path toward more capable and autonomously self-evolving AI.

</details>


### [170] [On the Convergence of Stochastic Low-Rank Adaptation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21975)
*Ru Wang,Chengchang Liu,John C. S. Lui*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Deterministic LoRA optimization admits improved full-gradient complexity; stochastic LoRA with NSGDM and STORM achieves further reductions under variance reduction and mean-square smoothness.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To tighten convergence guarantees for LoRA updates by analyzing oracle complexity under deterministic and stochastic regimes and to propose efficient algorithms.

Method: Analyze the LoRA objective J(B,A)=L(W_base+s B A) with B in R^{m×r}, A in R^{r×n}, derive gradient complexities; propose two stochastic algorithms LoRA-NSGDM and LoRA-STORM with variance reduction.

Result: Deterministic setting requires O(ε^-4) full-gradient evaluations to reach ε-stationary; stochastic NSGDM achieves O(ε^-8) oracle complexity; variance-reduced STORM achieves O(ε^-6) under mean-square smoothness.

Conclusion: The study tightens, extends the theory of LoRA optimization efficiency, offering practical algorithms for both deterministic and stochastic regimes with provable first-order guarantees.

Abstract: Low-rank adaptation (LoRA) optimizes $J(B,A)=\mathcal L(W_\mathrm{base}+sBA)$ over two adapters $B \in \mathbb{R}^{m \times r}$ and $A \in \mathbb{R}^{r \times n}$ that form a low-rank update to a frozen pretrained weight matrix $W_\mathrm{base} \in \mathbb{R}^{m \times n}$. The prior analysis shows LoRA-GD takes $\exp\{\mathcal{O}(ε^{-2})\}$ oracle calls to find an $ε$-stationary point such that $\|\nabla J(B,A)\|\leq ε$ in the deterministic setting. We sharpen the analysis and show that $\mathcal{O}(ε^{-4})$ full-gradient evaluations suffice for the same first-order criterion. We further study stochastic LoRA under unbiased gradient estimates and finite variance. We propose LoRA-NSGDM, which finds an $ε$-stationary point with $\mathcal{O}(ε^{-8})$ stochastic oracle complexity. Under the additional mean-square smoothness condition, we use variance reduction strategy and propose LoRA-STORM, which improves the stochastic oracle complexity to $\mathcal{O}(ε^{-6})$.

</details>


### [171] [From Perturbation Correction to Geometry-Aware Sampling: Sharpness-Guided Equilibrium Sampling for Balanced Flat Minima in Long-Tailed Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21999)
*Jiaxin Deng,Junbiao Pang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SGS uses sampling as an active control of optimization geometry in long-tailed learning. By reweighting mini-batches to favor rarely seen classes and dampen classes with large SAM-induced loss changes, SGS improves tail accuracy and overall accuracy with minimal training overhead, without per-class perturbations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Long-tailed data cause two sources of poor generalization: underrepresented tail classes and sharpness-induced optimization instability from SAM. Existing re-sampling treats exposure or geometry in isolation; SAM-based methods modify losses after biased sampling. SGS aims to jointly regulate data exposure and optimization geometry through sampling.

Method: Treat the sampling distribution as a control variable. Dynamically adjust mini-batches by increasing probability for less frequent classes and suppressing classes with large SAM-induced loss changes, using only cumulative class counts and EMA sharpness from the standard SAM update—no class-wise perturbations or extra backward passes. Analyze via a continuous-time stochastic differential equation and a sampling-dependent PAC-Bayes bound to explain how frequency-sharpness feedback yields a flatter, more balanced loss landscape. Empirically evaluate on CIFAR-100 LT (imbalance 100) and ImageNet-LT against SAM-based baselines.

Result: On CIFAR-100 LT (imbalance 100), SGS-SAM improves tail accuracy by 10.85 and overall by 3.56 points over Focal-SAM. On ImageNet-LT, tail accuracy improves by 6.59 points and overall by 1.20 points over ImbSAM. Training time is 1.02× that of vanilla SAM.

Conclusion: A sampling-side mechanism to control loss-landscape geometry, SGS demonstrates that data exposure and optimization geometry can be jointly regulated for improved long-tailed generalization. This approach opens a path for future methods to integrate sampling strategies with optimization dynamics.

Abstract: Long-tailed learning couples two sources of poor generalization: head classes dominate training exposure, while under-represented classes often converge to sharper regions of the loss landscape. Conventional re-sampling addresses the former without considering geometry, whereas existing long-tailed sharpness-aware minimization (SAM) methods modify losses or perturbations only after biased mini-batches have been drawn. We introduce Sharpness-Guided Equilibrium Sampling (SGS), which treats the sampling distribution as an active control variable for optimization geometry. SGS dynamically adjusts subsequent mini-batches by increasing the sampling probability of less frequently sampled classes while suppressing classes with large SAM-induced loss changes, using only cumulative class counts and EMA sharpness estimates obtained from the standard SAM update, without class-wise perturbations or additional backward passes. We characterize this sampling process through a continuous-time stochastic differential equation and a sampling-dependent PAC-Bayes analysis, explaining how frequency-sharpness feedback can move training toward a more balanced flatness profile. On CIFAR-100 LT with an imbalance ratio of 100, SGS-SAM improves Focal-SAM by 10.85 points in tail accuracy and 3.56 points overall. On ImageNet-LT, it improves ImbSAM by 6.59 points on tail classes and 1.20 points overall. Its training time is only $1.02\times$ that of vanilla SAM. Beyond these gains, SGS establishes a sampling-side route to loss-landscape control, suggesting that future long-tailed methods can jointly regulate data exposure and optimization geometry rather than treating either as fixed.

</details>


### [172] [Energy Manifold Natural Gradient Descent: Riemannian Optimization for Neural PDE Solvers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22004)
*Zhangyong Liang,Huanhuan Gao*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Presents EMNGD, a Riemannian-manifold energy-based natural gradient method for physics-informed neural PDE solvers that respects manifold constraints via retractions; provides theoretical guarantees and scalable solves; demonstrates superior neural PDE performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Extend energy-based natural gradient ideas to constrained parameter spaces (manifolds) used in physics-informed and variational neural PDE solvers, where Euclidean assumptions fail.

Method: Define EMNGD by restricting energy-induced quadratic model to feasible tangent directions on a manifold, using retractions to maintain constraints; derive push-forward optimality under coercivity; prove coordinate invariance and Euclidean reduction; establish global first-order convergence with Armijo backtracking; show robustness to inexact tangent solves; apply Woodbury identities for Gauss-Newton-like pullbacks; use Nyström for scalable sample-space solves; analyze preconditioning and residual-subsampling diagnostics.

Result: Theoretically guarantees and scalable algorithms are developed; EMNGD matches energy metric Newton direction, with exact Euclidean reduction; improved accuracy and faster convergence on neural PDE benchmarks relative to baselines.

Conclusion: EMNGD provides a principled, scalable optimization framework for constrained neural PDE solvers, combining geometry-respecting updates with efficient linear-algebra tricks, and is ready for broader application and extension.

Abstract: Energy natural gradient descent (ENGD) aligns parameter updates with the curvature of an underlying function-space energy, but existing formulations assume an unconstrained Euclidean parameter domain. We introduce \EMNGDfull{}, a manifold optimization framework for physics-informed and variational neural PDE solvers whose parameters lie on a Riemannian manifold. EMNGD restricts the energy-induced quadratic model to feasible tangent directions and uses retractions to preserve parameter constraints throughout optimization. Under coercivity, we prove that the push-forward of the undamped EMNGD direction is the best feasible approximation to the function-space Newton vector in the energy metric. We establish coordinate invariance, exact reduction to ENGD in Euclidean space, global first-order convergence with Armijo backtracking, and robustness to inexact tangent solves. For quadratic residual energies and generalized Gauss--Newton pullbacks, the Woodbury identity transfers the tangent system to sample space without changing the direction. Nyström approximation provides scalable sample-space solves with controlled direction error and recovers the exact direction after iterative convergence. On the evaluated neural PDE benchmarks, EMNGD achieves higher accuracy and faster convergence than the compared state-of-the-art baselines. Woodbury preserves the EMNGD direction, while scalable-solver diagnostics quantify the accuracy--cost trade-off of preconditioning and residual subsampling.

</details>


### [173] [Cross-Domain Off-Policy Evaluation and Learning for Contextual Bandits](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22012)
*Yuta Natsubori,Masataka Ushiku,Yuta Saito*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Proposes Cross-Domain Off-Policy Evaluation and Learning (OPE/L) for contextual bandits, leveraging source-domain logged data alongside target-domain data to overcome few-shot data, deterministic logging, and new actions; introduces a new estimator and policy-gradient method, with empirical gains.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: OPE/L in practice is hampered by limited exploration, deterministic logs, and new actions. Cross-domain data from related domains can mitigate these issues by providing additional coverage and contextual information.

Method: Develops a cross-domain estimator for OPE and a cross-domain policy gradient method that jointly uses target and source logged datasets to estimate values and optimize policies.

Result: Empirical evaluations show substantial enhancements in OPE/L performance in challenging settings, validating the utility of incorporating source-domain data.

Conclusion: Cross-domain OPE/L broadens the applicability of off-policy evaluation and learning by exploiting related domain data, enabling better evaluation and policy optimization when target data are scarce or constrained.

Abstract: Off-Policy Evaluation and Learning (OPE/L) in contextual bandits is rapidly gaining popularity in real systems because new policies can be evaluated and learned securely using only historical logged data. However, existing methods in OPE/L cannot handle many challenging but prevalent scenarios such as few-shot data, deterministic logging policies, and new actions. In many applications, such as personalized medicine, content recommendations, education, and advertising, we need to evaluate and learn new policies in the presence of these challenges. Existing methods cannot evaluate and optimize effectively in these situations due to the notorious variance issue or limited exploration in the logged data. To enable OPE/L even under these unsolved challenges, we propose a new problem setup of Cross-Domain OPE/L, where we have access not only to the logged data from the target domain in which the new policy will be implemented but also to logged datasets collected from other domains. This novel formulation is widely applicable because we can often use historical data not only from the target hospital, country, device, or user segment but also from other hospitals, countries, devices, or segments. We develop a new estimator and policy gradient method to solve OPE/L by leveraging both target and source datasets, resulting in substantially enhanced OPE/L in the previously unsolved situations in our empirical evaluations.

</details>


### [174] [DCS: A Unified Conditional Sensitivity Framework for Cross-Modal Copyright Infringement Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22035)
*Xiafeng Man*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A principled, post-hoc framework for detecting copyright-infringing memorization in foundation models by viewing evidence as a counterfactual distribution shift, using Dual-Branch Conditional Sensitivity (DCS) and conditional differential privacy, instantiated across several model families.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Output similarity is insufficient to judge infringement due to overlap with public-domain content, stylistic conventions, and generalization; a counterfactual, privacy-aware criterion is needed to attribute memorization to training data.

Method: Propose a unified post-hoc detection framework that creates a learning and unlearning branch around the deployed model, links their displacement to counterfactual retraining via influence functions, and bounds observable sensitivity with a counterfactual privacy-budget surrogate, local curvature, training-set size, and perturbation step size. Introduce a calibrated detection statistic to isolate target-specific memorization from generic instability, and instantiate the framework for ridge regression, diffusion models, LMs, and multimodal models using prediction gaps, embedding divergences, token/entropy shifts, and cross-modal changes.

Result: Concrete instantiations across multiple model types demonstrating how the same principle yields measurable indicators (prediction gaps, embedding divergences, token/entropy shifts, cross-modal changes) to detect copyright-related memorization; supports a unified detection approach rather than ad-hoc checks.

Conclusion: The Dual-Branch Conditional Sensitivity framework offers a principled, model-agnostic, post-hoc method to assess copyright-related memorization by linking observable model behaviour to counterfactual retraining effects under a conditional privacy perspective, with calibrated statistics to separate memorization from instability and broad applicability across model families.

Abstract: Currently, most foundation models can reproduce or strongly depend on copyrighted training content, but output similarity alone is insufficient for infringement detection, because similar outputs may also arise from public-domain concepts, common stylistic conventions, or ordinary statistical generalization.
  In this paper, we develops a unified post-hoc detection framework that treats copyright infringement evidence as a counterfactual conditional distribution shift: a protected target is suspicious when the model's behavior under aligned conditions would change measurably if that target were included in, or removed from, the training process.
  We formalize this view through conditional differential privacy and introduce Dual-Branch Conditional Sensitivity (DCS), an operational statistic that measures the observable gap between two locally perturbed model states. Specifically, the proposed DCS framework creates a learning branch and an unlearning branch around the deployed model, connects their displacement to the unavailable counterfactual retraining effect through influence-function analysis, and bounds the observable sensitivity by the counterfactual privacy-budget surrogate, local curvature, training-set scale, and perturbation step size. To distinguish target-specific memorization from generic fine-tuning instability, we further define a calibrated detection statistic that subtracts the sensitivity measured under orthogonal conditions.
  The DCS framework is instantiated for ridge-regularized linear regression, conditional diffusion models, autoregressive language models, and multimodal models. These instantiations show how the same principle can be evaluated through prediction gaps, image-embedding divergence, token-distribution or entropy shifts, and cross-modal representation changes.

</details>


### [175] [CEL: Comprehensive Counterfactual Explanations Library and Benchmark](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22045)
*Oleksii Furman,Łukasz Lenkiewicz,Marcel Musiałek,Maciej Zięba*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: CEL provides a unified benchmark and library for counterfactual explanations, enabling reproducible, fair comparison across 14 methods on 18 datasets with a standardized evaluation protocol.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Counterfactual explainers are evaluated inconsistently across studies, limiting objective comparison and reproducibility; a standardized benchmark is needed to assess validity, sparsity, proximity, coverage, and plausibility.

Method: CEL offers 18 datasets of varying size/types and implements/reimplements 14 CF methods; a unified evaluation protocol with multiple metrics (validity, coverage, sparsity, proximity, plausibility) including density- and outlier-based realism measures; an experimental workflow.

Result: A comprehensive quantitative comparison across methods and datasets demonstrates differences and underlines the benefit of standardization; establishes a reproducible benchmark for future CF method development.

Conclusion: CEL advances reproducibility and fair comparison in counterfactual explanations and provides a ready-to-use workbench to drive development of future methods; claims to be first comprehensive benchmark in unified framework.
