Our code is publicly available at https://github.com/mangocrazz/hyperball-may-not-be-a-free-lunch.

</details>


### [200] [Phylogenetic signal in marine mammal and bird vocalizations captured by audio foundation models: the limited benefit of domain-specific pretraining](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22458)
*Víctor Rincón Yepes*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Pretrained audio embeddings encode phylogenetic structure in vocalizations across two radiations without domain-specific pretraining; strong Mantel correlations with phylogenies, outperforming MFCC baselines and persisting after dimensionality reduction and controlling for pitch.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Investigate whether representations learned for broad audio tasks capture deeper hierarchical structure such as evolutionary relationships, not explicitly encoded in training labels.

Method: Compute pairwise embedding distances for each model's representations; compare to species distance from phylogenies via Mantel tests; two datasets: marine mammals (32 species, 1,754 recordings; cetacean subset) and birds (20 species). Baselines include MFCCs. Apply PCA to 105 dimensions; run partial Mantel controlling for dominant frequency; include BirdNET as an end-to-end classifier; compare to domain-aligned models.

Result: In cetaceans, CLAP r=0.82, BEATs-bio r=0.82, AST r=0.74; p<0.001. MFCC r=0.040, p=0.338. PCA down to 105 dims preserves signal; partial Mantel r=0.404; 97% variance explained remains. In birds, AST r=0.55, CLAP r=0.52; BirdNET and BEATs-bio ~0.32-0.36. Training-domain alignment does not guarantee improved phylogenetic signal.

Conclusion: Pretrained audio embeddings encode evolutionary information beyond training labels; domain-specific pretraining not required; these representations expose deeper structure across taxa.

Abstract: Do learned audio embeddings encode structure that nobody told them to encode? We probe four large pretrained audio models (AST, CLAP, BEATs-bio and BirdNET) with a downstream task none of them saw during training: recovering phylogenetic distance from species vocalizations. If the geometry of the embedding space tracks the tree of life, the representation is picking up something deeper than the labels the model was optimized for.
  We run Mantel tests across two independent radiations. In 32 marine mammal species (1,754 recordings from the Watkins Marine Mammal Sound Database) the foundation models recover strong phylogenetic signal within the 26 cetaceans (CLAP r=0.82, BEATs-bio r=0.82, AST r=0.74; all p<0.001), among the highest acoustic-phylogenetic correlations reported for any taxon. Hand-crafted MFCC features (105d) find nothing (r=0.040, p=0.338). The gap survives after PCA-projecting every embedding down to 105 dimensions, so it is not an artefact of representation size. It also survives a partial Mantel test controlling for dominant frequency (partial Mantel r=0.404, keeping 97% of the variance explained), so it is not just pitch in disguise.
  We repeat the analysis on 20 bird species using the Jetz et al. (2012) phylogeny, and this time add BirdNET, a classifier trained end-to-end on around 6,000 bird species. The general-purpose foundation models recover the signal again (AST r=0.55, CLAP r=0.52). The unexpected result is that neither BirdNET nor the bioacoustic BEATs-bio beat them (r around 0.32 to 0.36). Matching the training domain to the target taxon does not, by itself, help. Pretrained audio embeddings carry evolutionary information across two independent radiations, and domain-specific pretraining is not required for it to emerge.

</details>


### [201] [Complexity Bounds and Approaches to Learning Projected Gradient Descent Solver Iterates](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22467)
*Anjian Li,Ryne Beeson*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Proposes a k-neighborhood data collection strategy that augments training data for generative models used in parametric optimization by including intermediate solver iterates alongside converged solutions. Derives a generalization bound via Rademacher complexity highlighting the impact of k-neighborhoods. Focuses on one-sided box-constrained QPs solved with projected gradient descent, provides two illustrative examples, and links to GLENS to show data-efficient global search within a DDDAS framework.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Data scarcity limits the training of data-driven solvers in expensive parametric optimization problems. By exploiting solver trajectories (intermediate iterates) around converged solutions, the method aims to increase training data without extra solver runs, thereby improving data efficiency in a DDDAS setting.

Method: Introduce a k-neighborhood data collection scheme that augments datasets with solver iterates within k steps of converged solutions. Derive a generalization bound based on Rademacher complexity to quantify how k and related parameters affect learning. Analyze one-sided box-constrained quadratic programs solved via projected gradient descent, and illustrate behavior with two examples. Discuss connections to GLENS and implications for data-efficient global search.

Result: Theoretical analysis reveals how k-neighborhoods influence generalization bounds in this setting, demonstrating improved data efficiency for the data-model-optimization loop. Empirical illustrations on two example problems clarify solver behavior and support GLENS-inspired data-efficient search.

Conclusion: The k-neighborhood augmentation improves data efficiency for learning solvers in DDDAS contexts and provides a theoretical framework (Rademacher-based bound) to understand the impact of neighborhood size and related parameters. The work links to GLENS and suggests broader applicability to data-efficient global optimization.

Abstract: Data scarcity poses a fundamental challenge in training generative models to produce initial guesses for parametric optimization problems that are otherwise numerically expensive to solve. We therefore study a $k$-neighborhood data collection strategy that augments datasets of converged solutions with intermediate solver iterates, increasing the amount of training data without additional solver runs. To understand the benefits of this approach, we derive a generalization bound based on Rademacher complexity that reveals the role of the $k$-neighborhoods and related parameters. To achieve this result, we focus on one-sided box-constrained quadratic programs solved by projected gradient descent. We illustrate the behavior of this solver on two examples. The approach proposed in this paper enables a more capable DDDAS paradigm by improving the efficiency of the data-model-optimization loop. We finish by discussing two views of learning solver-iterate data and connect our analysis with GLENS, a new data-efficient global search method.

</details>


### [202] [Beyond Negative-Ridge Endpoints: Mixed-Sign Spectral Regularization via Negative-Shifted Gradient Descent](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22474)
*Peng Zhao*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Negative ridge and early-stopped negative-shifted gradient descent yield improved generalization in overparameterized linear regression; a Marchenko–Pastur barrier governs the implicit penalty; a general high-rank-tail theorem controls spectrum via trace and squared spectrum, with noncontractive dynamics handled by localized Duhamel integrals and a finite-grid hold-out inequality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Understand implicit spectral penalties and algorithmic biases in overparameterized regression; characterize how negative ridge and early stopping interact with the spectrum, and establish robust risk guarantees under high effective rank.

Method: Spectral decomposition analysis of ridge-like penalties, construction of negative ridge endpoints, analysis of gradient flow with early stopping on a noncontractive shifted operator, Gaussian spike-plus-flat model, random matrix theory (Marchenko–Pastur), development of a general tail theorem and hold-out inequality.

Result: Early-stopped negative-shifted gradient descent achieves lower risk than any admissible endpoint; identification of a MP barrier where the implicit penalty offset lies; a general theorem for high-effective-rank tails with quantifiable control via trace and spectrum; finite-grid hold-out transfers separations to validation-selected algorithms.

Conclusion: Stopped dynamics recover head scales and unify ridgeless rescalings beyond positive shrinkage; the framework handles noncontractive shifts and provides algorithmic guidance for exploiting implicit penalties in high-dimensional regression.

Abstract: In overparameterized linear regression, many weak spectral directions act like a ridge penalty on the signal-bearing spectrum; negative ridge is the natural correction, pushing filters above one. The stable negative-ridge endpoint, however, is structurally limited: its pole must stay below the smallest nonzero empirical eigenvalue, and it anti-shrinks smaller eigenvalues more than larger ones. Early-stopped negative-shifted gradient descent escapes this constraint. Its filter is smooth at the would-be pole and mixed-sign-capable: above-ridgeless directions form a leading prefix, with lower directions shrunk or exposure-controlled while stopping sets the crossover. In a Gaussian spike-plus-flat model we discover a Marchenko-Pastur barrier: the shift that cancels the implicit penalty lies a bulk width above the smallest empirical eigenvalue, and the stopped path improves on every admissible endpoint by a polynomial factor in risk under explicit conditions. Our main theorem permits a general high-effective-rank tail: its trace sets the implicit floor, its squared spectrum controls exposure, and the floor-critical path recovers all head scales at once, beyond positive shrinkage and, once scales separate, every uniform rescaling of ridgeless. Handling the noncontractive shifted dynamics is the central technical challenge; localized Duhamel integrals control them. A finite-grid hold-out inequality transfers the separations to the validation-selected algorithm.

</details>


### [203] [\k{appa}-LoRA: Condition Numbers Reveal Which LoRA Matrices Worth Updating](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22489)
*Jianghui Wang,Silong Yong,Francesco Orabona,Marco Canini,Katia P. Sycara,Yaqi Xie*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: kappa-LoRA identifies that only a subset of LoRA matrices, those with large condition numbers, meaningfully contribute to adaptation. By updating only the top 50% by condition number, it halves trainable parameters while maintaining accuracy, reducing compute/memory costs. Training shows spectral rebalancing as a mechanism for gains.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LoRA is an efficient fine-tuning method but still incurs substantial cost because it updates all LoRA matrices uniformly. Not all matrices contribute equally to adaptation, especially in large-scale models and edge settings.

Method: Compute the condition numbers of LoRA weight matrices; rank matrices by condition number; update only the top 50% of matrices with the largest condition numbers during training. Evaluate on multiple benchmarks against standard LoRA, measuring fine-tuning time, memory, and accuracy; analyze how condition numbers evolve during training.

Result: Top-50%-by-condition-number update reduces trainable parameters by ~50%, cuts fine-tuning time by 16.2% on average, and lowers memory cost by ~4.5% while matching standard LoRA accuracy. Selected matrices’ condition numbers consistently decrease during training, indicating spectral rebalancing rather than simple parameter pruning as the performance gain mechanism.

Conclusion: Selective spectral rebalancing via condition-number ranking can substantially improve fine-tuning efficiency without accuracy loss. The approach highlights the importance of targeting informative directions in parameter space and suggests further exploration of dynamic or adaptive selection criteria during training.

Abstract: Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA) has become a widely adopted technique for efficient neural network fine-tuning, decomposing model updates into low-rank matrices. However, LoRA remains computationally costly because it updates all matrices uniformly, regardless of their actual contribution to adaptation. This cost is especially prohibitive for large-scale models with billions of parameters and for resource-constrained settings such as edge deployment and on-device fine-tuning. We show for the first time that not all LoRA matrices are equally worth tuning: matrices with smaller condition numbers (the ratio of largest to smallest singular value) are already well-balanced across directions and contribute only marginally to adaptation, whereas matrices with larger condition numbers contain underdeveloped directions that span richer subspaces and drive most of the performance gains. This observation itself is a key contribution of our work, and it motivates a more selective approach to fine-tuning. Building on this insight, we propose \k{appa}-LoRA, a method that optimizes LoRA by focusing updates on the matrices with the largest condition numbers, which capture the most informative directions of change. By restricting LoRA updates to the top 50% of weight matrices ranked by condition number, \k{appa}-LoRA halves the trainable parameter count and correspondingly reduces compute and memory cost. Extensive experiments across multiple benchmarks show that this design cuts fine-tuning time by 16.2% on average while matching the accuracy of standard LoRA and reducing memory cost by 4.5%. Further analysis reveals that the condition numbers of the selected matrices consistently decrease over training, suggesting that \k{appa}-LoRA's effectiveness stems from targeted spectral rebalancing rather than parameter selection alone.

</details>


### [204] [Susceptible Reservoir Architectures for Regime-Conditional Volatility Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22491)
*Aliaksei Kaliutau*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SUSA: a reservoir-design framework for volatility forecasting using complex-valued/open-chain and periodic reservoirs, plus quantum-inspired (Qiskit) implementations; achieves competitive performance versus GARCH and improves QLIKE; ensemble stacking yields additional gains.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Volatility forecasting is dominated by persistence and measurement noise, leaving limited residual structure for nonlinear models. The authors propose SUSA to exploit residual dynamics via reservoir computing, including complex-valued and quantum-inspired architectures, plus regime-aware interpretation and ensemble enhancement.

Method: Introduce Susceptible Architectures (SUSA) with two reservoir variants (complex-valued open-chain and periodic reservoirs) and regime-conditioned experts to interpret features across calm/onset/recovery/persistent-stress states. Implement open-system q-qubit counterparts in Qiskit with a common AR-Ridge anchor and a bounded residual correction trained under QLIKE. Evaluate on 16 US equity/ETF series using three disjoint chronological folds, a 12-observation input window, and a 5-observation forecast horizon. Use QLIKE as the loss and compare to GARCH; additionally form a stacked ensemble (HARQ-style) to mix predictions.

Result: The models are competitive with GARCH, achieving statistically significant QLIKE improvements for specific assets (IWM, XLP). The stacked ensemble improves mean QLIKE by 0.0116 over its strongest constituent and wins in 75% of test scenarios.

Conclusion: SUSA introduces a versatile reservoir-based toolkit for volatility forecasting, including complex-valued and quantum-inspired variants with regime-aware interpretation and ensemble enhancement. The approach yields competitive performance and asset-specific gains against GARCH, with ensemble methods delivering additional improvements; future work could extend horizons, assets, and comparisons to broader baselines.

Abstract: Volatility forecasting is dominated by persistence and measurement noise, leaving limited residual structure for nonlinear models to exploit. We introduce Susceptible Architectures (SUSA), a reservoir-design principle for volatility forecasting, and its two concrete implementations, based on complex-valued open-chain and periodic reservoirs and regime-conditioned experts to interpret reservoir features across calm, onset, recovery, and persistent-stress states. We also implement open-system $q$-qubit counterparts in Qiskit while retaining a common AR-Ridge anchor and a bounded residual correction trained under QLIKE. We evaluate models on 16 U.S. equity and exchange-traded-fund series using three disjoint chronological training, validation, and test folds, a 12-observation input window, and a five-observation forecast horizon. The proposed models perform competitively with GARCH, achieving statistically significant QLIKE improvements for specific assets (IWM, XLP). Also models' forecasts complement HARQ-style predictions: a stacked ensemble improves mean QLIKE by 0.0116 over its strongest constituent and wins in 75% of test scenarios.

</details>


### [205] [Interpretable EEG biomarkers with bag-of-waves: Spatial and temporal waveform dictionaries for low-data regimes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22508)
*Athanasios Papastathopoulos-Katsaros,Steven T. Lee,Lin Yao,Ajay Thomas,Junseok Park,Matthew J. McGinley,Zhandong Liu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A novel interpretable, low-data EEG representation (bag-of-waves) learned via shift-invariant k-means to form waveform atoms; extends with atom-level n-grams and multichannel spatial atoms; delivers competitive performance with far fewer parameters and with direct interpretability compared to deep/foundation models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address the bias of predefined spectral features and the opacity/data-hungry nature of deep models in EEG analysis; seek a data-efficient, interpretable alternative that clinicians can validate.

Method: Unsupervised learning of a small dictionary of recurring EEG waveform templates (atoms) using shift-invariant k-means on continuous EEG without labels. Transform EEG into a sequence of atom tokens; downstream classifier or clustering uses token counts. Extend with atom-to-atom transitions (n-grams) to capture temporal structure and with regional/cross-channel spatial atoms for multichannel data.

Result: Across three datasets (single-channel mouse genotype clustering with 16 animals; resting-state dementia classification; TUEV EEG event classification), bag-of-waves achieves competitive performance relative to state-of-the-art deep/foundation models, while using far fewer parameters and offering full interpretability by recovering recognizable clinical morphologies.

Conclusion: Bag-of-waves provides a compact, interpretable, data-efficient EEG representation that performs competitively with heavy models, particularly in low-data regimes, and yields directly inspectable waveform atoms that clinicians can validate.

Abstract: Electroencephalography (EEG) is widely used to diagnose neurological conditions, but its analysis usually relies on either predefined spectral features or deep neural networks. Predefined features carry a strong bias, since they fix in advance what counts as informative, while deep neural networks and foundation models are hard to interpret and need large amounts of data and compute. We present bag-of-waves, an interpretable framework that learns a small dictionary of recurring EEG waveform templates, called atoms, using shift-invariant k-means without labels. The continuous EEG is then turned into a sequence of atom tokens, whose counts feed a simple downstream classifier or clustering step. We extend this representation in two ways: we add atom-to-atom transitions, which we call n- grams, to capture temporal structure, and we move from single-channel atoms to regional and cross-channel spatial atoms for the multichannel case. We test the method on three complementary datasets, each probing a different aspect: single-channel mouse genotype clustering with only sixteen animals (the low-data and temporal case), resting-state dementia classification (the spatial case), and the TUEV benchmark, a six-way classification of clinical EEG events (a high-data comparison against strong deep and foundation baselines). Across all three datasets, bag-of-waves achieves performance competitive with state-of-the-art deep and foundation models. Yet, it operates with a fraction of the parameter count and provides full interpretability: because every atom corresponds to an inspectable waveform, the method explicitly recovers known clinical morphologies that a neurophysiologist can directly validate. Its main advantage is that it works in the low-data regime where heavier models are a poor fit.

</details>


### [206] [Dysphagia Risk Stratification in Head and Neck Cancer via Two-Stage PRO-Clinical Stacking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22514)
*Siyuan Zhao,Eric Ababio Anyimadu,Zachary G. Brumm,Yue Ma,Clifton David Fuller,Xinhua Zhang,G. Elisabeta Marai,Guadalupe Canahuate*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: An imaging-free, interpretable two-stage PRO-clinical stacking model uses MDADI and clinical data to predict swallowing impairment in head and neck cancer survivors, with individual MDADI items adding predictive value beyond global scores for risk stratification.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Dysphagia is a disabling late effect of head and neck cancer treatment. The standard imaging-based DIGEST assessment is accurate but resource-intensive and burdensome, limiting routine surveillance. Patient-reported outcomes (PROs) are scalable and low-cost but lack a clear framework for triggering clinical escalation; there is a need for a clinically actionable, imaging-free prognostic framework.

Method: Develop a single-visit PRO-clinical prediction framework employing a two-stage stacking model that integrates PRO responses (MDADI) with structured clinical variables. The approach avoids videofluoroscopy, quantifies independent contributions of patient-reported symptoms and clinical factors, and includes interpretability analyses to reveal patterns associated with swallowing impairment.

Result: The model demonstrates that individual MDADI responses contain predictive information beyond composite/global MDADI scores. Interpretability analyses identify symptom patterns and clinical risk factors linked to swallowing impairment, supporting the viability of structured PRO-clinical integration as an imaging-free risk stratification approach in HNC survivorship.

Conclusion: A practical, imaging-free framework for dysphagia risk stratification in HNC survivorship is feasible through PRO-clinical integration, with MDADI item-level data adding value and providing clinically interpretable guidance for escalation decisions.

Abstract: Dysphagia is a debilitating late effect of head and neck cancer (HNC) treatment, yet timely identification of at-risk patients remains challenging in survivorship care. Definitive assessment relies on videofluoroscopic imaging, as captured by the Dynamic Imaging Grade of Swallowing Toxicity (CTCAE-DIGEST), which, while validated, requires specialized equipment, trained personnel, and significant patient burden, limiting its routine use in surveillance. Patient-reported outcomes (PROs), by contrast, are low-cost, scalable, and easily collected at any clinical encounter, making them an attractive alternative signal for identifying patients who may warrant further evaluation. However, a clear clinical framework for translating PRO responses into actionable interventions is still evolving. In particular, uncertainty remains regarding when a patient's self-reported symptom burden should prompt escalation of care.
  This study addresses this gap by formulating a single-visit PRO-clinical prediction framework and introducing a clinically interpretable two-stage stacking model to predict swallowing impairment risk using PRO responses and structured clinical variables, without requiring videofluoroscopic imaging. The proposed framework quantifies the independent contributions of patient-reported symptoms and clinical factors within a unified and interpretable risk assessment model. Our findings demonstrate that individual MDADI responses contain predictive information beyond that captured by composite or global summary scores, while interpretability analyses reveal symptom patterns and clinical risk factors associated with swallowing impairment. Together, these results support the use of structured PRO-clinical integration as a practical, imaging-free approach for dysphagia risk stratification in HNC survivorship.

</details>
